**Summary.** This paper introduces FlowMeas, a generative learning approach that treats resource-constrained quantum measurement design as an optimization problem. By using a generative flow network, it learns to sample optimal ensembles of shallow Clifford circuits tailored for specific hardware constraints. This allows for highly efficient energy estimation in complex molecular and lattice systems while respecting practical limitations on circuit depth.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, data-driven framework for optimizing quantum measurements, which is crucial for making near-term quantum algorithms practical by explicitly incorporating hardware limitations into the design process.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is designing quantum measurement protocols that efficiently extract quantum information while balancing statistical accuracy against practical hardware resource constraints like circuit depth and gate count.

**Main result.** The proposed FlowMeas framework achieves state-of-the-art performance by learning optimal, shallow Clifford circuits, reducing energy estimation error by up to 27% compared to strong product-measurement baselines.

**Method.** It recasts resource-constrained measurement design as a generative learning problem, utilizing a Generative Flow Network (GFlowNet) to sample ensembles of hardware-compatible measurement circuits.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to estimating energy for molecular Hamiltonians and large interacting fermionic models (Hubbard model), testing its utility across varying qubit counts up to 54 qubits.

**Key observables.** Energy estimation error (RMSE), Pauli coverage statistics, circuit depth, and entangling gate count.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The trade-off between product measurements (low depth) and fully commuting measurements (high depth); the allowed number of entangling layers ($d_{\max}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The problem is framed as optimizing over a distribution sampled by the generative model, effectively searching the intermediate regime between simple product bases and complex fully commuting sets.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate comparisons between different measurement strategies (FC vs. QWC vs. FlowMeas) and detail the GFlowNet process for constructing measurement ensembles based on Pauli coverage statistics.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the generative learning approach (GFlowNet), validates its performance against existing benchmarks across molecular systems, and extends testing to large fermionic lattice models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extracting quantum information from a quantum state is a fundamental task of quantum computation, often requiring the estimation of many non-commuting observables under a finite measurement budget. For both near-term and early fault-tolerant settings, the measurement protocol must balance statistical efficiency against implementation resources such as circuit depth, connectivity, and entangling-gate count. Many existing strategies focus on two extremes: hardware-friendly product measurements with high sampling cost, and fully commuting measurements with deep circuits. Here we recast resource-constrained measurement design as a generative learning problem. We introduce FlowMeas, which uses a generative flow network to directly sample finite ensembles of shallow Clifford measurement circuits subject to a prescribed shot budget and hardware constraints. At zero entangling depth, FlowMeas learns qubit-wise commuting measurement schedules and already matches or improves leading product-measurement methods on nearly all molecular benchmarks. Allowing one or two entangling gate layers yields further reductions in energy estimation error of up to $27\%$ relative to the strongest state-independent product-measurement baseline. The learned policy can also be reused across related Hamiltonians, substantially accelerating retraining along a molecular potential-energy surface. We further obtain results for molecular Hamiltonians with up to 20 qubits and apply the framework to a compactly encoded 54-qubit interacting fermionic model, extending the demonstrated scale beyond prior molecular benchmarks. These results establish generative learning as a flexible and unified framework for quantum measurement design under practical resource constraints.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper tackles the inefficiency of current quantum sampling techniques used for molecular electronic structure calculations. It proposes a novel measurement-basis engineering approach called NOCI, which samples measurements across multiple optimized orbital bases rather than just the standard Hartree-Fock basis. This significantly improves the discovery of important electronic configurations while maintaining performance even when classical computational resources are limited.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource constraints in simulating complex molecular Hamiltonians using near-term quantum hardware concepts (quantum sampling), which is highly relevant to AMO physics applications like spectroscopy or reaction dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is improving quantum sampling methods for molecular simulations by efficiently identifying physically relevant, energy-lowering electronic configurations.

**Main result.** Measurement-basis engineering using Non-orthogonal Configuration Interaction (NOCI) shows promise by increasing configurational diversity and alleviating sampling concentration issues, especially when the Hartree-Fock basis is insufficient.

**Method.** The work develops an NOCI measurement protocol that distributes quantum measurements across multiple optimized, non-orthogonal orbital bases to improve sample efficiency over standard methods.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on molecular electronic structure simulation, using systems like $	ext{N}_2$ and $	ext{H}_{12}$. The core physics involves diagonalizing the Hamiltonian within a reduced subspace spanned by quantum-selected configurations derived from measurements in various orbital bases.

**Key observables.** Energy error convergence, configurational diversity (number of unique samples), average orbital occupancies ($o_{\sigma}^{m,\mu}$), and diagonalization subspace size ($d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fixed classical resource budgets, noise levels ($\lambda$), and the choice between orthogonal (HF) vs. non-orthogonal measurement bases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methods do not solve the fundamental challenge of preparing quantum states with high overlap to the true ground state; furthermore, NOCI introduces increased classical cost due to constructing dense Hamiltonian matrices.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare energy error convergence under varying noise levels and fixed subspace sizes for different sampling strategies (LUCJ vs. Uniform Random Sampling). They also illustrate the workflow comparing standard computational basis measurements versus multi-basis NOCI measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of current quantum sampling methods like SQD, showing that unconstrained classical resources can mask true performance issues. It then introduces and validates the NOCI measurement protocol by optimizing orbital bases to improve sample efficiency for electronic structure calculations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum-selected configuration interaction (QSCI) methods use a quantum computer to identify dominant electronic configurations in the molecular ground state, while a classical computer diagonalizes the Hamiltonian within the reduced subspace spanned by those configurations. Sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD), a leading QSCI approach, uses iterative classical post-processing to correct noisy quantum measurement to ensure that the corresponding configurations remain physically sensible. In this work, we show that SQD performance can be strongly influenced by uncontrolled growth of the classical diagonalization subspace. When classical resources are not explicitly constrained, classical uniform random sampling can reproduce SQD benchmarks as noise increases the diversity of sampled configurations. We show any fair benchmarking protocol of SQD must explicitly control diagonalization size over unique samples. We then address the problem of efficiently discovering physically relevant, energy-lowering configurations by introducing a measurement protocol based on non-orthogonal configuration interaction (NOCI). By distributing measurements across orbital bases optimized with respect to the molecular Hamiltonian, we obtain improved sample efficiency relative to measurements performed solely in the Hartree--Fock basis. Importantly, these improvements persist even under fixed classical resource budgets, demonstrating that the resulting configurations are of higher quality rather than being more numerous. Under our proposed benchmarking procedure, we establish measurement-basis engineering as a promising route to improving quantum sampling methods for electronic structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper rigorously analyzes the spectral stability of Weyl--Heisenberg measurements relative to the ideal SIC benchmark. By developing explicit constructions in various dimensions (cyclic, finite-field), the authors derive quantitative lower bounds on the minimum eigenvalue ($\lambda$) and show that these measurements approach optimal performance asymptotically without requiring the existence of a true SIC state.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, constructive methods to quantify measurement performance metrics like local Fisher efficiency and canonical error bounds, which are crucial for designing optimal quantum state tomography protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to quantify the spectral stability of minimal Weyl--Heisenberg (WH) measurements, determining how closely they can approach the ideal Symmetric Informationally Complete (SIC) benchmark without assuming SIC existence.

**Main result.** The authors construct a hierarchy of minimal measurements showing that for prime-power dimensions $p \ge 5$, perturbations of cubic Alltop states yield spectra confined to $[L_q, U_q]$ with the ratio $U_q/L_q$ approaching 1, and $\lambda(\phi_q)/\Lambda^\star_q 	o 1$.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on spectral theory applied to the projector-Gram matrix derived from WH measurements, utilizing Fourier transforms, max-min optimization, and specialized algebraic techniques for cyclic and finite-field correlations.

**Model / system.** The system under study is quantum measurement theory using minimal rank-one Weyl--Heisenberg (WH) measurements in a $d$-dimensional Hilbert space. The quality of the measurement is diagnosed via the spectrum of the Projector Gram matrix, $	ext{Spec}(G_\Pi^\phi)$.

**Key observables.** Smallest nonidentity eigenvalue ($\lambda(\phi)$), spectral stability measure ($\eta(\phi)$), and the canonical Mean-Squared Error ratio ($R(\phi)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimension $d$, prime power dimension $q=p^k$ with characteristic $p \ge 5$, and the SIC benchmark value.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often proceeds by assuming specific algebraic structures (e.g., cyclic or finite-field settings) to derive bounds, though it aims to provide results without assuming exact SIC existence.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare spectral stability ($\eta$) across different dimensions and plot the exact MSE ratio ($R$) for canonical linear inversion against the WH-SIC benchmark ($R=1$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the relationship between IC, uniform stability, and the SIC endpoint using the projector-Gram matrix spectrum. It then develops specific constructions (cyclic, finite-field) to derive quantitative bounds on $\lambda(\phi)$ and $\eta(\phi)$, culminating in results for prime-power dimensions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Informational completeness (IC) guarantees that an inverse exists, not that it is statistically well conditioned. For minimal rank-one Weyl--Heisenberg (WH) measurements, covariance makes the nonidentity projector-Gram spectrum proportional to the fiducial's ambiguity intensities, with eigenvalues \(d|χ_φ(u)|^2\), turning stability into an explicit worst-direction design problem; write \(λ\) for its smallest nonidentity eigenvalue. Haar fiducials are IC almost surely while \(\mathbb E[λ^{-1}]=\infty\), and an explicit geometric family used to establish balanced informationally complete measurements in every dimension has a normalized spectral floor bounded above by an exponentially decaying envelope. We then construct a hierarchy of minimal measurements. A cyclic family with exactly \(d^2\) outcomes in every integer dimension has floors \(Θ(d^{-3})\) for odd \(d\) and \(Θ(d^{-5})\) for even \(d\); a finite-field family for \(q=2^m\) obeys the uniform bound \(λ\ge4/9\). Our main result treats every prime-power dimension of characteristic \(p\ge5\). A balanced one-coordinate perturbation repairs the zero ambiguity axis of a cubic Alltop state, gives an attained floor uniformly bounded below by a positive constant, and confines the entire nonidentity spectrum to \([L_q,U_q]\) with \(U_q/L_q\to1\). Its SIC-normalized minimum tends to one, and \(λ(φ_q)/Λ_q^\star\to1\) for the global finite-field WH max--min optimum \(Λ_q^\star\), without assuming SIC existence. The complete spectrum determines the exact finite-sample Hilbert--Schmidt error of canonical linear inversion at \(I/d\), while its lower edge controls local Fisher efficiency and canonical-shadow bounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the use of parity mapping to enhance quantum optimization on frustrated Ising rings. By applying this technique, the authors demonstrate significant theoretical improvements for both Quantum Annealing and QAOA protocols compared to standard methods. This suggests that constraint encoding via parity qubits can lead to more robust and scalable quantum computation algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete algorithmic improvement for quantum computation by showing how mapping constraints can stabilize spectral gaps and reduce scaling in variational quantum algorithms applied to physically relevant models like frustrated magnets.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how applying a 'parity mapping' technique affects the performance of quantum optimization algorithms (QA and QAOA) when solving problems on frustrated Ising rings.

**Main result.** Parity mapping improves both continuous-time Quantum Annealing by increasing spectral gaps and discrete QAOA by achieving constant layer scaling, outperforming conventional methods in complexity metrics.

**Method.** The authors analyze the performance of two quantum optimization protocols—Quantum Annealing (QA) and QAOA—on a frustrated Ising ring using theoretical analysis and state-vector simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is the frustrated Ising ring, modeled by a target Hamiltonian $\hat{H}_{\text{target}}$. The core technique involves mapping local interactions to parity qubits via Parity Mapping, which introduces constraint Hamiltonians ($\tilde{H}_c$) into the overall problem formulation.

**Key observables.** Minimum spectral gap (for QA), number of required QAOA layers, and circuit complexity metrics like CNOT depth.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strengths ($J_j$), constraint strength ($C$), and the annealing schedule parameter ($s(t)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares ideal Parity-QA vs. hardware-motivated decompositions, and assumes that single-qubit rotations are sufficient for ground state preparation in certain regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Ising ring structure in both logical and parity qubit representations, detailing the required multi-qubit gate transformations.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the frustrated Ising ring model, details the Parity Mapping technique to transform the Hamiltonian, and then analyzes its impact separately on continuous-time Quantum Annealing (QA) and the discrete QAOA protocol, concluding with complexity comparisons.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The frustrated Ising ring is one of the simplest models exhibiting exponential closing spectral gaps, making it a paradigmatic and challenging benchmark for quantum annealing (QA). Ground-state preparation for this model has therefore been studied extensively in both continuous-time QA and digitized protocols such as the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA). Here, we use the frustrated Ising ring to investigate how the parity mapping affects the performance of both QA and QAOA. For QA, finite-size calculations show that the parity mapping increases the minimum spectral gap under the energy normalization used in this work, thereby enabling faster continuous-time ground state preparation protocols. An ideal implementation of Parity-QA, with a single global constraint, shows no evidence of exponential gap closing over the accessible system sizes, whereas a hardware-motivated decomposition into local constraints restores the exponential decrease, albeit with a smaller fitted exponent than conventional QA. For the digitized protocol, we find that the number of Parity-QAOA layers required to prepare the exact ground state remains constant over the simulated sizes, improving upon the quadratic scaling required by conventional QAOA. To investigate the role of constraints in Parity-QAOA, we further consider a modified Ising ring instance in which the constraint term is essential for preparing the target ground state. We then compare the corresponding resource requirements with those of conventional QAOA.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes quetzalcoatlite, an ideal kagome magnet, to understand its magnetic ground state. By mapping the electronic structure onto an effective Heisenberg model, the authors show that competing intra- and interlayer exchange interactions stabilize a unique, intrinsic three-dimensional chiral spin order. This work highlights how structural geometry dictates complex quantum magnetic behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit derivation of chirality from competing geometric interactions in a lattice model is highly relevant for understanding frustrated magnetism and emergent order parameters in quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how geometric frustration, chirality, and interlayer coupling dictate the magnetic ground state of the mineral quetzalcoatlite.

**Main result.** The competition between nearest-neighbor intralayer and next-nearest-neighbor interlayer couplings stabilizes an intrinsic three-dimensional chiral magnetic order characterized by a $60^\circ$ spin rotation between adjacent layers.

**Method.** The authors employed ab initio calculations (DFT+U) to derive effective Heisenberg exchange interactions, which were then used to minimize the classical magnetic Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as an ideal kagome magnet structure within quetzalcoatlite. The low-energy physics is mapped onto a generalized Heisenberg model incorporating multiple competing exchange couplings ($J_\alpha$) up to the 5th nearest neighbor.

**Key observables.** Chiral magnetic order, propagation vector Q, static and dynamic spin structure factors, and electronic band gap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative strengths of the intralayer vs. interlayer exchange couplings ($J_2$ vs $J_3$), which determine the transition between different ordered states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes an idealized stoichiometry for simplification and uses DFT+U to treat strong electronic correlations, which opens a gap in the insulating state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure showing the kagome lattice; one figure shows the opening of the electronic band gap upon including correlation effects (GGA vs GGA+U); others map out the resulting $\sqrt{3} 	imes \sqrt{3}$ coplanar magnetic order with layer-to-layer $60^\circ$ rotation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the material as a suitable platform, then uses DFT+U to calculate exchange couplings ($J_\alpha$). Finally, it analyzes the resulting effective Heisenberg model by minimizing the energy and analyzing the magnetic structure factors to determine the chiral ground state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The natural mineral quetzalcoatlite Zn$_6$Cu$_3$(TeO$_6$)$_2$(OH)$_6$ $\cdot$ (Ag$_x$Pb$_y$Cl$_{x+2y}$) is a structurally ideal kagome magnet, providing a platform for exploring the interplay of geometric frustration, chirality, and tunability in a disorder-free framework. Here, we present the (first) comprehensive ab initio study of its electronic and magnetic properties. The electronic structure is dominated by localized half-filled Cu $d_{x^2-y^2}$ orbitals that become insulating through electronic correlations. Mapping the low-energy physics onto a Heisenberg model reveals that the magnetism is governed primarily by two exchange interactions: a nearest-neighbor intralayer kagome coupling and a next-nearest-neighbor interlayer coupling. Their competition stabilizes an unconventional three-dimensional chiral magnetic state. Each kagome layer hosts a $\sqrt{3}\times\sqrt{3}$ order, while adjacent layers are rotated by $60^\circ$, producing a right-handed spiral along the crystallographic $c$-axis. This intrinsic chiral order emerges naturally from the crystal structure and magnetic interactions, establishing quetzalcoatlite as a distinctive realization of chiral magnetism on a perfect kagome lattice. At the same time, the small energy scale of the exchange interactions places the material close to competing magnetic regimes, suggesting that moderate pressure, chemical substitution, or structural modifications may strongly enhance frustration, suppress long-range order, and potentially drive the system toward a quantum spin-liquid state.

</details>

Black cells denote analytically exact reconstruction.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper establishes that reconstructing an operator of rank $S$ in a spin system requires the measurement sampling points to form a spherical $(2J+S)$-design. It provides explicit constructions and protocols, offering a powerful mathematical tool to guide the design of optimal, minimal-sample measurements for quantum systems like magnetometers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly impacts quantum measurement theory by providing rigorous, mathematically necessary conditions (spherical designs) for achieving high-fidelity state or observable readouts using minimal measurements, which is crucial for experimental quantum sensing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the minimal discrete set of measurement points (coherent states) required to exactly reconstruct an arbitrary quantum operator within a specific rank sector ($S$) for spin systems.

**Main result.** Exact reconstruction is mathematically equivalent to the sampling points forming a spherical $(2J+S)$-design, providing a unified framework for reading out physical observables from limited measurements.

**Method.** The authors use techniques involving multipole expansions and comparing discrete sums over design points against continuous integral representations of quantum operators.

**Model / system.** The system is based on spin-$J$ angular momentum systems ($	ext{SU}(2)$), whose phase space is modeled by the sphere $S^2$. Operators are analyzed via their decomposition into irreducible tensor sectors ($V_S$).

**Key observables.** Spin moments ($\langle J_q angle$), second moment contravariant symbol ($I^{ab}$), and the expectation values of operators in specific rank sectors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spin quantum number $J$, the target operator rank $S$, and the required design strength $t = 2J+S$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is specialized for $	ext{SU}(2)$ systems, focusing on reconstructing specific tensor sectors rather than the entire Hilbert space.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the scaling of known spherical designs ($M$ vs $t$) and confirm that exact reconstruction (black cells) occurs when the design strength $t$ meets or exceeds $2J+S$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the mathematical equivalence between exact operator reconstruction and the existence of a specific type of spherical design, provides explicit constructions (Gauss-Legendre), and concludes with practical protocols for moment estimation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coherent states bridge the gap between quantum and classical physics, but their overcomplete and nonorthogonal nature makes it difficult to identify the minimal discrete set needed to reconstruct quantum information. Finite spin-coherent tomography and discrete coherent-state operator bases are known, but here we address the more specific rank-resolved problem of preserving the canonical contravariant-symbol representation. We show that the canonical finite coherent-state formula reconstructs every operator in the rank-$S$ sector exactly if and only if the sampling points form a spherical $(2J+S)$-design. We call the associated configurations spin-coherent quantum designs. We further give a fully explicit positive-weight Gauss-Legendre construction that avoids the need for an equal-weight spherical design. Together, these results establish a unified framework for reading out physical observables from a handful of measurement samples, playing for spin systems the role that the so-called von Neumann lattice plays for canonical coherent states. Finally, we derive practical protocols for estimating moments of spin operators from these constructions, with direct applications to polarimetry, magnetometry, and quantum state tomography.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper tackles the challenge of improving single-photon purity from organic molecules by designing monolithic microcavities. Using advanced theoretical modeling and optimization, they propose specific Fabry–Perot and CBG designs that maximize Purcell enhancement into the desired zero-phonon line. This provides a crucial pathway for realizing practical, high-quality quantum light sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum light sources, a core topic in AMO physics and quantum optics. The detailed coupling of molecular emission properties with engineered cavity QED effects is highly relevant for building practical quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Single organic molecules are promising single-photon sources but suffer from unwanted phonon sideband emission, reducing the desired zero-phonon line (0-0 ZPL) purity.

**Main result.** The authors propose and analyze three monolithic microcavity designs that can significantly enhance Purcell enhancement into the 0-0 ZPL, offering a clear route to high-quality monochromatic single-photon sources.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical modeling, including finite-element method (FEM) simulations and analytical approximations for various cavity types (Fabry–Perot, CBG), coupled with Bayesian optimization techniques.

**Model / system.** The system involves embedding organic quantum emitters within monolithic photonic nanostructures like Fabry–Perot or Circular Bragg Grating (CBG) microcavities. These structures are designed to engineer the local photonic density of states.

**Key observables.** Purcell Enhancement ($F_P$), Spectral Redistribution Factor ($\alpha'$), Overall Collection Efficiency ($\eta_{	ext{ZPL}}$), and cavity quality factor ($Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cavity dimensions (e.g., spacer thickness $h_{	ext{SiO2}}$, disk radius $R_{	ext{disk}}$), emitter wavelength ($\lambda_0$), and coupling parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that single molecules can be positioned at the electric field maximum of the engineered cavity mode, which is a major technological hurdle.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate theoretical field profiles (Gaussian/Bessel-like), far-field intensity patterns showing Gaussian divergence, and parameter dependency sweeps mapping optimal ranges for various structural dimensions to maximize $F_P$ and $\eta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops an analytical framework by analyzing different cavity geometries (Fabry–Perot vs. CBG) using Gaussian beam optics and Bloch wave vector analysis. It then applies optimization techniques (Bayesian optimization) to determine optimal structural parameters that maximize the desired photon collection efficiency.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Single organic molecules are a well-established platform for high-quality single-photon generation: they can emit lifetime-limited photons with high purity and indistinguishability. However, their emission is accompanied by a pronounced red-shifted phonon sideband and higher-order vibrational peaks, which reduce the fraction of photons emitted into the desired narrowband zero-phonon line. The standard approach to suppressing this unwanted emission is to integrate the emitter into a monolithic photonic nanostructure that provides Purcell enhancement. However, incorporating organic materials using clean-room techniques has proven challenging, and has in fact so far prevented their integration into monolithic microcavities altogether. As a result, efficient, narrowband organic single-photon sources for applications in quantum information processing have remained elusive despite their considerable promise. Here, we propose three monolithic microcavity designs tailored to provide sufficient Purcell enhancement for organic quantum emitters. The Purcell effect induced by these cavities preferentially enhances emission into the 0-0 zero-phonon line, increasing spectral purity, photon extraction, and shortening the excited-state lifetime, which in turn relaxes the requirements for generating indistinguishable photons. The cavities have been optimized using Bayesian optimization and the adaptive Antoulas-Anderson (AAA) algorithm for rational approximation, which offer global optimization and the efficient reconstruction of spectra from scattering simulations, respectively. These structures are compatible with both standard clean-room processing and single-molecule preparation techniques. Therefore, they offer a clear route to realize high-quality, practically monochromatic organic single-photon light sources.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes population competition in microbial systems subjected to complex, multi-state environmental fluctuations that mimic gradual feast-famine cycles. By developing a multi-state stochastic model, the authors show that these intermediate states significantly alter evolutionary outcomes compared to simple two-state models. The work provides quantitative tools for understanding how the complexity of environmental noise dictates population fate.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biology/ecology, the mathematical machinery—analyzing non-equilibrium dynamics in fluctuating environments using stochastic processes (like master equations or birth-death processes)—is highly relevant to open quantum systems and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how population dynamics of competing strains are affected by environmental fluctuations that change gradually through multiple intermediate states, moving beyond simple binary switching models.

**Main result.** Multi-state stochastic switching models provide a more accurate description of feast-famine cycles than binary models, allowing characterization of how fluctuation frequency and amplitude impact fixation probability and mean fixation time.

**Method.** The authors use computational (Gillespie simulations) and analytical techniques to characterize population dynamics using multi-state birth-death processes and Moran model approximations.

**Model / system.** The system models two competing microbial strains in an environment whose carrying capacity fluctuates stochastically among a finite number of intermediate states, generalizing classical binary telegraph noise.

**Key observables.** Population size statistics, fixation probability, mean fixation time, and the population size distribution (PSD).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Environmental switching rates ($
u$), carrying capacity distribution parameters ($\omega$), fitness difference ($s$), and initial strain fraction ($x_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on coarse-graining resource limitation into a time-varying carrying capacity $K(t)$ and uses approximations like the Moran process for finite populations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic switching dynamics (3-state vs 5-state) and sample paths of environmental states, population size, and strain proportions over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of binary models by introducing the multi-state stochastic switching framework. It then details the model setup, analyzes mean-field dynamics, and finally applies the Moran process to calculate key evolutionary diagnostics like fixation probability and mean fixation time.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Microbial populations generally evolve in fluctuating environments under time-varying conditions. These are often described by binary switching models, sometimes seen as coarse-grained feast-famine cycles, in which resource availability switches abruptly between abundant and scarce conditions. However, experimental studies suggest that feast-famine environments actually exhibit more complex temporal dynamics. Here, we study how two strains, one growing slightly slower than the other, compete for the same resources in fluctuating environments comprising a finite number of intermediate states, each having its own carrying capacity. Environmental switching between these states and their carrying capacities represents gradual changes in nutrient availability. This class of multi-state stochastic switching models can be interpreted as a coarse-grained description of feast--famine cycles and allows us to investigate strain competition under the gradual recovery and depletion of resources. By computational and analytical means, we characterise the population dynamics in these multi-state fluctuating environments. In particular, we study how the switching rates and distribution of carrying capacities affect the population-size statistics, fixation probability, and mean fixation time. By comparing these results with their counterparts in binary environments, we clarify how the frequency and amplitude of environmental fluctuations influence population dynamics in coarse-grained feast-famine cycles.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically analyzes how first-order coherence builds and evolves when a single bosonic mode interacts with a structured bath. By solving the full linear equations of motion, the authors demonstrate that reservoir memory effects (narrow spectral width) enhance transient coherence peaks. Furthermore, they show that active control via random frequency modulation can effectively suppress energy leakage into the environment, stabilizing quantum coherence at long times.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous analytical insights into non-Markovian open quantum system dynamics, which is crucial for designing robust quantum devices where environmental coupling must be precisely controlled to preserve coherence.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the exact dynamics of first-order system–bath coherence induced by excitation exchange in bilinear bosonic models.

**Main result.** Random frequency modulation is shown to be superior to periodic modulation for suppressing excitation leakage and maintaining stable, finite long-time coherence fluctuations.

**Method.** The authors solve the linear Heisenberg equations exactly, avoiding approximations like Born or Markov limits, to derive time evolution coefficients for system and bath operators.

**Model / system.** The model uses bilinear bosonic Hamiltonians describing a localized bosonic mode coupled to a structured continuum reservoir (e.g., cavity coupled to photonic/phononic crystal modes).

**Key observables.** First-order system–bath coherence ($C(t)$), system occupation number ($n_s(t)$), and the time evolution of coupling coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($\Gamma$), spectral width ($\gamma$, controlling bath memory time), detuning ($\Delta = \Omega - \omega_0$), and modulation parameters ($\delta\omega_{	ext{max}}$, $	au_{	ext{ran}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the linearity of the Heisenberg equations, allowing for closed-form solutions for expansion coefficients.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the effect of system temperature ($T_s$), spectral width ($\gamma$), and detuning ($\Delta$) on coherence. Crucially, they compare random versus rectangular modulation protocols, demonstrating random modulation's superior ability to suppress leakage and maintain coherence.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving exact dynamics using linear equations, analyzing the dependence of coherence on reservoir spectral features (memory/detuning), and finally developing a control scheme using LEO-inspired frequency modulation techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the exact dynamics of first-order system--bath coherence induced by excitation exchange in bilinear bosonic models. By solving the linear Heisenberg equations, we obtain the exact evolution of system and bath operators and evaluate the first-order coherence between the system mode and the collective bath mode directly coupled to it. We analyze how this coherence depends on initial occupations, coupling strength, spectral width, and detuning, showing that its buildup and oscillatory behavior are closely related to excitation exchange and reservoir memory. We further examine controlled coherence dynamics under leakage-elimination-operator inspired modulation of the system frequency. The results show that random modulation can suppress excitation leakage and maintain finite and relatively stable coherence fluctuations at long times. These results clarify the evolution and control of first-order system--bath coherence in settings where a localized bosonic mode is coupled to a structured bosonic reservoir.

</details>

**Summary.** The research experimentally demonstrates that the performance of a quantum heat engine can be optimized by controlling the system-reservoir coupling. Using ultracold atoms, the authors show that tuning the reservoir's kinetic temperature modifies the microscopic scattering rates governing heat transfer during the cycle's isochoric strokes. This provides a powerful tool for engineering finite-time energy conversion processes at the nanoscale.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges fundamental non-equilibrium thermodynamics with cutting-edge AMO physics. It shows how engineering the microscopic coupling (the reservoir interaction) can be a direct tool for optimizing macroscopic performance metrics like power in quantum engines, which is highly relevant to quantum energy technologies.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to enhance the power output of a quantum heat engine operating at finite time by microscopically controlling the energy exchange between the working system and its environment (reservoir).

**Main result.** The authors experimentally demonstrated that tuning the kinetic temperature of the reservoir allows for microscopic control over the heat transfer law, enabling optimization of the engine's power output while maintaining a fixed efficiency.

**Method.** The study uses an experimental platform involving ultracold atoms to realize and measure the performance of a quantum Otto cycle, analyzing how collision-mediated energy exchange dictates the time evolution during isochoric strokes.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum Otto engine using $^{133}	ext{Cs}$ atoms as the working medium coupled to an $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ atomic reservoir. Heat transfer occurs via inelastic $s$-wave collisions, which are modeled by rate matrices governing population dynamics during heating and cooling strokes.

**Key observables.** Power output ($P$), efficiency ($\eta$), cycle duration ($	au_{cyc}$), and the heat absorbed/released ($Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Kinetic temperature of the reservoir ($T_{	ext{Rb}}$), magnetic field difference ($\Delta B$), and the durations of the isochoric strokes ($	au_h, 	au_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Adiabatic work strokes are assumed short enough that no spin-exchange collisions occur; the control mechanism relies on the asymmetric energy scaling of scattering cross-sections for different collision types.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the four-stroke cycle (isochoric heating/cooling, adiabatic expansion/compression) and show how cycle times ($	au_{cyc}$) depend strongly on $T_{	ext{Rb}}$ but weakly on $\Delta B$, while efficiency shows a monotonic dependence on $\Delta B$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework for heat exchange via collision rates, details the four-stroke cycle dynamics using master equations, and then experimentally demonstrates that tuning $T_{	ext{Rb}}$ provides the primary handle to optimize power output by controlling the relative time allocation between heating and cooling strokes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The non-equilibrium properties of open quantum systems are determined by the microscopic laws governing energy exchange with their environment. In particular, an enhancement of the performance of quantum heat engines has been predicted by speeding up the dynamics through control of the system--bath interaction. However, direct microscopic control of heat transfer between the machine and the reservoir has remained elusive so far. Here, we experimentally demonstrate such control in a quantum Otto engine realized with ultracold Cs-133 atoms coupled to an atomic reservoir of ultracold Rb-87 atoms. Heat exchange between the two is mediated by inelastic s-wave collisions whose energy-dependent scattering cross sections lead to an asymmetric equilibration dynamics in the isochoric heating and cooling strokes. By tuning the kinetic temperature of the atomic reservoir, we modify the associated microscopic scattering rates, and thereby the heat transfer law, giving control over the time allocation within the engine cycle through control over the microscopic, multi-exponential relaxation dynamics. This enables power output optimization at fixed efficiency. Our results establish microscopic control of system-reservoir interactions as a tool for manipulating heat flow at the nanoscale and engineering the finite-time performance of quantum thermal machines.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the degradation of quantum entanglement and teleportation fidelity when qubits pass through time-varying decoherence channels. By mapping resource thresholds onto a fixed parameter space, the authors derive bounds on how much coherence can 'backflow' due to modulation. The results show that while resources decay overall, controlled modulation can lead to temporary revivals of quantum advantage.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical bounds on how quantum resources can be temporarily restored or enhanced by time-varying environments, which is crucial for designing robust quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates quantum teleportation fidelity through time-varying, non-Markovian decoherence channels.

**Main result.** The analysis establishes a complete positivity bound on the backflow of coherence ($\Delta\Gamma_k \le 2\pi\gamma_{k,0}/\Omega$) and shows that modulation can induce transient revival of quantum advantage.

**Method.** The authors model decoherence using time-local master equations, analyzing resource degradation by mapping thresholds onto a fixed $(p, q)$ plane.

**Model / system.** The system involves teleporting an unknown qubit state via a shared entangled pair passing through a link combining amplitude damping and dephasing at time-dependent rates. The dynamics are parameterized by two accumulated damping factors, $p(t)$ and $q(t)$.

**Key observables.** Negativity ($N$), Fully Entangled Fraction ($f$), Quantum Discord ($D$), and Average Teleportation Fidelity ($ar{F}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Accumulated damping parameters $p(t)$ and $q(t)$; modulation depth $\xi_k$; the critical threshold condition $p+q \ge 1$ for entanglement vanishing.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The time evolution separates into a fixed part and a moving one, depending only on accumulated damping parameters $p(t)$ and $q(t)$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the rate profiles ($\gamma_1(t), \Gamma_1(t)$) across static, fast, and modulated regimes. Figure 2 plots resource measures like negativity and fidelity over time for one-sided exposure.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the dynamics using accumulated parameters $p(t)$ and $q(t)$, then analyzes different noise geometries (one-sided vs. two-sided), deriving key bounds from complete positivity, and finally discussing resource revival phenomena under harmonic modulation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A Bell pair distributed through a link that combines amplitude damping with dephasing at time-dependent rates has dynamics that separate into a fixed part and a moving one. The negativity, fully entangled fraction, discord, and average teleportation fidelity depend on time only through the accumulated damping parameters $p(t)$ and $q(t)$. Every threshold is therefore a curve fixed in the unit square, and the rates select nothing but a trajectory across it. The Horodecki fidelity formula $\bar{F} = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{6}\mathrm{Tr}|T|$ covers one- and two-sided exposure alike: the condition $\det T \leq 0$ under which it takes this form holds throughout the unit square for both. Under symmetric two-sided noise the entanglement vanishes when $p+q\geq1$, where the exact relation $\bar{F}^{2s}=\frac{2}{3}+\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{N}_{2s}$ ties disentanglement and the loss of quantum advantage to the same instant. One-sided exposure admits no finite-time sudden death for any rate profile, and the discord stays strictly positive throughout the open square, so the distributed state can be separable, useless for teleportation, and still nonclassical. For harmonically modulated rates, complete positivity caps the backflow at one modulation period of static decay, $ΔΓ_{k}\leq2πγ_{k,0}/Ω$, equivalently at a modulation depth $ξ_{k}\leq4.6033$ independent of $Ω$. Inside that window the trajectory reverses, producing finite intervals of restored quantum advantage and entanglement sudden birth.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a general geometric theory to analyze the late-time dynamics of noisy quantum many-body systems with continuous symmetries. By mapping the evolution onto the geometry of $k$-commutant manifolds, it explains anomalous decay rates and entanglement growth using concepts from quantum geometry. This framework offers systematic tools for studying observables in complex, non-equilibrium settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful geometric framework for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in complex quantum systems, linking symmetry structure directly to observable scaling laws like entanglement entropy.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates unitary quantum dynamics in noisy many-body systems possessing global continuous symmetries, focusing on how entanglement and correlations evolve over time.

**Main result.** The late-time evolution is governed by the quantum geometry of effective $k$-commutant manifolds, providing a geometric explanation for sub-ballistic entanglement growth and anomalous correlator decay linked to manifold singularities.

**Method.** The authors employ replica theory, mapping dynamics to low-energy physics on effective Hamiltonians, and utilize the Time-Dependent Variational Principle (TDVP) adapted for singular ground state manifolds.

**Model / system.** The analysis covers noisy Brownian models with continuous symmetries ($U(1), SU(2)$), generalized to interacting systems whose dynamics are characterized by $k$-commutant algebras. Specific toy models include the $es\eta$ model and free-fermion systems.

**Key observables.** Rényi entanglement entropies, hydrodynamic and non-hydrodynamic correlators, and the geometry of $k$-commutants.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry group ($U(1)$ vs. $SU(2)$), replica number ($k$), and system parameters defining the noise/interaction strength ($\lambda$, $\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key challenges involve treating ground state manifolds that possess singularities, requiring extensions beyond standard smooth manifold dynamics treatments like TDVP.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but mention calculations involving overlaps and entropy scaling for specific models.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a review: Section II covers Brownian models; Section III characterizes $k$-commutant manifolds; Section IV applies TDVP to smooth systems (Heisenberg); Section V introduces singularity physics via toy models; Sections VI and VII address entanglement growth in $U(1)$ and $SU(2)$ symmetric systems, respectively.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study unitary quantum dynamics in noisy Brownian models with global continuous symmetries, such as $U(1)$ and $SU(2)$, focusing on Rényi entanglement entropies and hydrodynamic and non-hydrodynamic correlators. By mapping the averaged late-time dynamics to the low-energy physics of effective replica Hamiltonians, we find that the evolution is controlled by the quantum geometry of their ground-state manifolds, which is directly related to the geometry of $k$-commutants---the symmetry algebra of $k$ replicas of the system. In interacting systems, these $k$-commutants are generically determined solely by the symmetries of the system, independent of microscopic details of the noisy evolution. This allows us to use the time-dependent variational principle (TDVP) to provide simple geometric explanations for the sub-ballistic Rényi entanglement growth and the anomalous decay of non-hydrodynamic correlators in interacting systems with continuous symmetries. We find this behavior to be intimately connected to singularities within the $k$-commutant manifolds, arising from frozen ``void'' states in the Hilbert space that exist due to continuous on-site symmetries. This also demystifies the important role of voids in the dynamics of these observables, previously identified in $U(1)$ symmetric systems. We compare these behaviors in interacting systems with Abelian and non-Abelian continuous symmetries and in free-fermion systems, which differ in the geometry of their $k$-commutants. Ultimately, this work provides a general geometric framework for systematically studying observables in noisy systems with continuous symmetries, including Haar-random circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally demonstrates how circularly polarized light can induce transient effective magnetic fields in graphene by selectively exciting transitions between Landau quantized energy levels. By analyzing the resulting magneto-optical response, the authors establish a clear microscopic picture of the electronic inverse Faraday effect, opening avenues for ultrafast control over spin and magnetoelectric functionalities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a clean, tunable platform to observe fundamental many-body quantum coupling effects (like the inverse Faraday effect) in Dirac materials using light, which is highly relevant for developing next-generation opto-spintronic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating light-induced effective magnetic fields generated by circularly polarized radiation in graphene, relevant for ultrafast spin control.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates a direct, time-resolved link between resonant Landau-level excitation and the modification of optical Hall conductivity, generating an effective magnetic field on the order of 10 mT.

**Method.** Time-resolved pump-probe spectroscopy using circularly polarized terahertz pulses ($\sigma^+$ and $\sigma^-$) to selectively excite non-equidistant Landau quantized states in graphene.

**Model / system.** The physical system is graphene, a prototypical Dirac material, studied under an applied magnetic field ($B$) where electron energy levels are quantized into Landau levels. The analysis focuses on the electronic origin of the inverse Faraday effect.

**Key observables.** Transient Faraday rotation signals ($\Delta	heta$), differential transmission ($\Delta T/T$), and the extracted effective magnetic field ($\mathbf{B}_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pump fluence ($\sim 1.6 \mu	ext{J}/	ext{cm}^2$); Effective magnetic field magnitude ($\sim 10 	ext{ mT}$); Landau level transitions ($n=-1 	o n=0$, etc.).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fast-timescale dynamics are attributed purely to electronic effects via the optical Hall conductivity, isolating this mechanism from phonon or sublattice contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show energy diagrams of Landau levels and time traces of polarization rotation ($\Delta	heta$) vs. delay time at various magnetic fields, demonstrating field dependence across resonance.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem (ultrafast spin control), details the experimental setup using selective resonant excitation in graphene's Landau levels, analyzes the magneto-optical response by separating contributions ($\Delta	heta_F$), and concludes that the signal originates from the optical Hall conductivity mechanism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ultrafast magnetism triggered by circularly polarized radiation underpins ultrafast spin control, relevant to future technologies, e.g., opto-spintronics and magnonics. The dynamics are often complicated and intertwined among correlated subsystems, such as electrons, spins, phonons, plasmons, topology, and lattice, due to many-body quantum coupling at ultrafast timescales. Here, we demonstrate light-induced effective magnetic fields generated by selective excitation between non-equidistant Landau quantized states in graphene, a prototypical Dirac material, using circularly polarized pulses. By magnetically tuning the Landau-level transition resonance away from other low-energy excitations, we obtain a clean electrostatically controllable platform and identify the microscopic origin of the light-induced magnetic signals, independent of sublattice coupling. Because different Landau levels carry distinct optical Hall conductivities, direct modification of their occupancies via optical excitations creates transient Faraday rotation signals with dispersive magnetic-field dependence, mirroring the static magneto-optical lineshape. The induced effective magnetic field normalized by the pump electric field exceeds typical reported values for the inverse Faraday effect of electronic origin. Our results establish a clear microscopic picture of the inverse Faraday effect of electronic origin, which can trigger hierarchical dynamics among correlated sublattices once Landau-level transitions are magnetically tuned to coincide with other low-energy excitations in Dirac systems and related materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reformulates general optical imaging as a multiparameter quantum estimation problem and proposes using Diffractive Optical Neural Networks (DONN) to build an optimal measurement apparatus. By training the DONN, the authors show it can saturate fundamental quantum precision limits, enabling super-resolution image reconstruction that surpasses classical diffraction limitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum metrology with deep learning, providing a concrete, scalable physical implementation (the DONN) that achieves theoretically predicted quantum limits for complex sensing tasks like superresolution microscopy.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how to perform general imaging, which is formulated as a multiparameter quantum estimation problem of spatial-frequency amplitudes, aiming to surpass the diffraction limit.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that an architecture based on Diffractive Optical Neural Networks (DONN) can saturate the theoretical quantum limits (specifically the Nagaoka-Hayashi Cramér-Rao bound), achieving superresolution reconstruction.

**Method.** The authors combine quantum estimation theory (using bounds like NHCRB and QCRB) with machine learning by training a DONN to implement an optimal measurement apparatus that estimates multiple object amplitudes simultaneously.

**Model / system.** The physical system is optical imaging, modeled as collecting a quantum state whose parameters are the unknown Fourier-cosine expansion coefficients of the object intensity. The proposed measurement device is the DONN, which acts as a trainable unitary transformation followed by photon counting.

**Key observables.** Object amplitudes ($	heta = \{a_k\}$), Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (FIM), and measured photon counts ($n_d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low-contrast regime ($a_k \ll a_0$), number of estimated parameters ($M$), and the achievable quantum limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on the low-contrast approximation ($ho 	o ho_0$) for deriving analytic bounds, and assumes Poissonian statistics for photon counts when calculating the estimator variance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that the trained DONN saturates the quantum limit across single, two, and multiple amplitude estimations, showing superior performance compared to direct imaging deconvolution in 2D reconstruction.

**Paper structure.** The paper first formulates general imaging as a quantum estimation problem, deriving theoretical bounds (NHCRB). It then introduces the DONN architecture, trains it using gradient descent to minimize the bound loss, and finally validates its performance by comparing reconstructions against direct imaging methods across increasing numbers of estimated parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We cast general imaging as multiparameter quantum estimation of band-limited spatial-frequency amplitudes. For separable (single-copy) measurements, we compute precision limits using semidefinite programming to evaluate the Nagaoka-Hayashi Cramér-Rao bound. We then introduce an architecture for a measurement apparatus based on diffractive optical neural networks and photon counting that saturates this bound. Extending the framework to arbitrary objects and many amplitudes, we show image reconstructions in which our architecture recovers fine features at the quantum limit, outperforming direct imaging. Together, these results open a scalable route to saturating multiparameter quantum limits in superresolution microscopy, telescopy, and remote sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a density-matrix theory to study how the quantum structure of vortex electrons is maintained during acceleration in circular lattices. It models noise effects using a Lindblad master equation, showing that while ideal transport preserves an OAM invariant, stochastic imperfections cause leakage quantified by specific rates for real accelerators.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous framework for analyzing decoherence and quantum state preservation in highly coherent particle beam dynamics, drawing parallels between accelerator physics and open quantum systems theory (Lindblad master equations).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a density-matrix theory to determine if state-resolved quantum structure, specifically intrinsic Orbital Angular Momentum (OAM), can be preserved during the transport of vortex electrons in accelerator lattices.

**Main result.** Ideal transport preserves the Lewis-Floquet OAM invariant stroboscopically; however, stochastic imperfections generate a Lindblad channel, with quadrupole noise driving an intrinsic $\Delta\ell = \pm 2$ leakage rate that can be quantified for specific accelerators.

**Method.** The analysis uses density-matrix theory, deriving a master equation incorporating coherent evolution (from the Hamiltonian) and irreversible decoherence (from stochastic noise terms).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as vortex electrons moving in periodic round accelerator lattices. The dynamics are described by an effective quadratic Hamiltonian, leading to a density matrix evolution governed by both unitary and Lindblad terms.

**Key observables.** Lewis-Floquet orbital angular momentum (OAM) invariant ($\hat{I}_L$), local kinetic OAM ($\hat{L}_{kin}$), centroid smearing, and intrinsic $\Delta\ell = \pm 2$ leakage rate ($\Lambda_q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The inverse initial leakage scale is calculated for specific machines (e.g., $2	imes10^5$ turns for IOTA).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on quadratic approximations of the Hamiltonian and separates deterministic mismatch from stochastic noise sources to construct the Lindblad master equation.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but comparisons are made between laboratory smearing limits ($\Lambda^{-1}_d$) and intrinsic quadrupole leakage rates ($\Lambda^{-1}_q$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the system Hamiltonian and invariants (Lewis-Floquet basis) to deriving the full master equation incorporating noise sources. It then analyzes how different types of noise perturb the conserved quantum structure, culminating in quantitative estimates of coherence loss.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a density-matrix theory of vortex-electron transport in accelerator lattices. In a periodic round lattice, the protected object is a Lewis-Floquet orbital angular momentum (OAM) invariant rather than the instantaneous kinetic OAM. Ideal transport has a metaplectic lift; stochastic imperfections generate a Lindblad channel. Dipole noise produces removable centroid smearing; quadrupole noise drives intrinsic $Δ\ell=\pm2$ leakage. Under explicit white-noise benchmarks, the inverse initial leakage scale is $2\times10^5$ turns for IOTA and $2\times10^6$ turns for PETRA III.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper unifies the theory of physical backpropagation by developing an adjoint-based framework for computing gradients in physical systems. It derives necessary and sufficient conditions—such as reciprocity—for a system to compute its own gradient on-device. This provides a general template for designing physically realizable, exact learning algorithms across various domains like photonics and mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical framework connecting fundamental physics principles (like reciprocity) directly to the practical requirements of training complex models on analog or photonic hardware, bridging quantum optics/AMO concepts with machine learning optimization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a unifying theoretical framework for achieving formally exact gradient computation (physical backpropagation) in physical computing systems.

**Main result.** It establishes sufficient conditions, based on the adjoint method, under which the required adjoint field can be generated physically on the same hardware performing the forward computation.

**Method.** The core methodology is the application of the adjoint method from PDE-constrained optimization to derive gradient formulas that rely on overlaps between forward and adjoint fields.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to diverse physical systems modeled by discretized ODEs, including photonic circuits, mechanical networks, and general nonlinear trajectory systems. The governing dynamics are generally represented as second-order differential equations.

**Key observables.** The gradient of the cost function ($\mathrm{d}\chi/\mathrm{d}p$), the adjoint field ($a(t)$), and various overlap integrals between forward and adjoint states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Reciprocity, time-reversal symmetry, and the specific structure of linear vs. nonlinear dynamics are critical parameters determining feasibility.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes an external teacher applies parameter updates; it does not cover self-adapting systems or heuristic learning rules.

**Figures summary.** Conceptual diagrams illustrate the adjoint method applied to physical optimization, showing the relationship between forward passes, loss functions, and the required adjoint solves for different system types.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the general problem (PDE-constrained optimization), developing the adjoint formalism, deriving specific conditions for linear vs. nonlinear systems (reciprocity/time-reversal symmetry), and finally unifying these results to recover known physical learning algorithms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Physical computing systems exploit device dynamics for computation, but their gradient-based optimization is challenging: backpropagation through a digital twin suffers from model-reality gap. On-device gradient computation could resolve this issue, and a handful of theoretical and experimental studies have proposed ways to achieve it. Yet a unifying theory identifying when a physical system can compute the gradient of its own performance has been missing. Here we develop such a unification, based on the adjoint method: we identify sufficient conditions under which the adjoint field required for formally exact gradients can be generated on the same hardware that performs the computation. Linear and nonlinear systems obey fundamentally different conditions: for linear systems damping or gain is admissible provided reciprocity is preserved. For nonlinear trajectory systems the sufficient conditions are reciprocity of the linearized system and the existence of a time-reversal mirror. Algorithmically, the nonlinear case requires infinitesimal nudging, whereas linear systems admit a finite-amplitude experiment. We recover Equilibrium Propagation, Hamiltonian echo backpropagation, fully forward mode training and in situ gradient methods in integrated-photonic and free-space-optical systems. We further show that reciprocity is only the simplest instance of a more general intertwining condition, which extends exact on-device gradient computation to a class of non-Hermitian, non-reciprocal systems. Further generalizations include time-dependent parameters, Onsager-reciprocal dynamics and nonlinear, PT-symmetric Schrödinger equations. Our work provides a unified theoretical basis for formally exact physical learning algorithms and a template for constructing them across a range of physical systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the equilibrium state of a Riesz gas confined to a half-space. It proves that the system's behavior undergoes a sharp transition depending on the interaction strength $s$. Specifically, it identifies a critical wall position beyond which the particles are forced entirely onto the boundary for strongly long-ranged interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, understanding phase transitions and critical thresholds in interacting particle systems is fundamental to many-body physics, including quantum gas models where effective interactions can be long-ranged.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the equilibrium state of a Riesz gas changes when confined to a half-space, specifically determining if this confinement exhibits qualitative transitions based on the interaction range.

**Main result.** A dichotomy exists: for weakly long-ranged interactions ($s \in (d-2, d)$), the measure always has bulk support; however, for strongly long-ranged interactions ($s \in (d-3, d-2]$), a critical wall position $a_{crit}$ dictates whether the system is fully confined to the wall.

**Method.** The analysis uses variational methods by minimizing an energy functional and proving necessary conditions via Euler–Lagrange inequalities applied to the constrained equilibrium measure.

**Model / system.** The model is a Riesz gas with interaction parameter $s \in (d-3, d)$ in dimension $d$, confined to a half-space by a hard wall. The energy functional involves the Riesz kernel $g_s(x)$.

**Key observables.** The nature of the equilibrium measure $\mu_a$: whether it retains non-trivial bulk support or becomes entirely supported on the confining wall.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction exponent $s$, dimension $d$, and the critical wall position $a_{crit}(d, s)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to $s \in (d-3, d)$; specific mathematical tools like the incomplete beta function and properties of Gauss hypergeometric functions are employed.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate particle configurations showing non-confinement in the weakly long-ranged regime and demonstrating full confinement at a critical wall position in the strongly long-ranged regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by defining the energy functional, then proves the dichotomy theorem (Theorem 2.2) by analyzing the Euler–Lagrange inequality across different regimes of $s$, culminating in rigorous proofs for confinement conditions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a Riesz gas with interaction parameter $s \in (d-3,d)$ in an arbitrary spatial dimension $d$, confined to a half-space by a hard wall. We prove that the equilibrium measure exhibits a dichotomy according to the interaction range. For $s \in (d-2,d)$, corresponding to the weakly long-ranged regime, the equilibrium measure always retains a non-trivial bulk component and thus is never completely confined to the wall. In contrast, for $s \in (d-3,d-2]$, corresponding to the strongly long-ranged regime, a confinement transition occurs: there exists a critical wall position beyond which the equilibrium measure is supported entirely on the wall. Our theorem generalises recent results established for the Coulomb gas, corresponding to the case $s=d-2$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously studies 'tabletop reversibility' (TTR) for phase-covariant quantum channels. It establishes that TTR can be realized by a dilation for finite-dimensional systems but finds an obstruction for Gaussian ones. Crucially, it overcomes this limitation by constructing a non-Gaussian unitary architecture that successfully realizes TTR for the single-mode Gaussian amplification channel.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental constraints on quantum operations in open systems, which is central to understanding realistic physical implementations of quantum processes and resource theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the concept of 'tabletop reversibility' (TTR) for phase-covariant quantum operations, aiming to understand the fundamental interplay between irreversibility and time-translation symmetry in open quantum dynamics.

**Main result.** For finite dimensions, TTR is achievable for phase-covariant maps admitting a Petz recovery map. However, a concrete obstruction was found for Gaussian systems, though this was circumvented by constructing a non-Gaussian dilation that achieves TTR for the single-mode Gaussian amplification channel.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum channel theory, focusing on unitary dilations and leveraging structural lemmas (like Lemma 1) to construct global unitaries satisfying constraints from both the forward process and the Petz recovery map.

**Model / system.** The study considers general CPTP maps, specifically contrasting finite-dimensional systems with continuous-variable Gaussian systems. The model involves constructing unitary dilations $U$ acting on an extended system (System $\otimes$ Bath).

**Key observables.** Tabletop Reversibility (TTR), Petz recovery map realization, and the structure of the global unitary $U$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the system (finite vs. infinite/Gaussian) and the specific symmetry constraint (phase-covariance).

**Assumptions / limitations.** For finite dimensions, it assumes the Petz recovery map is well-defined; for Gaussian systems, the analysis relies on constructing non-Gaussian unitaries to bypass theoretical obstructions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework of TTR and phase covariance. It then proves the positive result for finite dimensions using structural theorems. Subsequently, it contrasts this with Gaussian systems by exhibiting an obstruction, before finally providing a constructive counterexample using a non-Gaussian unitary to show TTR is possible for the single-mode Gaussian amplification channel.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Irreversibility and time-translation symmetry are both fundamental in open quantum dynamics, and it is of great importance to study the interplay between them. In this paper, we study tabletop reversibility (TTR) for phase-covariant quantum operations and obtain sharply different conclusions for finite-dimensional and Gaussian systems. For finite-dimensional systems, we prove that any phase-covariant operation that admits a Petz recovery map can be implemented by a dilation which is both time-translational symmetric and tabletop time reversible. In contrast, for phase-covariant Gaussian operations we exhibit a concrete obstruction: any Gaussian dilation of a single-mode Gaussian amplification channel is not tabletop time reversible. As a byproduct, we find that almost every completely positive and trace preserving (CPTP) map admits a dilation that realizes TTR. These results clarify whether and how TTR can be realized under phase-covariant symmetry constraints and reveal a qualitative difference between finite-dimensional and Gaussian systems.

</details>
