**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel, unified interferometric framework to mathematically connect Interaction-Free Measurement and Delayed-Choice experiments. By modeling the system using an extended MZI circuit involving four qubits, it shows that both phenomena arise from the same underlying quantum evolution. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding wave-particle duality and counterfactual information extraction simultaneously.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for quantum optics and open quantum systems, providing a unified mathematical description that connects fundamental concepts like wave-particle duality (DC) with counterfactual measurements (IFM).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a theoretical framework to unify two distinct quantum phenomena: Interaction-Free Measurement (IFM) and Delayed-Choice (DC) experiments.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that IFM and DC behavior share a common operational origin, emerging from the same underlying interferometric structure controlled by ancillary qubits.

**Method.** The authors use a quantum circuit representation based on an extended Mach-Zehnder Interferometer involving four qubits to model the coherent evolution of the system.

**Model / system.** The model utilizes an MZI setup incorporating a 'quantum bomb' (absorber) and a quantum-controlled second beam splitter ($	ext{BS}2$). The system is tracked using four interacting qubits ($|q_0angle, |q_1angle, |q_2angle, |q_3angle$).

**Key observables.** Detector probabilities $P(D0)$ and $P(D1)$, the conditional IFM probability $P(	ext{IFM}|	ext{survival})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $	heta$ (initial path superposition), $eta$ (bomb interaction strength), and $\alpha$ (delayed choice control parameter).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that absorbed photons do not participate in further unitary evolution, regardless of the BS2 presence.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the optical realization and quantum circuit diagram. Figure 4 shows detector probabilities transitioning continuously from particle-like to wave-like behavior as $\alpha$ varies. Figure 5 plots the conditional IFM probability versus $\alpha$ and $eta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first defining the four-qubit MZI model, then deriving the post-selected state $|\Psi_{	ext{post}}angle$, calculating detector probabilities $P(D0)$ and $P(D1)$ as continuous functions of $\alpha$, and finally analyzing limiting cases to show unification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A theoretical unified interferometric framework is developed for interaction-free measurement (IFM) and delayed-choice (DC) experiments. The proposed model leads to a quantum circuit based architecture in which an ancillary qubit coherently controls the interaction between a photon and a bomb, allowing the system to evolve in a superposition of interaction and non-interaction regimes. The ancillary control qubit serves as a quantum switch that continuously interpolates between IFM and DC behavior, revealing a common operational origin for both phenomena. We demonstrate that both the phenomena can be realized within this framework through controlled quantum-gate operations. The analysis shows that the framework enables a continuous transition between the particle-like and wave-like behavior in DC setup. The framework is shown to be consistent with standard quantum mechanics, preserving unitary evolution, quantum superposition and the measurement postulate.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates a stochastic min-plus process on a binary tree, analyzing its behavior across different parameter regimes defined by $p$. Using extensive Monte Carlo simulations and mathematical identities, the authors confirm that at criticality ($p=1/2$), the root value exhibits stretched-exponential scaling matching the Beta(2,1) distribution. The work provides both asymptotic theoretical results and precise finite-depth numerical confirmations for this complex random process.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum systems, the analysis of recursive random processes on tree structures and the identification of critical exponents ($\sqrt{N}$ scaling) are highly relevant to understanding entanglement structure or correlation decay in many quantum physical models (e.g., tensor network renormalization group flows).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the asymptotic distribution of $X_N$, the root value, for a min-plus stochastic process defined recursively on a binary tree as the depth $N$ approaches infinity.

**Main result.** The paper corroborates that in the critical regime ($p=1/2$), the scaled logarithm of the root value follows a stretched-exponential limit characterized by the Beta(2,1) distribution. Furthermore, it derives an exact Bernoulli-leaf identity for specific initial conditions.

**Method.** The research combines large Monte Carlo simulations (up to $N=60$) with rigorous mathematical analysis, including deriving closed-form solutions and performing extrapolation techniques on scaling laws.

**Model / system.** The model is the min-plus stochastic process defined recursively on a rooted binary tree. The evolution at each node depends probabilistically on whether the parent value is determined by summation (probability $p$) or minimum (probability $1-p$).

**Key observables.** $X_N$ (root value), $\mathbb{E}[\log X_N]$, and the order parameter $P(X_N=0)$ for Bernoulli initial conditions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control parameter $p$, which defines three regimes: critical ($p=1/2$), sub-critical ($p<1/2$), and super-critical ($p>1/2$). The scaling rate is $\sqrt{N}$ in the critical case.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that finite-depth simulations can accurately corroborate established asymptotic theorems, particularly concerning the $	ext{Beta}(2,1)$ limit and the identification of universality classes for random homogeneous systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the empirical distribution of $X_N$ confirming the Beta(2,1) profile at criticality, show scaling tests for $\mathbb{E}[\log X_N]$ vs. $\sqrt{N}$, and display the exact identity $P(X_N=0)=q$ for the Bernoulli variant.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model and analyzes off-critical regimes to find closed forms for sub-critical convergence. It then focuses heavily on the critical case ($p=1/2$), using simulations to confirm the $	ext{Beta}(2,1)$ scaling limit. Finally, it derives an exact identity for a Bernoulli variant, locating phase transitions in the operator-mixing probability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The min-plus process is a stochastic coagulation-annihilation-type process on the binary tree, of interest in mathematics, physics, and computer science as a tractable instance of max-type recursive distributional equations. We carry out large Monte Carlo simulations at effective tree depths up to $N=60$ that provide finite-depth corroboration of the Beta(2,1) stretched-exponential limit for its root value $X_{N}$ at $p=1/2$, on the asymmetric $\sqrt{N}$ side of the random-homogeneous-systems classification recently introduced by Chen, Duquesne, and Shi and by Morfe. Off criticality, our simulations confirm the sub-critical closed form $\mathbb{P}(X_{\infty}=1)=(1-2p)/(1-p)$ within Monte Carlo error and document a super-critical mean growth exceeding the elementary $(2p)^{N}$ lower bound at the depths we reach. For a Bernoulli($q$)-initial-condition variant, we identify an elementary closed-form identity at $p=1/2$ that pins down the order parameter $\mathbb{P}(X_{N}=0)=q$ exactly, locates the absorbing-state phase transition at $p_{c}=1/2$ in the operator-mixing probability rather than in the initial-zero density, and shows that the conditional law on positives deforms substantially with $q$. Our simulations use a level-wise recursion and an FFT-based precomputed leaf table which reduce the effective simulation depth while preserving the recursive tree law and may be useful for the simulation of related recursive equations on large trees.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors used temperature-dependent Raman spectroscopy on the magnetic material $	ext{Na}_2	ext{Cu}_3	ext{Ge}_4	ext{O}_{12}$ to study spin-lattice coupling. They found a critical scaling behavior near $T_c \approx 167$ K, demonstrating that phonon anomalies are directly governed by the underlying dynamic spin correlations. This establishes NCGO as an excellent model for studying cooperative quantum phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly links lattice dynamics (phonons) to electronic/magnetic correlations (spin excitations), which is a core topic in understanding emergent phenomena in strongly correlated quantum materials relevant to AMO physics and open quantum systems modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates spin-lattice coupling-mediated quantum correlations and critical scaling behavior of phonon dynamics in low-dimensional magnetic materials.

**Main result.** The research found evidence that the phonon renormalization across a magnetic crossover is driven by dynamic spin states, establishing the material as a model system for cooperative spin–lattice coupling.

**Method.** Temperature-dependent Raman spectroscopy was used to measure both sharp phonon peaks and broad spectral backgrounds related to spin excitations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the antiferromagnetically coupled spin-$1/2$ trimer chain $	ext{Na}_2	ext{Cu}_3	ext{Ge}_4	ext{O}_{12}$ (NCGO). The structure involves a zigzag arrangement of copper trimers.

**Key observables.** Dynamic spin susceptibility ($\chi''$) from the background spectrum, phonon integral intensity ($I_i$), and critical exponents ($eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $T_c = 167 \pm 1 	ext{ K}$ (critical temperature) and $eta = 0.24 \pm 0.02$ (critical exponent). Measurements span from 80 K to 400 K.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Interchain interactions are assumed weak; the analysis relies on fitting the Raman response function with phonon Lorentzians and a background term for spin excitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure, schematic of exchange couplings, and show temperature-dependent Raman spectra used to extract both sharp phonon modes and broad spectral backgrounds related to spin dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by characterizing the material's magnetic and structural properties using DFT calculations, followed by detailed temperature-dependent Raman measurements. Key analyses include fitting the background for dynamic susceptibility and analyzing power-law scaling of phonon intensities near the critical point.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Low-dimensional quantum magnets provide an ideal platform to explore spin-lattice coupling-mediated quantum correlations, which give rise to emergent quasiparticle excitations. The antiferromagnetically coupled spin-1/2 trimer chain of copper ions in Na$_2$Cu$_3$Ge$_4$O$_{12}$ (NCGO) hosts high-energy spin excitations of different species, whose energy scales overlap with those of lattice vibrations. Here, we report a comprehensive temperature-dependent Raman spectroscopic study performed between 80 and 400 K. The dynamic spin susceptibility, as obtained from the analysis of the broad spectral background, reveals the emergence of quasiparticle excitations below 170 K. We further identify an unusual crossover of phonon dynamics when the material transits from a normal paramagnetic state to a correlated quantum magnetic state. A power law dependence of the integrated Raman susceptibility of the phonon modes, $I_{χ^{\prime\prime}}^{i}\sim|T-T_{c}|^β$, is observed with the critical temperature $T_c$=167$\pm$1 K, and critical exponent $β= 0.24\pm 0.02$. The combined results obtained from the broad spectral background and sharp phonon peaks further indicate that the phonon renormalization observed across the crossover is driven by dynamic spin states. Additionally, statistical correlations among phonon energy eigenvalues, quantified through matrix-norm and power-test analyses of 200 spectra recorded at 80 K, reveal an unexpected linear correlation among phonon modes, also indicating that the collective lattice response is governed by spin correlations. These findings establish NCGO as a model system for investigating cooperative spin-lattice coupling and critical scaling behavior of phonon dynamics in low-dimensional magnetic materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This review surveys magnetic active matter, detailing how intrinsic magnetic dipoles drive self-organization in systems ranging from bacteria to synthetic robots. It synthesizes theoretical models and experimental results showing that dipolar interactions are key for controlling collective behaviors like swarming and pattern formation. The work highlights the broad applicability of these principles across diverse physical scales.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical non-equilibrium dynamics, the study of collective behavior, pattern formation, and energy dissipation in these systems touches upon concepts relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and complex many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The review focuses on understanding how magnetic dipole interactions control and organize active matter across all length scales, from nanoscale particles to macroscopic robots.

**Main result.** Magnetic dipolar forces provide a versatile tool for achieving complex self-organization, leading to phenomena like chaining, swarming, and tunable pattern formation in non-equilibrium systems.

**Method.** The paper surveys both theoretical models (e.g., Langevin dynamics using point dipole approximations) and experimental realizations across various physical platforms.

**Model / system.** The system involves active particles carrying intrinsic magnetic dipoles whose interactions mediate self-organization, distinct from external field propulsion. Examples include magnetotactic bacteria, colloidal swimmers, and granular robots.

**Key observables.** Chaining, swarming, tunable pattern formation, alignment, collective hydrodynamic states (e.g., vortices), and reconfigurable structures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Péclet number (Pe) comparing activity strength to diffusion; dimensionless magnetic coupling parameter ($\lambda$) comparing dipolar energy to thermal energy ($k_B T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The point-dipole approximation is used for long-range interactions, and the dynamics are often modeled using overdamped Langevin equations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically classifies experimental realizations based on activity (Pe) and interaction strength ($\lambda$), categorizing systems into colloids, granular matter, and biological examples.

**Paper structure.** The review progresses by establishing the fundamental role of magnetic interactions in active matter, detailing the theoretical modeling across scales (nano to macro), surveying diverse physical implementations (bacteria, synthetic swimmers), and concluding with applications and open challenges.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic interactions provide a versatile and powerful tool for controlling and organizing active matter, where individual units continuously consume energy to drive autonomous motion. These interactions arise naturally in biological systems, such as magnetotactic bacteria, and can be engineered into synthetic platforms, including colloidal microswimmers, magnetic nanoparticles, and macroscopic granular robots. This review focuses on active, self-propelled particles that carry an intrinsic magnetic dipole moment, powered by their own energy consumption rather than driven by external fields; here, the dipole moment mediates interactions and self-organization, not propulsion. We survey experimental and theoretical studies across all length scales, showing how dipolar interactions shape single-particle dynamics, collective behavior, and self-organization. We discuss models incorporating pairwise dipolar forces and confinement, and examine emergent phenomena such as chaining, swarming, and tunable pattern formation. We close by outlining challenges and opportunities in the design, control, and application of magnetic active systems, from programmable materials and biomedical actuation to nonequilibrium physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work derives quantum inequalities—which bound how negative the local energy density can be—by applying the second law of thermodynamics. It models this using a thermal detector coupled to a quantum field, showing that entropy conservation dictates a minimum heat loss. This establishes a direct thermodynamic pathway to understanding fundamental energy constraints in quantum field theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, non-field-theoretic derivation of quantum inequalities by grounding them in fundamental thermodynamic principles (the Second Law), making it relevant for understanding energy constraints in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives quantum inequalities—bounds on negative energy density—by establishing a connection between these bounds and the second law of thermodynamics.

**Main result.** It shows that the non-negativity of entropy change (the Second Law) imposes a state-independent lower bound on the heat loss measured by a thermal detector, leading to quantum inequalities for the field's energy density.

**Method.** The analysis uses an operational framework involving coupling a quantum field to a small thermal detector and applying entropy balance arguments derived from thermodynamics.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a free, massless scalar field coupled via an Unruh-DeWitt interaction Hamiltonian to a harmonic oscillator detector. The dynamics are analyzed using perturbation theory in the coupling strength.

**Key observables.** Average heat absorbed by the detector ($\langle Q angle_\psi$), change in detector entropy ($\Delta S_A$), and time-averaged energy density ($\langle E_g[\psi] angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$) or inverse temperature ($eta=1/T$), coupling strength ($\lambda$), and the characteristic frequency ($\Omega$) of the detector.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed to second order in the coupling strength $\lambda$, and it assumes that for physical states, the first-order term in the density matrix change vanishes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from general principles of entropy conservation ($\Delta S_{tot}=0$) to applying the Second Law constraint ($\Delta S_A \le eta \langle Q angle$), which is then used in a perturbative calculation involving correlation functions to derive the energy bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum field theory allows local violations of the classical energy conditions, such as regions of negative energy density. The Ford-Roman quantum inequalities quantify how negative these regions can be and for how long. We present a derivation of these inequalities from the second law of thermodynamics, in an operational framework that couples the field to a small thermal detector. The detector acts as a physical probe of the field's energy density and, by the non-negativity of the entanglement entropy between the two systems, one can show that its heat loss is bounded from below by a state-independent quantity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses systematic errors in measuring nanoscale structural dynamics using Electron Correlation Microscopy by proposing a novel, physics-inspired intensity normalization technique. The method corrects for artifacts arising from misapplying X-ray scattering analysis tools (XPCS) to electron diffraction data. By validating this approach against simulations and applying it to experimental samples, the authors establish an artifact-free pathway for characterizing localized structural relaxation in disordered materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial methodological rigor for interpreting complex scattering data from disordered systems (like glasses or amorphous materials), which is highly relevant to understanding structural relaxation dynamics in condensed matter theory and experiment.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Previous Electron Correlation Microscopy (ECM) methods incorrectly applied X-ray Photon Correlation Spectroscopy (XPCS) normalization frameworks to nanobeam electron diffraction data, leading to systematic errors in measuring nanoscale structural relaxation times.

**Main result.** The authors developed a physics-inspired, ECM-specific normalization framework that successfully reproduces known relaxation dynamics using molecular dynamics simulations and correctly identifies stable crystalline phases in experimental nanowire data previously misclassified as relaxing domains.

**Method.** They propose an alternative intensity normalization scheme based on time- and azimuthal-averaging of the first diffraction ring intensities, which corrects for artifacts inherent to conventional XPCS analysis in nanoscale electron scattering geometries.

**Model / system.** The work uses molecular dynamics simulations of a supercooled liquid (CuZr) as a benchmark, and applies the method to experimental time-resolved 4D STEM datasets of a complex metallic nanowire ($	ext{Pt}_{57.5}	ext{Cu}_{14.7}	ext{Ni}_{5.3}	ext{P}_{22.5}$).

**Key observables.** Structural relaxation times and stretching exponents derived from intensity autocorrelation functions, specifically the self-intermediate scattering function ($	ext{self-ISF}$), $	ext{g}_2$, and $	ext{c}_2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nanoscale spatial resolution (1 nm probe size); time delays ($\Delta t$); temperature/cooling rate regimes in supercooled liquids.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the systematic errors arise from inappropriate normalization; the proposed method assumes isotropy and uses specific averaging over diffraction rings to correct for these artifacts.

**Figures summary.** Figure 12 compares simulated X-ray and electron scattering normalized intensity profiles, highlighting the difference between standard XPCS ranges and the actual electron speckle diameter sampled by ECM.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the systematic failure of XPCS normalization in ECM; it then proposes a new physics-inspired normalization framework; this is validated using MD simulations of supercooled liquids ($	ext{CuZr}$); finally, the method is applied to analyze experimental 4D STEM data on nanowires.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron correlation microscopy (ECM) can measure materials dynamics with nanoscale spatial resolution from intensity correlation functions. However, adopting X-ray photon correlation spectroscopy (XPCS) normalization frameworks unchanged when calculating intensity correlations can introduce errors. Due to the constrained sampling volumes and larger speckle sizes in nanobeam electron diffraction, XPCS-style time-averaging and scattering-vector averaging introduce systematic artifacts, such as artificial anticorrelations or elevated baselines that lead to systematic errors in structural relaxation times and stretching exponents. This work presents physics-inspired, ECM-specific intensity normalizations over time- and azimuthal-averaged intensities of the first diffraction ring that limit those errors. The framework is validated using molecular dynamics simulations of a CuZr supercooled liquid to benchmark against the self intermediate scattering function, successfully reproducing relaxation times. When applied to experimental time-resolved 4D STEM datasets of a Pt57.5Cu14.7Ni5.3P22.5 nanowire, the method correctly identifies highly stable, unchanging nanoscale crystalline phases that were erroneously misclassified as relaxing domains by previous frameworks. Other previous ECM research is reevaluated in light of these observation. This robust approach establishes an artifact-free pathway for evaluating localized spatiotemporal relaxation behaviors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes wave function localization in non-Hermitian quasicrystals where hopping amplitudes modulate through zero. By mapping the system onto a logarithmically correlated potential landscape, the authors derive a critical stiffness threshold that dictates whether the state localizes or remains multifractal. The resulting signatures—including unique gap scaling laws—provide a powerful diagnostic tool for understanding complex quantum transport in engineered non-Hermitian systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how non-Hermiticity, combined with quasiperiodicity, generates novel forms of spectral localization that are mathematically distinct from standard models. The connection to robust physical circuits (topolectrical) is highly relevant for AMO/Quantum Optics applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates localization phenomena in non-Hermitian quantum systems subjected to quasiperiodically modulated hopping that passes through zero (a singular regime). The goal is to characterize the resulting wave function behavior based on system parameters.

**Main result.** Localization occurs only when a derived 'stiffness' factor exceeds a critical threshold, leading to distinct signatures like super-logarithmic gap scaling in extreme-value statistics. This mechanism is robust and differs from standard Anderson or Aubry-André universality classes.

**Method.** The authors use gauge transformations to map the non-Hermitian system onto an effective potential landscape whose variance grows logarithmically with system size, allowing for rigorous mathematical analysis of localization criteria.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional Hatano-Nelson ring with quasiperiodically modulated nonreciprocal hopping. The Hamiltonian involves terms like $t \pm \mu \cos(2\pi \alpha n + 	heta)$, placing it in the realm of non-Hermitian quasicrystals.

**Key observables.** Landscape Variance ($	ext{Var}(\xi) \propto \ln L$), Stiffness ($g$), Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR), and the scaling exponent ($\gamma_\alpha$) governing peak gaps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Modulation depth ($\mu$), mean hopping ($t$), quasiperiodic frequency ($\alpha$), and the stiffness factor $g$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the non-Hermiticity into a gauge field whose fluctuations determine localization, assuming the dominant contribution to eigenstate modulus comes from this singular factor.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare localization mechanisms (NHSE vs. Anderson), show energy spectra and intensity profiles under periodic/open boundary conditions, and illustrate the phase diagram showing variance scaling versus system size.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the model via a gauge transformation, derives the logarithmic growth of landscape variance in the singular regime ($|\mu| > |t|$), defines the localization criterion based on stiffness $g$, and finally characterizes this mechanism using unique super-logarithmic gap statistics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a one-dimensional Hatano-Nelson ring whose nonreciprocal hopping is quasiperiodically modulated through zero. A gauge transformation maps every eigenstate onto a single spatial envelope, whose logarithm becomes a deterministic, logarithmically correlated field once the hopping vanishes along the quasiperiodic orbit. We derive an exact Fourier representation and prove that the landscape variance grows logarithmically with system size, with a stiffness set by the modulation power and the arithmetic of the incommensurate frequency. The extended phase terminates at an algebraic boundary given by Jensen's formula. Inside the singular regime, localization requires the stiffness to exceed a critical threshold: above it, the wavefunction weight concentrates on the few highest landscape peaks and the state is localized; below it, the weight spreads over too many competing peaks and the state is a critical multifractal. The extreme-value statistics are anomalous: peak gaps grow as a power of the logarithm of rank, with an exponent that encodes the continued-fraction type of the frequency, distinct from the Anderson, Aubry-André, and random-gauge universality classes. The stiffness adds across channels in multiband lattices, and all signatures survive percent-level component disorder, placing the mechanism within reach of nonreciprocal topolectrical circuits.

</details>

<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.11721_figures/2608.11721_fig5.jpg" alt="Figure 5">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 5.</b> Figure 5: Schematic one-loop one-particle-irreducible two-point kernel: the tree inverse kernel plus the scalar tadpole self-energy.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work develops a Finite-Proper-Time Field Theory framework for $\lambda\phi^4$ theory, treating a finite endpoint scale $s_0$ as a physical parameter. By calculating loop corrections to scattering amplitudes, the authors prove that this retained scale introduces non-redundant physics that cannot be removed by standard renormalization procedures or local field redefinitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the model is simple ($\lambda\phi^4$), the methodology of distinguishing between physical scales ($s_0$) and arbitrary regulators provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding how scale dependence enters quantum field theories beyond traditional RG flow descriptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To formulate a finite-proper-time construction for Euclidean scalar $\lambda\phi^4$ theory and rigorously determine if the dependence on the retained endpoint scale ($s_0$) survives standard renormalization procedures.

**Main result.** The analysis establishes that the retained finite proper-time scale $s_0$ introduces perturbative non-redundancy in scattering amplitudes, meaning it cannot be absorbed by local field redefinitions or parameter matching at one loop and beyond.

**Method.** The authors use functional methods involving paired open/closed spectral functions and a complete-history diagrammatic calculus to calculate effective actions and scattering amplitudes up to two loops.

**Model / system.** The theory analyzed is the Euclidean scalar $\lambda\phi^4$ model. The core novelty lies in defining the theory via a 'retained finite proper-time' scale ($s_0$), which differs fundamentally from standard UV cutoffs ($\Lambda_{UV}$) or renormalization group scales ($\mu$).

**Key observables.** The one-loop four-point scattering amplitude, specifically its on-shell dependence and the correlation between coefficients in the low-energy effective action.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The retained finite proper-time scale $s_0$, which is treated as a physical parameter distinct from regulators or renormalization scales.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to fixed-order Euclidean calculations, and the authors explicitly state that this does not imply nonperturbative Lorentzian completion or solve all open questions regarding field redefinition equivalence.

**Figures summary.** Figure 11 illustrates the angular dependence of the leading non-constant finite-endpoint correction to massless $2 	o 2$ scalar scattering, showing a specific predicted shape ($3 + \cos^2	heta$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first introducing the FPT formalism using spectral functions and deriving diagrammatic rules. It then calculates loop corrections (one-loop four-point function) to demonstrate that $s_0$ dependence is non-redundant, culminating in theorems proving this non-equivalence to standard local renormalization schemes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate a finite-proper-time construction for Euclidean scalar $λφ^4$ theory in which a retained endpoint $s_0$ is assigned to complete internal histories rather than independently to individual Schwinger segments. The theory is defined through paired open and closed spectral functions and their Fréchet/Duhamel hierarchy. Functional differentiation inserts operators along an existing proper-time history and partitions its total length, while interaction vertices sew separately complete histories. We introduce a corresponding complete-history diagrammatic calculus and derive the resulting one- and two-loop structures. We distinguish the retained endpoint from an auxiliary regulator or renormalisation-group scale and test whether its effects survive ordinary parameter matching and admissible field redefinitions. For the one-loop four-point function, fixing the renormalised mass, field normalisation, and quartic coupling leaves a finite momentum-dependent remainder. We establish a perturbative non-redundancy description of the retained endpoint against finite renormalisable-parameter redefinitions and local $S$-matrix-preserving field redefinitions at this order. The low-energy theory can be represented as an effective field theory, but its higher-derivative coefficients are not independent. This provides a concrete distinction between a retained finite proper-time scale and an arbitrary cutoff prescription.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces advanced thermodynamic spectroscopy techniques to overcome experimental difficulties in measuring the ring-exchange energy scale in Quantum Spin Ice. By analyzing how observables like thermal expansion and magnetization change with weak external fields, the authors propose clean diagnostic signatures that can distinguish between different magnetic flux states within QSI materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed, quantitative roadmap for interpreting complex thermodynamic measurements in frustrated quantum magnets, linking microscopic Hamiltonian parameters ($g$) directly to macroscopic, measurable responses ($\Delta \alpha$, $dM/dT$).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a reliable thermodynamic probe to detect the elusive ring-exchange energy scale in Quantum Spin Ice (QSI).

**Main result.** Novel probes, such as anisotropic thermal expansion ($\Delta \alpha$) for non-Kramers QSI and $dM/dT$ for dipolar-octupolar pyrochlores, are shown to be highly sensitive to this ring-exchange scale.

**Method.** The authors use a novel thermodynamic approach involving coupling the system to weak perturbations (strain or magnetic fields) and analyzing the resulting temperature derivatives of observables ($\gamma_X$).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Quantum Spin Ice, modeled by the XXZ Hamiltonian on the pyrochlore lattice. The analysis distinguishes between non-Kramers and dipolar-octupolar variants to propose distinct experimental signatures.

**Key observables.** Difference in thermal expansion coefficients ($\Delta \alpha$), temperature derivative of magnetization ($dM/dT$), and the generalized response function $\gamma_X$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ring-exchange energy scale ($g$), zero-flux vs. $\pi$-flux state, weak perturbation strength ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on perturbative expansions in the coupling constant ($\lambda$) and assumes that specific probes (like $\Delta \alpha$) are immune to direct nuclear Schottky anomalies.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate anisotropic thermal expansion spectroscopy showing how the measured signal tracks the lower energy anomaly of the specific heat, and diagrams show the microscopic dependence on flux sector for $dM/dT$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using perturbation theory to derive $\gamma_X$ from weak coupling. It then applies this formalism to two distinct experimental platforms (NK and DO pyrochlores) to predict measurable signatures distinguishing different QSI ground states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum spin ice (QSI) is a three-dimensional quantum spin liquid where fractionalized spinons interact with emergent photons. The XXZ model on the pyrochlore lattice realizes such a $U(1)$ quantum spin liquid and a number of pyrochlore magnets have been investigated as candidate materials, but the detection of emergent excitations has been a major challenge. The specific heat is expected to show the higher-energy spinon excitations and a lower-energy anomaly at the ring-exchange scale, which sets both the photon bandwidth and the energy of the emergent magnetic monopoles (or visons). In reality, the lower-energy peak is obscured by the nuclear Schottky anomaly in non-Kramers Pr-based systems, while it is not clearly resolved from the spinon contributions in Ce-based dipolar-octupolar systems. In this work, we propose a novel thermodynamic probe of the elusive ring-exchange energy scale. We show that in the presence of a weak perturbation coupled to the transverse component of the pseudospin degrees of freedom, the temperature derivative of an observable conjugate to such a weak perturbing force is highly sensitive to the ring-exchange energy scale. Using this scheme, it is shown that the difference between thermal expansion coefficients along the [100] and [010] directions should show a peak at the ring-exchange energy scale for non-Kramers QSI. Similarly, the temperature derivative of the magnetization, $dM/dT$, of dipolar-octupolar pyrochlores under a weak magnetic field can also detect the same signal. Moreover, the sign of these signatures distinguishes the zero-flux and $π$-flux QSI states.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how measuring subsets of qubits in shallow random quantum circuits induces long-range entanglement, leading to conditional dependencies. The authors conjecture that these post-measurement states follow a 'Scrooge ensemble,' which mathematically implies that the complex quantum dynamics are efficiently simulable classically, even when noise is present.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete mechanism by which local quantum dynamics can generate non-local correlations that are nevertheless efficiently simulated classically due to underlying structural constraints revealed by measurement theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the structure of conditional dependence induced by measurements on qubits within shallow random quantum circuits (SRQCs) and how this relates to classical simulation complexity.

**Main result.** For sufficiently deep SRQCs, the post-measurement state ensemble is conjectured to be approximated by a 'Scrooge ensemble,' leading to the conclusion that these circuits are classically efficiently simulable even with small depolarizing noise.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical conjectures (the Scrooge hypothesis) and rigorous mathematical tools like analyzing moment matching via Diagonal Frame Potentials (DFP) to bound the relative error between the actual post-measurement ensemble and the predicted Haar/Scrooge measure.

**Model / system.** The model is an n-qubit two-dimensional lattice subjected to shallow random quantum circuits (SRQCs) with nearest-neighbor gates, partitioned into three regions A, B, and C for measurement analysis.

**Key observables.** Conditional Mutual Information $I(A; C|B)$, the relative error between moment expectations of the actual ensemble vs. the Scrooge ensemble ($\epsilon$), and the Diagonal Frame Potential (DFP).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($d > d^* = \Theta(1)$), system size ($n$), and the width parameter $	ext{width}(B) = 	ext{dist}(A, C) - 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core conjecture is that the post-measurement ensemble approximates the Scrooge ensemble; further analysis relies on assuming stabilizer state structures for bounding errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate numerical evidence showing non-zero conditional mutual information at sufficient depth, and plots demonstrate that the average relative error decays inverse exponentially with the separation width between measured regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical phenomenon of measurement-induced long-range entanglement to formulating the Scrooge ensemble conjecture. It then develops rigorous mathematical machinery using moment matching (DFP) to prove the convergence of observables towards this idealized, classically simulable limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The output state of a 2D geometrically local shallow random quantum circuit does not have long range correlations due to its lightcone structure. But this changes if one measures a subset of the qubits: long-range entanglement can be induced by the measurement process, leading to conditional correlations between distant qubits. In this paper we investigate the structure of conditional dependence in these circuits and its consequences for quantum advantage. For a tripartition $ABC$ of the qubits, we consider the ensemble of post-measurement states on $A$ that is conditioned on a specific measurement outcome on $B$ and ranges over all possible measurement outcomes on $C$. For circuit depths exceeding a constant critical value $d^*$, we conjecture that this ensemble is well approximated by a certain generalization of the Haar ensemble, called the Scrooge ensemble~[Jozsa \textit{et al.}, \href{https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevA.49.668}{Phys. Rev. A \textbf{49}, 668 (1994)}]; we also provide supporting numerical and analytical evidence. Our conjecture describes a precise sense in which the state retains its lightcone structure on the remaining unmeasured qubits, but also develops some globally random features arising from the measurement. A consequence is that $n$-qubit shallow random quantum circuits in two dimensions are classically efficiently simulable in the presence of a tiny depolarizing noise rate $Ω(\log(n)/n)$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to model electric fields in complex, multi-species plasma atmospheres under gravity. By extending classical plasma theories to include alpha particles and stochastic heating, the authors show that the resulting ambipolar field has distinct gravitational and thermoelectric components. This provides a comprehensive tool for understanding highly stratified astrophysical plasmas.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on astrophysical plasma physics, the rigorous application of kinetic theory (Liouville's theorem) to derive macroscopic fields from microscopic equilibrium distributions is a powerful technique applicable to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics problems in other domains.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the stationary states of collisionless, gravitationally stratified plasma atmospheres composed of electrons, protons, and alpha particles.

**Main result.** The ambipolar electric field is shown to decompose into a dominant gravitational component and a thermoelectric contribution arising from species-dependent temperature gradients in multi-temperature plasmas.

**Method.** The authors extend Pannekoek--Rosseland theory by deriving the self-consistent ambipolar electric field from kinetic equilibrium, charge neutrality, and Liouville's theorem.

**Model / system.** The model is a three-component plasma (e-, p+, alpha) in a gravitational field. The analysis progresses from single-temperature isothermal assumptions to multi-temperature regimes generated by stochastic boundary heating.

**Key observables.** Ambipolar electric field (d\phi/dz), density profiles, and temperature gradients for each species.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Alpha-particle abundance ($f$), gravitational acceleration ($g$), plasma temperatures ($T_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is assumed collisionless; the analysis uses perturbative expansions in $\alpha$-particle abundance and extends to multi-temperature states generated by stochastic heating.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 compares numerical solutions for the electrostatic potential with analytical approximations derived from the first-order perturbation theory, showing good agreement.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from a single-temperature analysis using Liouville's theorem to determine the ambipolar field. It then generalizes this formalism to multi-temperature plasmas, deriving the decomposition of the electric field into gravitational and thermoelectric parts.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate stationary states of a collisionless, gravitationally stratified plasma atmosphere composed of electrons, protons, and alpha particles by extending Pannekoek--Rosseland theory to multispecies and multi-temperature plasmas. Starting from Liouville's theorem, we derive the self-consistent ambipolar electric field from kinetic equilibrium and charge neutrality. For a single-temperature atmosphere, we obtain analytical expressions for the ambipolar field, show its dependence on alpha-particle abundance, and determine the relative stratification of the three species. A first-order analytical approximation to the electrostatic potential accurately reproduces the numerical solution. We then generalize the formalism to multi-temperature plasmas generated by stochastic boundary heating, representing the stationary distribution as a superposition of Maxwellian populations. Gravitational filtering produces non-exponential density profiles and increasing temperatures with altitude, while preserving the relative species stratification, with alpha particles most strongly stratified and protons least. The ambipolar electric field contains a dominant gravitational contribution, corresponding to the generalized Pannekoek--Rosseland field, and a thermoelectric contribution arising from species-dependent temperature gradients, which accounts for its non-monotonic structure. These results provide a framework for studying the combined effects of plasma composition and stochastic heating in gravitationally stratified astrophysical plasmas.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a theoretical framework detailing two distinct methods—using a staggered potential or an external magnetic field—to induce Quantum Anomalous Hall (QAHE) effects in altermagnetic materials. The authors predict quantized Hall conductivities of $e^2/h$ and $2e^2/h$, respectively, by analyzing topological transitions marked by specific gap closings and robust chiral edge states.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter topology, the concept of realizing robust quantum states via controlled symmetry breaking and external fields is highly relevant to engineering novel superconducting or topological qubits in solid-state platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper theoretically proposes two distinct, experimentally accessible routes for realizing Quantum Anomalous Hall (QAHE) states in altermagnetic materials.

**Main result.** These routes involve either applying a staggered potential ($\Delta$) to achieve $C=1$ or applying an external magnetic field ($H_Z$) to realize a metastable state with $C=2$.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical modeling based on a Hubbard Hamiltonian, employing Hartree-Fock approximations and calculating topological invariants like the Chern number via band structure analysis.

**Model / system.** The system is an altermagnetic material modeled by a minimal square-lattice Hubbard model incorporating antisymmetric and Rashba spin-orbit couplings. The physics is analyzed under external perturbations: a staggered potential ($\Delta$) or a perpendicular magnetic field ($H_Z$).

**Key observables.** Quantized Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$), Chern number ($C$), energy gap closing/reopening at the Brillouin zone boundary, and spin-polarized chiral edge states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the coupling strengths (e.g., $\lambda_A, \lambda_R$) and the relative strengths of the perturbations ($\Delta/t$ or $h/t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a minimal Hubbard model framework and assumes that topological transitions can be diagnosed by gap closings at specific momenta.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures showing gap closing points corresponding to the transition between trivial and topological phases, and ribbon geometry calculations visualize the distinct spin-polarized chiral edge states for both $C=1$ and $C=2$ regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the model Hamiltonian, first analyzing the ground state evolution with SOC, then detailing the two separate mechanisms (staggered potential vs. magnetic field) that drive the system into quantized topological phases, concluding with detailed edge state characterization for both cases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically propose two possible routes to realizing quantum anomalous Hall states in altermagnetic materials. We consider a minimal square-lattice Hubbard model with antisymmetric spin-orbit coupling associated with an orthorhombic crystal structure, which supports a topologically trivial altermagnetic state. By incorporating Rashba-type spin-orbit coupling and external perturbations, we demonstrate that this trivial state can be turned into topological altermagnetic phases in two distinct ways. The first route is driven by a staggered potential that breaks the symmetry connecting crystallographically equivalent sublattices, leading to a topological altermagnetic ground state characterized by a quantized Hall conductivity $\left| σ_{xy} \right|=e^2/h$ and a Chern number $C=1$. The second route is realized by applying a magnetic field perpendicular to the two-dimensional plane. The resulting topological state appears as a metastable state in the magnetic hysteresis loop, exhibiting a quantized Hall conductivity $\left| σ_{xy} \right|=2e^2/h$ associated with a Chern number $C=2$. We show that these topological transitions are accompanied by characteristic gap closings at the Brillouin-zone boundary, with the number of gap-closing points determining the Chern number. Ribbon-geometry calculations reveal chiral edge states consistent with the bulk topological invariants and demonstrate distinct spin polarizations between the $C=1$ and $C=2$ states. Our results establish experimentally accessible routes to quantized anomalous Hall responses in altermagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This work systematically explores how changing the number of layers in rhombohedral graphene modifies its electronic behavior. By combining transport measurements with theoretical modeling, the authors reveal that layer count controls critical phase transitions and enhances interlayer screening. This establishes layer number as a powerful tool for engineering correlated and topological quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong dependence on layer number and the explicit study of correlation effects (LAF state) and screening mechanisms provide a rich platform for understanding emergent quantum phases in low-dimensional correlated electron systems, relevant to topological materials research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the number of layers controls symmetry breaking, surface screening, and electronic phases in rhombohedral graphene multilayers.

**Main result.** Layer number acts as a key tuning knob, leading to distinct layer-dependent phase transitions (LAF $\leftrightarrow$ SM $\leftrightarrow$ LPI) and enhanced interlayer screening effects in thicker stacks.

**Method.** The research combines electrical transport measurements ($ho_{xx}$ vs. gate voltages/displacement fields) with self-consistent electrostatic tight-binding calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is rhombohedral graphene multilayers (r-NLG), studied across several layer numbers ($N=4$ to $8$). The physics involves correlated electronic states, spontaneous symmetry breaking (LAF state), and screening effects governed by the external displacement field.

**Key observables.** Critical displacement fields ($D_c$) for phase transitions, longitudinal resistivity ($ho_{xx}$), Landau level (LL) crossing patterns, and the electronic screening factor ($S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer number ($N$), applied displacement field ($D$), magnetic field ($B$), and carrier density ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The insulating states observed are attributed to electron-electron interactions (LAF state) rather than single-particle band gaps, which is confirmed by calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams ($ho_{xx}$ vs $n$ and $D$), layer-resolved potential profiles showing screening increase with $N$, and LL structures used to determine the number of layers.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps out phase transitions (LAF $	o$ SM $	o$ LPI) as a function of $N$ and $D$. It then delves into the mechanism, highlighting how increased screening in thicker films modifies surface-state transport and LL behavior under magnetic fields.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rhombohedral multilayer graphene hosts layer-polarized flat bands, providing an intriguing platform for correlated and topological electronic states; however, the role of layer number in governing symmetry breaking and surface screening remains elusive. Here we prepare rhombohedral graphene multilayers and systematically conduct electrical transport measurements. We uncover an unconventional layer dependence of phase transitions: the critical displacement field (D$_{c}$) for the layer-antiferromagnetic (LAF)-to-semimetal transitions remains constant across tetralayer to hexalayer graphene, whereas the D$_{c}$ for semimetal-to-layer-polarized-insulator (LPI) transition increases with layer number, defying unscreened Coulomb interaction models. In hexalayer graphene, surface-state-dominated transport emerges, with Landau levels (LLs) and resistive peaks selectively controlled by adjacent gates, a signature of strong interlayer screening absent in thinner stacks. High magnetic fields reveal valley-layer-locked LLs and dissipative states possibly from interlayer backscattering, highlighting the presence of decoupled surface states. Our findings establish layer number as a key tuning knob for engineering correlated and topological phases in rhombohedral graphene multilayers.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper unifies two advanced mathematical descriptions—tensor categories and operator algebras—to study anyon condensation in topological matter. It introduces an entropic order parameter, providing a rigorous bound on this measure that characterizes the transition into a condensed phase. The work is significant for its deep mathematical connection between quantum information theory and exotic phases of matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the use of entanglement measures like relative entropy to characterize phase transitions is directly relevant to understanding quantum criticality and topological phases in strongly correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to connect two formalisms—tensor-categorical and operator algebraic—used to describe anyon condensation in topological quantum field theories.

**Main result.** It establishes a manifest connection between these two frameworks and provides a simple proof for an upper bound on the entropic order parameter characterizing the condensation transition.

**Method.** The authors deploy Doplicher-Haag-Roberts bimodules over quasi-local C*-algebras to investigate anyon condensation, relating this structure to tensor categories via module functors.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on anyon condensation, which describes phase transitions between $(2+1)$-dimensional topological orders or TQFTs. The analysis uses models like quantum doubles ($S_3$) and product theories (e.g., double Fibonacci/Ising).

**Key observables.** Entropic order parameter $S(\omega|\omega \circ E)$, which is a quantum information-theoretic measure of condensation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The quantum dimension ($d_A$) of the condensable algebra $A$, and the topological state $\omega$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a topological (gapped) boundary, restricting the scope to cases where the underlying MTC has trivial Witt class.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the free A-module functor connecting bulk theory ($\mathcal{C}$) to an interface ($\mathcal{C}_A$). Figure 2 relates simple objects in $	ext{Irr}(\mathcal{C})$ to localizable bimodules over the boundary algebra $M$.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining the tensor-categorical view (using module categories) and then mapping this structure onto the operator algebraic framework using DHR bimodules, culminating in the derivation of bounds on the entropic order parameter.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Anyon condensation that describes the transition between topological quantum field theories can be formulated in the language of tensor categories or that of operator algebras. We deploy the formalism of Doplicher-Haag-Roberts bimodules over quasi-local $\mathrm{C}^{*}$-algebras recently developed in [1] to investigate anyon condensation, which is associated with an extension of a certain operator algebra. The connection between notions in the two formulations is thereby made manifest in an intuitive, diagrammatic manner. An entropic order parameter, as the quantum information-theoretic measure characterising a condensation, is naturally defined, and we give a very simple proof of a bound on it.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a general theoretical framework using Symmetry Topological Field Theory (SymTFT) to describe how condensable anyons cause a TQFT to undergo a phase transition. By classifying the underlying algebraic structure into three types, the authors provide systematic methods to calculate the resulting condensed topological order, applicable to complex non-Abelian systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful mathematical machinery (SymTFT) to analyze phase transitions in exotic quantum matter states like fractional quantum Hall systems, linking topological order parameters directly to symmetry breaking mechanisms analogous to Higgsing transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to systematically construct and analyze phase transitions that occur when condensable anyons proliferate within a general 2+1 dimensional Topological Quantum Field Theory (TQFT). This transition maps the initial TQFT $T$ to a reduced TQFT, $T/A$.

**Main result.** The proliferation transition is realized by coupling scalar fields on the symmetry boundary of a Symmetry Topological Field Theory (SymTFT) and tuning a mass term. The structure of this transition depends systematically on the classification type (i, ii, or iii) of the condensable algebra.

**Method.** The core methodology involves using the SymTFT framework to embed the 2+1d TQFT into a 3+1d gauge theory. The condensation process is modeled by tuning mass terms for boundary scalars, leading to different mathematical structures depending on the underlying symmetry algebra.

**Model / system.** The system under study is defined by Topological Quantum Field Theories (TQFTs) characterized by Modular Tensor Categories (MTCs), which describe fractional quantum Hall states and non-Abelian anyons. Specific examples include $D(S_3)$ and $	ext{SU}(2)_k$ Chern-Simons theories.

**Key observables.** The order parameter is related to the vacuum expectation values of boundary scalars, and key observables include the residual algebra ($b_A$) and the structure of the Higgsed phase.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The transition is controlled by tuning a mass term $m^2$ for boundary scalars: $m^2 > 0$ (original TQFT), $m^2 < 0$ (condensed TQFT), and $m^2 = 0$ (transition point).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes the condensable algebra $A$ can be associated with a symmetry group $G$ via its Mügenger center, allowing for the SymTFT realization. The analysis is initially focused on transparent bosons.

**Figures summary.** Table I summarizes the three types of algebras (i, ii, iii) and their corresponding proliferation transition structures within the SymTFT components ($M_A$, $N_A$, etc.).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework using SymTFT to define the condensation process. It then classifies condensable algebras into three types (i, ii, iii) based on their relationship with the Mügenger center. The structure of the transition is derived for each type, illustrated by examples ranging from abelian theories to non-abelian Chern-Simons models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct phase transitions that proliferate condensable anyons in general 2+1d topological orders, including non-abelian ones. The central tool that provides a systematic approach to this question is the Symmetry Topological Field Theory (SymTFT). For a given topological order, we identify the relevant symmetry from the transparent lines generated by the condensable anyons, and thereby realize the topological order in terms of a 3+1d SymTFT sandwich. The proliferation phase transition is realized by coupling scalar fields to the anyons purely on the symmetry boundary of the SymTFT. We illustrate the construction for abelian theories, as well as non-abelian ones, $D(S_3)$ and $SU(2)_k$ Chern-Simons theories, and extend it to anomalous anyons.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates whether multiple individually optimal coherence transfers in a quantum system can be achieved simultaneously by one single thermodynamic process. Using advanced techniques including Stinespring representations and semidefinite programming, the authors prove that this joint optimization is generally impossible due to inherent compatibility constraints imposed by thermal operations. The results highlight a fundamental limitation on what physical processes can achieve when optimizing multiple distinct quantum coherence measures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental mathematical constraints on the operational capabilities of quantum systems under thermalization, showing that local optimization goals can conflict when constrained by global physical processes like energy conservation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining if multiple coherence transfers, each individually achievable optimally by a quantum process, can be simultaneously optimized by the same single thermodynamic process.

**Main result.** The authors prove that joint optimization of these coherence transfers is generally impossible for thermal operations, even when individual optimizations are possible. This incompatibility is rigorously demonstrated using semidefinite programming and algebraic constraints.

**Method.** The analysis employs energy-resolved Stinespring representations, formulating the problem as a compatibility condition constrained by trace and Gibbs preservation laws, which leads to polygon inequalities and SDP formulations.

**Model / system.** The study uses quantum thermodynamics applied to a system described by a mixed-degenerate Hamiltonian $H=	ext{diag}(0,1,1,3)$. The processes considered are thermal operations (TO), which must be covariant and Gibbs preserving.

**Key observables.** Coherence transfer amplitudes ($\Phi(|iangle\langle j|)$) and the feasibility of joint saturation for these transfers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the mixed-degenerate Hamiltonian, population transfer data ($p_f$), and the constraints imposed by thermal operations (covariance, Gibbs preservation).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that any physical process must be a thermal operation derived from an energy-preserving system-bath unitary.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the problem by relating coherence transfers to inner products of bath-weighted vectors, establishing necessary conditions via Cauchy-Schwarz bounds. It then incorporates thermodynamic constraints (trace/Gibbs preservation) leading to inequalities and culminates in a rigorous SDP analysis proving incompatibility for specific cases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coherences between different energy levels are strongly constrained by thermodynamics. Here we ask a related question: if several coherence transfers can be optimized separately, can they also be optimized simultaneously by the same thermodynamic process? We show that this is in general a compatibility problem. Using an energy-resolved Stinespring representation of thermal operations, we express elementary coherence-transfer amplitudes as inner products of bath-weighted vectors associated with total-energy shells. Saturation of the corresponding Cauchy--Schwarz bounds requires these vectors to be collinear. Since all transfers have to arise from the same energy-preserving system--bath unitary, such saturation conditions cannot in general be imposed independently. Trace and Gibbs preservation lead to additional phase-closure conditions and to polygon inequalities that can rule out simultaneous saturation for fixed population-transfer data.   We complement these analytic results with a rigorous computer-assisted semidefinite-program analysis. For the mixed-degenerate Hamiltonian $H=\operatorname{diag}(0,1,1,3)$, machine-certified primal and dual bounds show that no covariant Gibbs-preserving channel can simultaneously attain three individually optimal directional coherence transfers. Since every thermal operation is covariant and Gibbs preserving, the same triple of optimal values cannot be attained by a thermal operation. We further relate this incompatibility to range-inclusion constraints in Choi-sector Gram matrices for mixed-degenerate energy levels. Our results show that coherence transfers which are optimal when considered separately need not be compatible with one physical thermodynamic process. A full characterization of this incompatibility directly within thermal operations remains open.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses advanced numerical simulations to investigate how SiO2 substrates modify spin transport in graphene. It finds that the interface dictates a complex spin texture, leading to measurable spin lifetime anisotropies depending on whether the substrate is modeled as 2D or bulk. The results offer critical guidance for interpreting experimental measurements and engineering better spintronic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed insights into spin relaxation in graphene interfaces, which is crucial for designing functional spintronic devices by understanding substrate-induced spin-orbit coupling effects beyond simple models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand how common dielectric substrates, specifically SiO2, influence the spin transport properties of graphene for advancing spintronic technologies.

**Main result.** The substrate induces a complex spin texture beyond the standard Rashba model; 2D SiO2 yields $\zeta=1/2$, while bulk SiO2 leads to anisotropic relaxation ($\zeta > 1/2$), and oxygen vacancies show distinct EY-like behavior.

**Method.** The research employs comprehensive numerical simulations, including first-principles density matrix dynamics (FPDMD) and tight-binding (TB) transport simulations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is graphene placed on SiO2 substrates (both 2D and bulk). The modeling incorporates Hamiltonian terms for nearest-neighbor hopping, Rashba SOC, electron-phonon scattering, impurity scattering, and electrostatic disorder.

**Key observables.** Spin texture, spin relaxation process, and the spin lifetime anisotropy ($\zeta = \tau_{s_z}/\tau_{s_x}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The substrate type (2D vs. bulk SiO2), scattering mechanisms (e-ph, impurity), and disorder strength are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the standard Rashba model is insufficient to describe spin relaxation at this ubiquitous interface; approximations include using RTA for charge scattering in some analyses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structures (2D vs. bulk SiO2), DFT band/spin textures, and scaling plots comparing spin relaxation rates and anisotropy ($\zeta$) under different conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical problem, detailing advanced simulation methods (FPDMD, TB modeling), presenting results for 2D and bulk substrates separately, analyzing specific scattering mechanisms (vacancies vs. disorder), and concluding with a comparative summary of the observed anisotropy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how common dielectric substrates influence the spin transport properties of graphene is essential for advancing graphene-based spintronic technologies. Here we use a comprehensive set of numerical simulations to reveal how a SiO$_2$ substrate modifies the spin texture and governs spin relaxation in graphene. Using first-principles density matrix dynamics simulations, as well as tight-binding (TB) transport simulations, we quantify the effects of electron-phonon scattering, impurity scattering, and electrostatic disorder on the spin relaxation process. We find that a 2D SiO$_2$ substrate induces a predominantly Rashba-type helical spin texture in graphene, leading to a spin lifetime anisotropy of 1/2. Meanwhile, bulk SiO$_2$ breaks in-plane symmetry in graphene, leading to anisotropic in-plane and out-of-plane components in the spin texture, which we capture with a newly-developed TB model of graphene. Transport simulations under realistic disorder conditions reveal a spin lifetime anisotropy between 0.5 and 1, similar to what is seen in measurements of graphene spin valves on a SiO$_2$ substrate. Our results reveal a more complex picture of spin relaxation at the ubiquitous graphene/SiO$_2$ interface, beyond the standard Rashba model, providing critical insight for interpreting experiments and guiding substrate engineering for graphene spintronics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the bottleneck in fast Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computation caused by TCNOT gates creating overly complex error patterns that overwhelm classical decoders. The proposed framework, PACE, mitigates this by implementing advanced decoding strategies and scheduling TCNOTs based on real-time decoder capacity rather than just quantum speedup.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on computation theory, the explicit mention of physical platforms like neutral atoms and the need to manage complex, correlated errors touches upon the practical limitations of quantum hardware interfacing with classical control electronics, which is relevant for AMO physicists working on scalable quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a scheduling strategy for Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computation (FTQC) that maximizes the speedup from Transversal CNOT (TCNOT) gates without overwhelming the classical decoding resources.

**Main result.** The authors introduce PACE, a decoder-aware framework that successfully balances TCNOT concurrency against the limitations of classical decoding capacity by employing novel scheduling and decoding techniques.

**Method.** PACE combines three complementary techniques—Hybrid Window Decoding, DEM Stitch, and Sub-window Parallel Decoding—to mitigate decoder costs before performing an optimized, resource-aware TCNOT schedule.

**Model / system.** The work is situated within the context of surface code quantum error correction. The system relies on syndrome extraction rounds mediated by TCNOT gates, which generate complex, non-graphlike Detector Error Models (DEMs) due to correlated errors across patches.

**Key observables.** The trade-off between quantum acceleration (TCNOT concurrency) and classical decoding cost (latency, memory requirements).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), TCNOT gate density, and the computational capacity of the classical decoder.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that the practical benefit of TCNOTs is limited not only by quantum hardware but critically by the classical decoding and DEM-compilation capacity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the trade-off, showing how dense scheduling leads to infeasible decoding windows compared to PACE's balanced approach. Figure 2 details how TCNOTs create hyperedges that break the standard graphlike structure of the DEM.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the quantum acceleration benefit of TCNOTs but then pivots to detailing the classical bottleneck caused by error correlation and exponential DEM growth. It then introduces PACE, describing its three constituent decoder-side techniques before presenting the final scheduler that optimizes concurrency against resource limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Transversal CNOT (TCNOT) gates can accelerate fault-tolerant quantum computation (FTQC) in the surface code by reducing the number of syndrome extraction rounds required between logical operations from $O(d)$ to $O(1)$. This is particularly attractive for quantum platforms with long-range connectivity, such as neutral atoms. However, dense TCNOT schedules substantially increase the classical decoding workload. TCNOTs propagate errors across multiple surface-code patches, enlarging the spatiotemporal region that must be decoded jointly. Consequently, denser TCNOT schedules increase decoding latency and memory requirements and potentially exceed available decoder capacity. Moreover, because the detector error model (DEM) of each decoding window depends on the TCNOT schedule, exhaustively precomputing all possible window-level DEMs is infeasible, requiring just-in-time (JIT) DEM compilation. Thus, the practical benefit of TCNOT gates is limited not only by quantum hardware performance but also by classical decoding and DEM-compilation capacity.   We introduce PACE, a decoder-aware scheduling framework for TCNOT-based FTQC. PACE first mitigates the decoder-side costs of aggressive TCNOT scheduling through three complementary techniques. Hybrid Window Decoding assigns different decoders for each decoding window according to its DEM structure. DEM Stitch generates schedule-specific window-level DEMs just in time by assembling reusable precompiled fragments. Sub-window Parallel Decoding decomposes large windows into smaller sub-windows with graph-coloring formulation. Building on these techniques, PACE then performs decoder-aware scheduling to maximize TCNOT concurrency within the available decoder resources. Our evaluation shows the trade-off between quantum acceleration and classical decoding cost, revealing the limitations of current decoding systems for TCNOT-based FTQC.

</details>
