**Summary.** The paper analyzes the geometry of the Uhlmann phase generated when qubits interact with specific non-coherence-generating quantum channels. It demonstrates that this interaction creates localized vortex singularities in the parameter space, unlike previous models. This finding highlights a mechanism where environmental coupling generates measurable geometric phases through defect dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed geometric understanding of quantum information flow through noisy channels, offering insights into how environmental coupling (the channel parameters) can generate measurable geometric phases locally, which is crucial for designing robust quantum protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the geometry of the Uhlmann phase induced by quantum channels, contrasting this with previous analyses focused on thermal transitions driven by Hamiltonian variations.

**Main result.** A cyclically controlled non-coherence-generating channel induces a nontrivial Uhlmann phase characterized by local vortex–antivortex structures on the parameter torus. These defects nucleate and annihilate in pairs following a square-root coalescence law, contrasting with global winding number jumps.

**Method.** The analysis uses Bloch-space geometry and calculates the Uhlmann phase via cyclic holonomy amplitudes derived from the channel parameters. Topological defect identification relies on solving for vanishing points of this amplitude.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using qubit systems undergoing evolution governed by a Non-Coherence-Generating (NCG) quantum channel, whose parameters drive the cycle in the space of CPTP maps.

**Key observables.** Uhlmann phase ($\Phi_U$), topological defects (vortices/antivortices), and the net topological charge.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Channel parameters $(\mu, 
u, \chi)$, input-state orientation approaching critical values, and the parameter regime $	heta \in [\pi/3, 2\pi/3]$ for defect existence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to NCG channels (Kraus rank two), and the initial state is assumed pure, carrying no intrinsic geometric phase.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows Bloch-vector trajectories tracing closed loops. Figure 2 maps $\Phi_U$ over the parameter cube, highlighting vortices at specific loci intersections. Figure 3 and 4 visualize these phase jumps and defect structures on the parameter torus.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by analyzing the Uhlmann phase geometry induced by NCG channels. It develops the mathematical framework using Bloch-space connections to locate singularities (vortices) in the channel parameter space, demonstrating their dynamics via coalescence laws while confirming zero net topological charge.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that a cyclically controlled non-coherence-generating channel supplies a two-parameter family of closed Bloch trajectories, absent in the single-parameter (temperature) cycles of the thermal Uhlmann literature, whose associated Uhlmann phase develops a genuine vortex--antivortex structure on the channel-parameter torus. A qubit prepared in a pure state, which carries no geometric phase of its own, acquires a nontrivial Uhlmann phase, obtained here in closed form, purely from a closed loop in the space of the native quantum channel parameters. The net topological charge of these defects is constrained to zero by the Poincaré--Hopf theorem. As the input-state orientation approaches its critical values, the defects merge and annihilate in pairs following a square-root coalescence law. This defect dynamics drives local geometric transitions, a mechanism that contrasts with single-parameter thermal Uhlmann transitions, where a global quantized winding jumps as a bulk invariant.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Hamilton-Zero, a massive foundation model designed to calculate ground states for arbitrary qubit Hamiltonians. It achieves this by reformulating the problem as variational optimization on a continuous mathematical manifold defined over SU(2)^N. This allows the model to generalize robustly across vastly different system sizes and interaction topologies, promising quantum advantage in simulating complex materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a major convergence point between quantum simulation and deep learning, offering a scalable, data-driven approach that bypasses the explicit exponential complexity limitations of traditional quantum state representations for many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is computing the ground state energy of arbitrary quadratic qubit Hamiltonians for quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'Hamilton-Zero,' a foundation model capable of effectively amortizing ground state computation across an entire, universal set of Hamiltonians, achieving high accuracy on very large, complex systems.

**Method.** They formulate the problem as manifold variational optimization over centrally odd scalar functions on SU(2)^N, replacing explicit amplitudes with continuous manifold representations acted upon by Lie derivatives.

**Model / system.** The model targets spin-1/2 quantum systems governed by arbitrary quadratic qubit Hamiltonians. It uses a neural tensor-network foundation model architecture with $\sim 0.5$B parameters.

**Key observables.** Variational energy ($\langle\psi_	heta|\hat{H}|\psi_	hetaangle / \langle\psi_	heta|\psi_	hetaangle$), which serves as an upper bound on the true ground-state energy $E_0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The model is trained/tested up to system sizes of 8100 qubits, with pretraining covering hundreds of thousands of varied Hamiltonians.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The variational principle is preserved by constraining the wavefunction to be a centrally odd scalar function on SU(2)^N, which guarantees it lies in the physical spin-1/2 sector.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the generalization surface of foundation wavefunction models, demonstrating Hamilton-Zero's superior ability to vary system topology compared to previous methods limited to varying only coefficients on a fixed graph.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem and the novel manifold variational formulation. It details the training procedure using large datasets and advanced optimizers (KFAC extension). Results demonstrate generalization across diverse Hamiltonians and scaling to very large systems, comparing performance against established methods like tensor networks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central promise of useful quantum advantage is the ability to compute ground states of Hamiltonian systems beyond the reach of classical simulation methods. Here we demonstrate that this problem can be effectively amortized across an arbitrary and universal set of Hamiltonians by a foundation model with $\sim0.5$B variational parameters, trained with contemporary techniques from large language models and deep reinforcement learning. To do this, we formulate $\text{spin-}1/2$ quantum ground-state learning as manifold variational optimisation over centrally odd scalar functions on $\mathrm{SU}(2)^N$. This replaces explicit Hilbert-space vector amplitudes with manifold functions on which the Hamiltonian acts through Lie derivatives, evaluated by custom automatic differentiation primitives. We prove that the resulting variational principle on this manifold preserves the $\text{spin-}1/2$ sector's ground-state upper bound using the Peter-Weyl theorem, then pre-train our foundation model on a dataset of hundreds of thousands of different Hamiltonian systems, varying the connection topology, system size, interaction types and strengths, bringing together a century of many-body literature. Using a novel $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ replica-exchange Langevin sampler and sharded natural-gradient optimisation, we train our model with our own extension of the Kronecker-Factored Approximate Curvature (KFAC) optimiser on system sizes up to 64 qubits. On a held-out generalisation dataset, we fine-tune our model on system sizes of up to 1024 qubits, and evaluate on systems up to 8100 qubits.

</details>

**Summary.** This work systematically benchmarks Quantum Machine Learning against classical methods using cancer datasets. The authors conclude that current benchmark data do not show a quantum advantage, establishing a rigorous framework for future research. They argue that meaningful progress requires analyzing significantly higher-dimensional and more biologically complex datasets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ML applications rather than fundamental physics, the rigorous framework developed for quantifying 'quantum advantage' via resource estimation is highly relevant to assessing computational feasibility in complex physical simulations or open quantum systems modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper benchmarks quantum vs. classical machine learning models for cancer diagnosis using complex biological data, aiming to determine if a practical quantum advantage exists.

**Main result.** The authors found no evidence of quantum advantage when testing on standard oncological benchmark datasets, suggesting the need for higher-dimensional, more realistic data.

**Method.** They developed a rigorous benchmarking methodology based on the Red Cedar framework, comparing performance across tabular, omics, and spatial datasets using both classical AutoML networks and QML models.

**Model / system.** The system involves Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models utilizing parameterized quantum circuits, benchmarked against optimized classical neural networks. The analysis uses specific encoding schemes like bit-bit encoding for data representation on qubits.

**Key observables.** Performance comparison between quantum and classical models; the resource estimation metric $Q_{dataset}(1.0)$; classification accuracy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($Q_n, Q_c$); number of parameters per layer; dimensionality reduction techniques (PCA, Copula transform).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical test accuracy metric is noted to be unrealistically optimistic as it cannot account for unseen test samples. All quantum results were run on a simulator.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the data processing pipeline: optional feature selection/reduction $ightarrow$ bit-bit encoding (data transformation and information-based bit assignment) $ightarrow$ training.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a benchmarking methodology, applies it to various oncological datasets (tabular, omics, spatial), compares performance metrics against classical baselines, and concludes by discussing the requirements for demonstrating true quantum advantage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Machine learning is being increasingly used for the detection, diagnosis, and treatment of cancer. However, models often struggle with biological data due to high dimensionality, limited sample diversity, and complex feature interactions. Recent works have investigated the potential for quantum machine learning models to exhibit improved performance over classical models on this kind of complex data, but have often lacked rigorous empirical evaluation of quantum advantage. In this work, we develop a methodology for fair benchmarking of quantum and classical machine learning models, based on the Red Cedar quantum machine learning and resource estimation framework and AutoML-optimized classical neural networks. We assess the potential for quantum advantage in machine learning across tabular, omics, and spatial oncological datasets drawn from the existing quantum machine learning literature, with a range of preprocessing methods, and find no evidence of quantum advantage. Our results suggest that the field should prioritize analyzing higher-dimensional, more biologically realistic datasets to make meaningful progress toward practical quantum advantage in oncological classification problems.

</details>

### [Flood of multipartite Rains entanglement](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11406v1)

**Authors:** Hailey S. Murray, Sagnik Bhattacharya, M. Cerezo, Liuke Lyu, Mark M. Wilde  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.11406v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

The genuine multipartite log-negativity, squall entanglement, hurricane entanglement, and Rains entanglement of tripartite reduced density matrices in the 1D transverse-field Ising model are plotted versus the magnetic field h. </figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper introduces several new multipartite entanglement measures—including Rains, Monsoon, Hurricane, and Squall entanglements—to address the limitations of existing bipartite measures. It establishes these quantities as upper bounds on key physical processes like pure-state distillation rates. The methodology relies heavily on advanced convex optimization techniques, particularly Semidefinite Programming.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to quantify complex multi-partite correlations, which is fundamental for understanding quantum communication protocols and resource theories in many-body physics simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is characterizing multipartite entanglement, as existing bipartite measures fail to generalize uniquely to multi-party quantum systems.

**Main result.** New measures (Rains, Monsoon, Hurricane, Squall) are defined, and upper bounds for one-shot and asymptotic pure-state distillation rates are established using these quantities.

**Method.** The authors define entanglement measures via generalizations of the Rains relative entropy and utilize Semidefinite Programming (SDP) to formulate optimization problems and derive dual programs.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes general multipartite quantum states ($ho$) in $k$-partite systems, with specific applications to Quantum Pairwise Independent Networks (qPINs) and the one-dimensional transverse-field Ising model.

**Key observables.** Rains entanglement ($R$), Monsoon, Hurricane, Squall entanglements; Genuine Multipartite Entanglement (GME); One-shot and asymptotic pure-state distillation rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parties ($k$); the parameter $\alpha$ used in Rényi generalizations ($	ext{e}D_\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Strong duality theorems are assumed or proven for SDP formulations; approximations (variational parametrization) are necessary to solve the full Rains entanglement optimization problem.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining multiple multipartite entanglement measures, proving their properties (like monotonicity), deriving upper bounds on distillation rates using these measures, and finally formulating the max-Rains entanglement via SDPs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multipartite entanglement admits phenomena such as the activation of genuine multipartite entanglement (GME) and the existence of inequivalent classes of entanglement, and existing bipartite entanglement measures have no unique generalization to this regime. In this work, we define the Rains, monsoon, hurricane, and squall entanglement as generalizations of the bipartite Rains relative entropy, and we establish various properties of these entanglement measures. We also prove that the Rains entanglement is monotone under selective quantum operations that completely preserve the positivity of the partial transpose. We establish single-letter upper bounds on the one-shot and asymptotic rates at which a fixed pure state can be distilled from an arbitrary state in both the standard and probabilistic approximate distillation scenarios. Among the entanglement measures we define, the tightest upper bound on the one-shot pure-state distillation rate is in terms of the Rains entanglement. However, the activation of GME (or, equivalently, the tensor instability of biseparability) makes it unclear if the one-shot bound in terms of the Rains entanglement can be extended to a single-letter asymptotic bound. Instead, we establish upper bounds on the asymptotic pure-state distillation rate in terms of the hurricane and squall entanglement. Upper bounds on the GHZ- and W-distillable entanglement follow as a consequence. Additionally, we define the multipartite max-Rains entanglement, write it as a semidefinite program, and derive a dual program for it. Finally, we analyze these measures for quantum pairwise independent networks, and we establish a conditional gradient (Frank-Wolfe) algorithm for computing the Rains entanglement.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the numerical challenge of accurately and efficiently calculating matrix functions like $\exp(-it\hat{H})$ arising in quantum simulations. The authors provide a comprehensive comparison of approximation methods—highlighting minimax and Chebyshev techniques—and offer optimized, rigorously analyzed C++ implementations for these calculations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it provides state-of-the-art computational tools and rigorous error analysis for simulating quantum dynamics, which is crucial for developing practical quantum simulation algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing rigorous analysis and high-performance implementations for approximating functions of a Hermitian matrix, such as $\exp(-it\hat{H})$, to simulate quantum time evolution.

**Main result.** The authors provide rigorous error bounds for Chebyshev approximation and implement true minimax approximation techniques, showing superior convergence compared to naive methods.

**Method.** The work compares multiple polynomial approximation schemes (Chebyshev, Minimax via CF algorithm, Taylor series) for matrix functions, focusing on efficient evaluation using the Clenshaw algorithm and optimizing for sparse Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum systems governed by the time-dependent Schrödinger equation, utilizing Hamiltonians ($\hat{H}$) relevant to quantum optimization (e.g., $\hat{H} = \hat{H}_P + \gamma \hat{H}_G$) and quantum walks.

**Key observables.** Approximation error ($\epsilon$), computational runtime, number of matrix-vector products required, and the action $|\psi(t)angle = \exp(-it\hat{H}) |\psiangle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral radius $ho(\hat{H})$ dictates the necessary interval size for approximation; performance is analyzed across varying system sizes ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Minimax approximations require fixing an interval, necessitating an upper bound on the spectral radius. The analysis distinguishes between truncation error, coefficient error, and evaluation error.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare convergence rates (Taylor vs. Minimax/Chebyshev), runtime comparisons of various approximation algorithms (e.g., optimized Clenshaw vs. naive Horner), and performance gains when using GPU acceleration or commutator-free time evolution methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general problem of matrix function approximation to specific implementations, comparing Chebyshev, minimax, and Taylor methods for calculating $\exp(-it\hat{H})|\psiangle$, concluding with discussions on optimizing these calculations for modern hardware (GPU) and time-dependent evolution.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is known that Chebyshev based polynomial approximation gives a near-optimal rate of convergence for calculating a function of a Hermitian matrix. However, previous work has not discussed the option of true minimax approximation, nor the specifics of writing a high performance implementation with a rigorous analysis of errors. This work provides such an analysis and an open-source implementation of three approximation methods in C++.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates how to classically optimize random quantum circuits to achieve a high probability peak on one output state. The authors challenge previous theoretical predictions regarding this optimization landscape by presenting detailed numerical measurements showing specific decay rates and comparing the performance of different optimizers. Ultimately, they establish rigorous bounds on achievable peakedness while noting that the structure of near-optimal solutions remains connected.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the feasibility limits of quantum computation protocols by characterizing the difficulty of finding highly non-random states within complex Hilbert spaces, which has implications for understanding resource scaling in quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is understanding the optimization landscape for generating 'peaked circuits'—random quantum circuits whose measurement output concentrates probability on a single bitstring, which is proposed as a route to verifiable quantum advantage.

**Main result.** The authors show that while the barren plateau exists, it does not explain the observed rate of decay in peakedness; furthermore, they prove an upper bound for the achievable peak probability in the deep limit. They also find evidence suggesting the near-optimal solutions form a connected structure rather than fragmenting.

**Method.** The methodology involves mapping the optimization landscape by maximizing the peakedness observable $\delta(	heta)$ using advanced gradient descent techniques (L-BFGS-B vs. Adam) and comparing results against theoretical bounds like the Haar floor.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a random quantum circuit ($U_r$) followed by a trainable variational 'brickwall' section $V(	heta)$ acting on $n$ qubits. The analysis focuses on maximizing the probability $\delta(	heta) = |\langle 0^n| V(	heta) U_r |0^nangle|^2$.

**Key observables.** Peakedness ($\delta$), Haar floor ($2^{-n}$), Reach ratio ($ho_{	ext{arm}}/R_{	ext{conv}}$), and the decay rate of $\delta^*$ with system size $n$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n=8-16$, extrapolated to $n=50$), circuit depth ($	au_r, 	au_p$), and optimization parameters ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares results against previous fixed-base exponential laws, rejects the initial hardness conjecture based on optimizer performance, and relies on rigorous numerical measurements across multiple instances.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the general setup comparing $\delta(0)$ to $\delta_{	ext{best}}$ relative to the Haar floor; other figures likely show scaling of these observables with $n$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the problem (peaked circuits), presenting numerical measurements that contradict prior theoretical fits, analyzing the role of optimization algorithms and plateaus, and concluding with rigorous proofs regarding the achievable bounds in the deep limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Peaked circuits are random quantum circuits whose measurement returns one bitstring far more often than chance. They are a candidate route to verifiable quantum advantage, and the bottleneck is classical generation. Aaronson and Zhang fix a random circuit, append a trainable brickwall of half that depth, and optimize it by gradient descent to raise the probability of one chosen output string. Their method plateaus at a size-dependent ceiling, which they attribute to a barren plateau. A dichotomy remains open: either the optimizer stops short, so a better algorithm would reach higher, or no efficient method exists. We map the landscape on which the answer depends. Across 18 instances per size at n = 8-16, at fixed and converged budgets, no fixed-base exponential, the law they fit, matches the optimizer's reach: at convergence the decay steepens from 1.16 to 1.295 per qubit through n = 16 (p = 0.011 frozen and 0.025 converged, on n = 8-14 alone), leaving their n = 50 estimate unsupported; at n = 16 they report more than we reach at any budget measured. One optimizer beats ours: L-BFGS-B ends 3.9 +/- 1.6% above converged Adam at n = 16, on three instances. That margin retires our hardness conjecture under its registered rule and leaves the rate untouched: every optimizer measured loses a factor 1.3 per qubit. The barren plateau is present and cannot explain that rate: the reach sits far above the Haar floor 2^-n, and the exact second-order amplitude data are depth-independent while the reach is not. Nor do solutions cluster at that floor: they are decorrelated yet path-connected by paths 10^2-10^3 above 2^-n whose floor falls from 0.73 to 0.23 of the endpoints. Path search is one-sided, so near-optimal clustering stays open. In the deep limit we prove no poly(n)-parameter family beats poly(n) 2^-n on average. What survives: a connected landscape and a shrinking reach.

</details>

**Authors:** Nivedita Dey, Mrityunjay Ghosh, Pranav Kaushal, Abhinab Khare, Amlan Chakrabarti

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an adaptive framework that merges multiple classical cryptanalytic techniques (linear, differential, side-channel) to aggressively filter a large key hypothesis space. This reduction allows subsequent quantum verification using amplitude amplification to operate on a much smaller effective space, drastically cutting down the required computational resources for breaking cryptographic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical blueprint for optimizing quantum algorithms applied to classical security problems, showing how pre-processing steps can drastically reduce resource requirements (qubits, time) for quantum computation in cryptography.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is efficiently integrating classical cryptanalytic evidence (from linear, differential, and side-channel analysis) with quantum verification in hybrid classical-quantum cryptanalysis.

**Main result.** The proposed framework demonstrates that adaptive classical filtering can substantially reduce the initial key hypothesis space (e.g., 4096 to 13 candidates), leading to a significant reduction (approx. 25-fold) in required quantum verification effort via reduced-space amplitude amplification.

**Method.** A capability-adaptive framework is developed that first uses statistical filtering based on multiple cryptanalytic techniques to contract the candidate key space, followed by applying reduced-space quantum verification using amplitude amplification.

**Model / system.** The model applies to symmetric-key cryptographic primitives ($\Pi$). The theoretical structure involves defining a formal mathematical model for candidate-space construction and adaptive filtering, culminating in a Hamiltonian formulation for physically realizing the reduced-space quantum verification process.

**Key observables.** Candidate-key hypothesis space size (e.g., 4096 initial $	o$ 13 effective), Grover iteration requirement reduction (50 to 2 iterations), and target-state success probability ($\approx 94.53\%$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity is characterized by the relationship between candidate-space contraction and quantum verification effort, quantified using metrics like linear approximation bias ($\hat{\epsilon}_i$) and differential characteristic probabilities ($P_i$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that classical filtering can accurately contract the search space while preserving cryptanalytic admissibility, allowing the use of reduced-space quantum algorithms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall process flow: Quantum-assisted observation sampling $ightarrow$ Effective candidate space generation ($K_{	ext{eff}}$) $ightarrow$ Reduced-space quantum verification. Tables summarize contraction statistics and complexity reduction.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first defining the need for unification, developing a formal mathematical model encompassing candidate-space construction (using linear/differential/leakage analysis), and finally demonstrating the quantitative benefits of reduced-space quantum verification using amplitude amplification on the filtered set.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient integration of cryptanalytic evidence with quantum verification remains a fundamental challenge in hybrid classical-quantum cryptanalysis. This work presents a capability-adaptive cryptanalytic framework that unifies linear cryptanalysis, differential cryptanalysis, and side-channel leakage analysis within a common candidate-space reduction architecture, followed by reduced-space quantum verification through amplitude amplification. A formal mathematical model is developed for candidate-space construction, adaptive filtering, verification-space reduction, and complexity characterization, supported by the oretical results establishing the relationship between candidate-space contraction and quantum verification effort. A Hamiltonian formulation is further introduced to provide a physically realizable interpretation of the reduced-space verification process. Evaluation using statistically generated cryptanalytic observations demonstrates that the proposed framework reduces an initial candidate-key hypothesis space of 4096 candidates to an effective candidate space of 13 hypotheses, corresponding to an overall reduction of approximately 99.683%. Consequently, the Grover verification requirement decreases from 50 iterations to only 2 iterations, yielding an approximately 25-fold reduction in verification effort, while reduced-space amplitude amplification achieves a target-state success probability of approximately 94.53%. These results demonstrate that adaptive cryptanalytic filtering can substantially reduce quantum verification complexity while preserving cryptanalytic admissibility, providing a practical foundation for capability-aware hybrid cryptanalysis and reduced-space quantum search.

</details>

**Summary.** This work simulates the melting of stripe phases in a 2D system, finding that density dictates the final fluid topology. The authors demonstrate that simple thermodynamic measurements fail to capture this complexity; only advanced topological observables can distinguish between a fragmented cluster state and a continuous network state.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical soft matter physics, the reliance on topological invariants (like Betti numbers) to characterize phase transitions and connectivity changes mirrors concepts used in analyzing complex quantum many-body ground states or entanglement structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the melting of stripe-forming systems changes connectivity, specifically determining if the resulting fluid topology is uniquely determined by the initial ordered state or if it is instead selected by the system's density.

**Main result.** The topological outcome of the melt—whether the filaments fragment into finite clusters or remain in a dynamically fluid, system-spanning network—is strongly dependent on the particle density ($ho$).

**Method.** Molecular dynamics simulations are performed along isochoric heating paths using various structural, orientational, and advanced graph-based topological observables.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-dimensional system of particles interacting via a Lennard-Jones plus Gaussian (LJG) pair potential, which stabilizes one-particle-thick stripe phases at low temperatures.

**Key observables.** Topological descriptors like the first Betti number ($eta_1$), graph 2-core fraction ($f_{2-	ext{core}}$), and connectivity measures are crucial alongside standard thermodynamic quantities like isochoric heat capacity ($c_V$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis spans seven densities ($ho = 0.395$ to $0.455$) across the stripe-forming regime, as well as varying temperatures during heating.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Conventional scalar order parameters are insufficient because they cannot distinguish between a system-spanning network and finite clusters when local packing is similar; thus, topological diagnostics are necessary.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize the density dependence of structural metrics (like nematic order $S_2$) during melting, showing that while global order decays uniformly, the topological structure diverges based on $ho$.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the multistage reconstruction upon heating, contrasting the behavior observed using only thermodynamic/orientational data versus the richer information provided by graph-based topology to distinguish between different fluid states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The melting of stripe-forming systems involves changes in connectivity that are not fully captured by conventional structural and orientational descriptors. We investigate a two-dimensional model with competing interactions whose low-temperature phase consists of one-particle-thick stripes. Molecular dynamics simulations along seven heating isochores are analyzed using thermodynamic, orientational, dynamical, and graph-based observables. Heating produces a multistage reconstruction in which the loss of stripe alignment and the reorganization of filament connectivity occur over distinct temperature ranges. Density controls whether the disordered filaments fragment into finite polymer-like clusters or remain joined in a dynamically fluid, system-spanning network. The topological observables distinguish these outcomes, which are not resolved by the thermodynamic and orientational responses alone. Thus, the same ordered stripe microphase can melt into topologically distinct fluids selected by density.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a quantitative link between quantum entanglement and spacetime geometry by deriving a 'metric-from-information' lower bound. It shows that the amount of boundary mutual information dictates how far apart points can be in the bulk spacetime, offering a powerful diagnostic tool beyond classical area laws.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on gravity/QFT, the core methodology—using entanglement measures like mutual information to constrain geometric properties—is highly relevant for understanding quantum correlations in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a quantitative, metric counterpart showing how boundary mutual information constrains the bulk geodesic separation distance within holographic spacetime settings.

**Main result.** The paper derives a rigorous lower bound (Metric-from-information bound) stating that vanishing mutual information forces the allowed bulk geodesic separation to diverge logarithmically.

**Method.** It combines quantum information measures, specifically Mutual Information $I(A:B)$, with holographic dictionary relations linking two-point functions to renormalized geodesic lengths $	ext{L}_{	ext{ren}}(x, y)$.

**Model / system.** The analysis is set in the context of AdS/CFT correspondence and involves calculating bounds for a boundary manifold $\Sigma$ with disjoint regions $A$ and $B$. Specific applications include parallel strips in AdS$_5$/CFT$_4$.

**Key observables.** Boundary Mutual Information ($I(A:B)$) and renormalized Bulk Geodesic Distance ($	ext{L}_{	ext{ren}}(x, y)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bound depends on calibration constants $\kappa_\Delta$ and $\epsilon_\star$, and the analysis distinguishes between semiclassical regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on operating within a semiclassical holographic code subspace and assumes controlled truncation errors ($\epsilon_{	ext{code}} \ll 1$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates how short geodesics are permitted when $I(A:B)$ is large, while long geodesics are forced when $I(A:B)$ is small.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing a pairwise metric-from-information bound (Theorem 1) to extending this local inequality to global connectivity via a multiscale iteration (Theorem 2).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ryu-Takayanagi established how boundary entanglement encodes bulk area. We provide the metric counterpart: boundary mutual information imposes a rigorous lower bound on bulk geodesic separation that diverges logarithmically as correlations vanish. A multiscale iteration promotes this local inequality to a global obstruction to bulk connectivity. For parallel strips in AdS$_5$/CFT$_4$, the bound necessitates a quantum resolution of the classical mutual-information transition and fixes the asymptotic growth of geodesic distance.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the spectral properties of quantum Hamiltonians used to encode NP-hard problems like Set Cover. It rigorously distinguishes between the full symmetry-allowed spectrum and the subspace actually reachable by the system's dynamics. The work provides structural guarantees regarding adiabatic evolution times, establishing a fundamental separation between potential symmetries and actual physical accessibility.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous separation of accessible spectral structure is highly relevant to understanding the limitations and guarantees of quantum computation protocols based on Hamiltonian simulation or annealing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses which parts of a Hamiltonian spectrum are physically relevant when both the initial state preparation and subsequent time evolution preserve multiple symmetries, specifically for encoding Minimum Set Cover.

**Main result.** The authors establish a rigorous structural separation between the global symmetry-allowed spectral structure and the dynamically accessible cyclic subspace. They derive bounds on the adiabatic runtime conditional on specific state preparations.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced mathematical tools including sector-resolved localization, analyzing algebraic structures generated by Hamiltonians, and deriving gap estimates using techniques related to Dirichlet forms and coupling arguments.

**Model / system.** The model is a compact multi-register quantum encoding of the Minimum Set Cover problem. The dynamics are governed by complex Hamiltonians involving walk terms, cover constraints, and exclusion terms acting on multiple registers.

**Key observables.** Joint fixed excitation gap, sector-resolved leakage bounds ($\Gamma_\gamma$), and the dynamically accessible cyclic space ($\mathcal{K}_u$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the problem instance ($n$), the number of registers, and parameters governing the Hamiltonian components ($\lambda, \mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** No quantum speedup claim is made; the results are structural separations. The analysis relies on specific conditions for symmetry sectors and state preparation (e.g., Dicke-state preparation).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the physical system and Hamiltonian, establishing the relationship between different spectral subspaces ($H_{	ext{sym}}$ vs $\mathcal{K}_u$), proving localization bounds (Proposition 1), constructing explicit parent paths for specific families, and finally deriving polynomial adiabatic runtime estimates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Which part of a Hamiltonian spectrum is physically relevant when both the initial state and interpolation preserve several symmetries? We address this question for a compact multi-register encoding of Minimum Set Cover. The represented symmetry combines register permutations with the faithful base action of the incidence automorphism group. We distinguish its fixed, symmetry-allowed space from the generally smaller cyclic space generated by the protocol and define gaps relative to isolated bands in that space. A sector-resolved Schur-complement bound certifies cover-measurement probability whenever the sector contains feasible covers and has positive invalid-state separation; the sector kinetic floor is essential. Exact orbit quotients expose inter-sector coincidences invisible to a symmetric protocol, while a stability theorem shows that transverse dark crossings persist under small symmetry-preserving perturbations. On an even-cycle family, the original linear interpolation has an exact global multiplicity closure while the joint-fixed excitation gap remains constant. For the same family we construct a Johnson/Metropolis parent path from a Dicke state to a Gibbs-amplitude state with cover probability $1-O(n^{-5})$. A zero-range comparison and a log-concave three-box coupling give the uniform cyclic-gap certificate $Ω(n^{-13})$. Quantitative derivative bounds then yield a polynomial adiabatic runtime in the abstract Hamiltonian-access model, conditional on Dicke-state preparation and access to the parent Hamiltonian. We make no quantum-speedup claim: the result is a rigorous separation of global, symmetry-allowed, and dynamically accessible spectral structure. Changing the initial state or breaking a preserved symmetry changes that accessible spectrum.

</details>

**Authors:** Van Sy Mai, Richard J. La, Abdella Battou, Abderrahim Amlou, Cory Nunn

**Summary.** This paper develops queueing models to analyze the capacity of memory-based quantum repeaters, balancing the trade-off between high entanglement throughput and maintaining a minimum required fidelity. By incorporating detailed noise dynamics from quantum memories and Bell State Measurements, the authors show that optimizing the maximum waiting times in these memories is key to maximizing usable network performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework connecting resource management (memory time limits) directly to performance metrics (rate vs. fidelity), which is crucial for designing practical quantum communication networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on optimizing the End-to-End (E2E) entanglement throughput of a memory-based quantum repeater while satisfying a minimum required fidelity constraint.

**Main result.** Optimal performance requires treating the maximum waiting times in quantum memories as optimization variables, leading to model-derived optimal holding times that significantly boost achievable throughput compared to unconstrained scenarios.

**Method.** The authors employ queueing models and stochastic process theory (e.g., CTMC) to approximate the system's rate dynamics under decoherence constraints.

**Model / system.** The model describes a quantum repeater network with two links, utilizing quantum memories subject to dephasing and depolarizing noise during entanglement swapping operations.

**Key observables.** E2E entanglement throughput (rate), average E2E fidelity ($F_{	ext{req}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Memory capacities, entanglement attempt rates, classical communication latencies, and the maximum waiting times (MHTs) in quantum memories.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes independent Poisson generation of elementary entanglements and models decoherence using time-dependent depolarizing/dephasing channels; it does not consider entanglement purification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the timeline of entanglement attempts, show the throughput vs. fidelity trade-off curve, and compare performance when optimal model-based MHTs are enforced versus when they are ignored.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops queueing models to approximate E2E throughput, derives an expression for E2E fidelity accounting for memory noise and BSM errors, formulates the problem as maximizing rate subject to fidelity constraints, and validates this using simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the capacity of a memory-based quantum repeater in entanglement swapping between two quantum links with either single or multiple memories, which we refer to as the end-to-end (E2E) entanglement throughput, subject to a constraint on the minimum fidelity. In order to approximate the E2E entanglement throughput, we adopt queueing models, where quantum links can have different characteristics: memory capacities, entanglement attempt rates and success probabilities, as well as classical communication latencies. We develop a model for estimating E2E entanglement fidelity, while taking into account the heterogeneous dephasing and depolarizing dynamics of quantum memories and Bell-state measurements in entanglement swapping as well as classical communication delays and noises. Finally, with the help of our models for approximating the E2E entanglement throughput and fidelity, we use the maximum waiting times of entanglements in quantum memories at the repeater as optimization variables to maximize the E2E entanglement throughput while ensuring required minimum E2E fidelity.

</details>

### [Spectrally local geometric response at the onset of many-body quantum chaos](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11309v1)

**Authors:** George Mihailescu, Pablo M. Poggi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.11309v1>  
**Analysis basis:** abstract only; PDF text extraction failed or PDF unavailable
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper introduces the spectral density of geometric response as a diagnostic tool to study how many-body quantum systems transition from integrable to chaotic behavior. It reveals that this crossover involves an initial localization of eigenbasis deformations before they spread across the spectrum. The findings suggest universal features governing chaos onset that are not captured by simple random matrix models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a quantitative measure (spectral density) for tracking the onset of quantum chaos in complex many-body dynamics, which is relevant for understanding thermalization and energy transport in interacting systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how quantum systems transition from integrable regimes towards chaos, focusing on spectral properties.

**Main result.** It finds that the crossover to chaos is characterized by an exponentially sensitive accumulation of eigenbasis deformations in localized spectral regions before spreading out.

**Method.** The authors introduce and analyze a novel diagnostic tool called the 'spectral density of geometric response' to measure eigenstate sensitivity across the energy spectrum.

**Model / system.** The study is applied to general many-body quantum systems, comparing results from physically motivated random-matrix ensembles against featureless Gaussian ensembles.

**Key observables.** Spectral density of geometric response; accumulation and spreading of eigenbasis deformations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Integrability-to-chaos crossover scale; energy spectrum structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing physical random matrix models against idealized Gaussian ensembles to isolate universal features.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the spectral density of geometric response, applies it to many-body systems to characterize the integrability-to-chaos crossover, and contrasts the findings between different random matrix models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce the spectral density of geometric response, a measure of eigenstate sensitivity across an energy spectrum. Applied to many-body quantum systems, it reveals an exponentially sensitive integrability-to-chaos crossover, where eigenbasis deformations first accumulate in localized spectral regions before spreading throughout the spectrum. Physically motivated random-matrix ensembles reproduce this behaviour, whereas Gaussian ensembles do not, indicating universal features of the route to chaos that are absent from featureless random-matrix models.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive theoretical model to describe emergent heavy fermion physics and superconductivity near the Mott transition in twisted bilayer graphene. By treating the system through an emergent Kondo coupling mechanism involving mixed-valence excitations, the authors predict the formation of a heavy semimetal phase and unconventional superconducting domes at charge neutrality. This establishes a powerful, unified framework for correlated materials research.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a unified framework connecting Mott transitions, heavy fermion behavior, and unconventional superconductivity in a tunable solid-state system like TBG is highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated electron physics beyond simple Hubbard models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates emergent heavy fermion behavior and superconductivity that arise near a Mott transition in twisted bilayer graphene (TBG).

**Main result.** The authors establish a unified framework showing how mixed-valence physics leads to an emergent Kondo screening transition, resulting in a heavy semimetal phase and unconventional superconductivity.

**Method.** The analysis employs an emergent heavy-fermion framework utilizing an 'ancilla' theory involving orthogonal fermions ($\psi$) to capture mixed-valence Mott states. Techniques include Schrieffer-Wolff elimination, mean-field theory for pairing, and self-consistent determination of excitation weights.

**Model / system.** The system is Twisted Bilayer Graphene (TBG) modeled by an extended Hamiltonian involving itinerant carriers ($c$), local moments ($\psi'$), and charge excitations treated via doublon/holon operators. The physics is analyzed near integer fillings $
u$ relative to the Mott transition.

**Key observables.** Quasiparticle residue ($Z$), Fermi velocity ($v_F$), superconducting gap structure ($\Delta_{SC}$), Kondo temperature ($T_K$), and momentum-resolved occupations of doublon/holon excitations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hybridization coupling ($\gamma$) vs. on-site repulsion ($U$); twist angle ($	heta$); doping level ($
u$); and the anti-Hund's coupling ($J_A$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on an emergent heavy-fermion description derived from mixed-valence physics, using approximations like canonical fermions in a mixed state ensemble.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structure changes (band inversion), the generation of holon/doublon excitations, and plots showing $T_K$ dependence on the twist angle $	heta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the effective heavy-fermion model using ancilla fermions, deriving the Kondo coupling mechanism from mixed-valence physics, calculating the resulting low-energy semimetal state below $T_K$, and finally incorporating anti-Hund's coupling to predict superconductivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Near a bandwidth-tuned Mott transition, the Fermi velocity $v_F$ and quasiparticle residue $Z$ of a metal often vanish. Here, we show that analogous phenomena emerge in twisted bilayer graphene (TBG) at integer fillings and can be captured by an emergent heavy-fermion framework within a projective active-band limit. Unlike models incorporating remote bands, our effective heavy-fermion description arises from \textit{mixed-valence Mott} physics via decoupled charge and local-moment sectors. In the charge sector, active bands $c(\mathbf{k})$ hybridize with an emergent \emph{orthogonal fermion} $ψ(\mathbf{k})$ to open a large Mott gap at $|\mathbf{k}| > k_*$ ($k_*$ sets the momentum-patch size) and a quadratic band-touching semimetal near $\mathbf{k}=0$ at neutrality ($ν=0$). The orthogonal fermion is a linear combination of the doublon and holon excitations and may be written as $ψ_i \sim (δn^f_i+\frac{1}{2})^{-1} f_i$. An emergent Kondo coupling $J_K \sim U$ ($U$ is the local Hubbard interaction) between $ψ$ and local moments $ψ'$ frames the Mott transition as a Kondo screening transition, tuned by the twist angle $θ$. Away from the magic angle, a Kondo-screened heavy semimetal develops below $T_K$ (the Kondo temperature) with vanishing $Z$. Introducing anti-Hund's coupling $J_A$ generates an s-wave fully gapped or nematic, nodally gapped superconducting dome near the Mott boundary even at $ν=0$. At other integer fillings $ν= \pm 1, \pm 2$, increasing bandwidth first drives the small-gap Mott state into an intermediate quadratic band-touching semimetal before entering a heavy Fermi liquid with large Fermi surfaces. Our results establish a unified framework for emergent heavy fermion physics with both itinerant carriers and local moments from the $f$ orbital.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates quantum criticality in the two-impurity Kondo model by using entanglement measures as witnesses. By calculating observables like Quantum Fisher Information and various correlation functions across different coupling regimes, the authors demonstrate that these measurable quantities exhibit singular behavior precisely at the predicted quantum critical point. This establishes a robust theoretical framework for detecting exotic quantum phases in solid-state systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly connects abstract quantum information concepts (entanglement) to concrete, measurable physical quantities (susceptibilities, fluctuations), providing a powerful diagnostic tool for strongly correlated materials like heavy fermions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine if measurable spin and charge fluctuations can serve as entanglement witnesses to detect quantum critical points (QCPs) in impurity systems.

**Main result.** The analysis confirms that the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), derived from measurable correlations, tracks the entanglement changes across the non-Fermi-liquid QCP separating Kondo-screened and inter-impurity singlet phases.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced numerical techniques like Density-Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) and Numerical Renormalization Group (NRG) to calculate entanglement measures and QFI at zero and finite temperatures.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the two-impurity Kondo model, consisting of two spin-1/2 impurities coupled by an exchange interaction ($K$) and each coupled independently to a bath of conduction electrons via Kondo couplings ($J$).

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy ($S(A_{	ext{imp}})$), impurity-impurity correlator ($\langle \mathbf{S}_1 \cdot \mathbf{S}_2 angle$), Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) for spin and charge operators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical coupling $K_c(J)$ controls the transition between the Kondo-screened Fermi-liquid state and the inter-impurity singlet phase; temperature ($T$) is also varied.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping entanglement properties onto experimentally accessible dynamical response functions via QFI calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the model setup, showing how various observables like correlators and entanglement measures change across different quantum phases (e.g., 2KS vs IIS).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian in different coupling regimes ($J$ vs $K$), calculates ground-state and finite-temperature expectation values using DMRG/NRG, and then maps these results onto entanglement measures and QFI to pinpoint the critical point.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement is a defining feature of quantum mechanics, and its relation to quantum criticality is of considerable current interest. Here we show that measurable spin and charge fluctuations provide an entanglement witness of quantum criticality in the two-impurity Kondo model, which has experimental realizations in terms of coupled quantum dots and magnetic impurities added to surfaces. We use density-matrix renormalization group and numerical renormalization group calculations to resolve the non-Fermi-liquid critical point separating two independently Kondo-screened impurities from an inter-impurity singlet and interpret it as a change in the dominant entanglement partner of each local moment: from entanglement of the local moment with an extended set of conduction-electron degrees of freedom to entanglement with the other impurity. This reorganization is accompanied by a singular response of the impurity-bath entanglement and the inter-impurity susceptibility. We show how the same structure is encoded in the quantum Fisher information of collective spin and charge operators at zero and finite temperatures, connecting the entanglement picture to experimentally accessible dynamical response functions. Our results establish impurity systems as controlled settings in which quantum-critical entanglement can be detected through measurable correlations, and provide further insight into the possibility of understanding heavy-fermion physics in terms of entanglement.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously analyzes the passage from crystalline topological phases on a lattice to their corresponding continuum field theories. Using advanced algebraic topology, it proves that while every continuum phase can be realized on a lattice, the process loses information, meaning distinct lattice configurations can map to the same continuous description.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical framework for understanding how microscopic symmetries and lattice details constrain macroscopic continuum descriptions in topological materials, which is crucial for designing novel quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is rigorously determining whether the topological properties of gapped bosonic systems defined on a discrete lattice (crystalline phases) survive when taking the continuum limit to form a field theory.

**Main result.** The map from crystalline to continuum topological phases is surjective, meaning every continuum phase has a lattice realization; however, it is generally not injective, implying that some microscopic lattice information collapses in the continuum description.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced mathematical tools, including algebraic topology and category theory, to construct a rigorous stratification map relating crystalline classification groups ($\Theta^{d+1}(BG)$) to continuum ones ($\Theta^{d+1}(	ext{BO}(d))$).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on gapped bosonic phases of matter characterized by finite point-group symmetries ($G \subset O(d)$), analyzing the relationship between lattice topological invariants and their effective low-energy continuum field theories.

**Key observables.** Topological invariants, such as those classified by group cohomology ($\Theta^{d+1}(BG)$) and cobordism theory ($\Omega_{d+1}^O(BG)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is restricted to point-group symmetries ($G \subset O(d)$), deliberately excluding lattice translations, and focuses specifically on the 2-primary torsion sector of the classification groups.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work assumes gapped phases and restricts attention to bosonic systems whose continuum limit TQFTs are assumed to be diffeomorphism-invariant (excluding certain metric dependencies).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates a surjective 'funnel' mapping the classification of crystalline SPTs onto the continuum SPT classification.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical framework by defining the map between lattice and continuum classifications, proving its surjectivity, analyzing the loss of information (non-injectivity), and applying this general theory to specific physical examples like 2D rotation-symmetric phases and quantum critical points.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The continuum limit is a widely used theoretical construct for obtaining continuum field-theories of crystalline systems. We study the formulation of a continuum limit for gapped bosonic phases and ask whether their topological properties survive passage to the continuum. Using methods in algebraic topology and category theory, we give a rigorous formulation of the continuum limit and construct a surjective global map relating crystalline topological phases across all finite point-group symmetries to continuum invertible topological phases. Consequently, we find that some lattice phases admit no continuum limit, while distinct lattice phases that do admit a continuum limit can share the same continuum image, hence implying that some lattice topological data can collapse. Conversely, we find that every continuum invertible phase admits a faithful crystalline realization. In addition to the general framework, we apply it to several examples, including studies of a 2D rotation-symmetric phase, higher-order topological phases, and the mixed spin-lattice anomaly of the deconfined quantum critical point between an antiferromagnet and valence bond solid.

</details>

### [Contextuality in the $n$-qubit Pauli group](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11993v1)

**Authors:** Markus Frembs  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.11993v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously investigates contextuality within the $n$-qubit Pauli group by introducing a generalized notion of noncontextual property. It proves that for three or more qubits, this system is highly contextual, linking its structure to the geometry of symplectic polar spaces. This provides deep mathematical insight into the fundamental resource of quantum information.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep mathematical characterization of quantum contextuality in a fundamental model ($	ext{Pauli}_n$), linking it rigorously to advanced topics in algebraic geometry (symplectic polar spaces) and combinatorial set theory (Cameron-Liebler sets).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes contextuality within the $n$-qubit Pauli group, generalizing the concept of noncontextuality beyond simple valuations using Boolean-valued frame functions.

**Main result.** It establishes that for $n \geq 3$, the Pauli group admits no nontrivial noncontextual properties; furthermore, it shows that contextuality depends on the geometry of symplectic polar spaces rather than just the projective nature of the representation.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced mathematical tools including defining context posets, introducing 'noncontextual properties' via connection holonomy groups, and relating these to Boolean-valued frame functions and Cameron–Liebler sets.

**Model / system.** The primary system is the $n$-qubit Pauli group ($	ilde{P}_n$), which is analyzed through its underlying symplectic theory. The contextuality is mapped onto geometric structures like isotropic subspaces and polar spaces in a binary affine-symplectic space.

**Key observables.** Contextuality, noncontextual properties (diagnosed by the holonomy group $	ext{Hol}_{C_0}(
abla)$), Boolean-valued frame functions, and the weight $k$ of these functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $n$ (number of qubits); the structure of the symplectic form $\omega$; and specific weights $\{0, 1, 2, 2n-2, 2n-1, 2n\}$ for frame function analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The generalization of KS noncontextuality via Definition 3 (noncontextual property) is central; the analysis also relies on relating these properties to Lagrangian subspaces and Cameron–Liebler sets.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates mutually anti-commuting Pauli operators determining rank-2 projectors. Table I summarizes the existence of frame functions for different $n$ and theories, while Table II compares four distinct context posets associated with the system.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining generalized noncontextuality (Definition 3), establishing its equivalence to Boolean-valued frame functions (Theorem 1), analyzing specific cases ($n=2$), comparing the Pauli group context poset to the symplectic theory, and finally relating these results to the classification of Cameron–Liebler sets.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The $n$-qubit Pauli group is an essential ingredient to most quantum applications, from computing and error correction to benchmarking and simulation. Despite comprising merely a discrete set of operators, it exhibits many quintessential features of quantum theory, including contextuality, which has been identified as a key resource to quantum advantage in a variety of different flavours. Here, we extend this analysis, introducing the notion of a `noncontextual property' whose nonexistence proves the Kochen-Specker theorem, similarly to and generalising common arguments based on the nonexistence of valuations. We relate this notion formally to the existence of Boolean-valued frame functions, and characterise all such frame functions in the case of the $n$-qubit Pauli group.   For two qubits, we show that the Pauli group admits noncontextual properties, despite admitting no valuations. We then establish this as the only nontrivial such case with $n\geq 2$, by proving that any Boolean-valued frame function on stabiliser states is constant for more than two qubits. We also perform a similar analysis for the symplectic theory underlying the $n$-qubit Pauli group, for which nonconstant Boolean-valued frame functions exist for all $n$, yet only in restricted form. By comparison, this shows that contextuality in the $n$-qubit Pauli group is not only a consequence of the projective nature of the Pauli group as a representation of its underlying symplectic vector space, but of the geometry of symplectic polar spaces itself. In geometric terms, our result determines all Cameron-Liebler sets of maximal totally isotropic flats in the binary affine-symplectic space.

</details>

These are transformed into layer-wise scaled parameters Θi,ℓfor the quantum generator G. The pixel intensity is computed as G(c, z) = 1−⟨Z0⟩</figcaption>

**Summary.** CoQui introduces a novel Quantum Generative Adversarial Network that models image generation using coordinate-conditioned implicit functions, overcoming the qubit scaling issues of traditional QGANs. By reading out pixel intensities via expectation values at specific coordinates rather than encoding them into circuit amplitudes, it achieves superior performance on benchmark datasets while maintaining structural control over the generated image.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes QGANs beyond simple amplitude encoding by adopting an implicit function representation conditioned on continuous spatial coordinates. This structural change addresses fundamental scaling bottlenecks in quantum machine learning for imaging tasks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors address limitations in existing Quantum Generative Adversarial Networks (QGANs) for image generation, specifically the scaling issues with resolution and the probability competition when decoding multiple pixels.

**Main result.** CoQui outperforms established amplitude-mapping QGAN baselines (like FRQI-based methods) in both visual quality and quantitative metrics while requiring fewer qubits. The method successfully decouples image resolution from address qubit requirements.

**Method.** The core methodology reformulates image generation as coordinate-conditioned implicit function learning, where pixel intensities are read out by evaluating a variational quantum circuit at specific spatial coordinates $(c)$ conditioned on latent variables $(z)$.

**Model / system.** The model is CoQui, a QGAN framework built upon Quantum Implicit Neural Representations (QINR). It uses a classical embedding network to generate parameters for a parameterized quantum circuit that maps continuous spatial coordinates and latent codes to pixel intensities via expectation values.

**Key observables.** Pixel intensity derived from the expectation value of a dedicated color qubit ($\langle Z_0 angle$), evaluated across all spatial coordinates $(c)$. Quantitative metrics include FID, JSD, Entropy, Brightness, and Sobel distance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The model's structure involves trainable parameters governing circuit layers (e.g., scale $S_l$, bias $B_l$) and the number of feature qubits ($N_f$), which are optimized during training.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that image generation can be accurately modeled as a continuous, coordinate-conditioned implicit function learned via quantum circuits. Limitations include reliance on classical simulation and neglecting realistic hardware noise.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall CoQui framework (input $ightarrow$ embedding $ightarrow$ VQC $ightarrow$ readout) and show representative MNIST samples comparing Full CoQui against variants lacking key structural components, demonstrating performance gains.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies limitations in existing QGANs. It then proposes the CoQui architecture, detailing its coordinate-conditioned implicit mapping approach. The methodology involves defining the classical embedding network and the complex structure of the variational quantum circuit (including color qubit initialization, data re-uploading layers, entanglement, and residual updates). Finally, it presents quantitative ablation studies and capacity analyses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum generative adversarial networks (QGANs) have attracted increasing attention for image generation using parameterized quantum circuits. Existing amplitude-based approaches face two key limitations: pixel locations are typically encoded by computational-basis indices or address qubits, causing quantum resources to grow with image resolution; meanwhile, jointly decoding many pixels from normalized quantum states introduces probability competition among pixels and limits precise pixel-wise control. To address these issues, we reformulate quantum image generation as coordinate-conditioned implicit function learning. Our method takes spatial coordinates and latent variables as inputs, uses a classical embedding network to generate input-dependent circuit parameters, and evaluates a variational quantum circuit at each coordinate. Pixel intensities are directly obtained from the expectation value of a dedicated color qubit, and a complete image is generated by querying all spatial coordinates. This design decouples image resolution from address-qubit requirements and avoids shared probability-normalization constraints across pixels. We further design a specialized variational quantum circuit to provide structural inductive bias for coordinate-conditioned generation. Simulated experiments on two benchmark datasets show that our method outperforms FRQI-based generation and PQWGAN in visual and quantitative quality while using fewer qubits, and also achieves better generation quality than the corresponding classical baseline.

</details>

### [Isospectral majorization and isoperimetric inequalities for coherent states on the Bloch sphere](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12248v1)

**Authors:** Luis Daniel Abreu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.12248v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

Amplification raises the representation degree from PN to PM, while the natural normalization preserves the covariant symbol and its level curves on the Bloch sphere: QXM(X) = QX.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a fixed-spectrum majorization theory for coherent states on the Bloch sphere. By proving an isospectral version of the Lieb-Solovej inequality, it shows that passive rearrangement minimizes entropy. This spectral result is then applied to derive sharp isoperimetric inequalities for Toeplitz operators associated with measurable subsets of the sphere.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects fundamental concepts from quantum information geometry (coherent states, entropy) with classical geometric inequalities (isoperimetry), providing powerful tools applicable to characterizing optimal state preparations or spatial distributions in quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes isospectral majorization relationships and derives sharp isoperimetric inequalities for coherent states defined on the Bloch sphere.

**Main result.** It proves a fixed-spectrum Lieb-Solovej inequality, showing that passive rearrangement minimizes certain entropy measures, which in turn yields isoperimetric bounds for Toeplitz operators related to spherical caps.

**Method.** The analysis uses functional inequalities (convex/concave functions $\Phi$) applied to the Husimi function and leverages majorization theory ($\prec$) derived from spectral properties of Toeplitz operators.

**Model / system.** The system is based on density operators $ho$ acting on the Hilbert space of analytic polynomials $P_N$, which models coherent states on the Bloch sphere ($	ext{SU}(2)$). The analysis involves Toeplitz operators $T_\Omega$ defined by subsets $\Omega$ of the sphere.

**Key observables.** Husimi function ($Q_ho(z)$), Wehrl entropy ($S_W(ho)$), and partial sums of eigenvalues ($\sum \lambda_j(\Omega)$) of Toeplitz operators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The polynomial degree $N$, the rank of the density operator $	ext{Rank}(ho)=r$, and the measure/area $m(\Omega)$ of the subset $\Omega$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are derived for fixed spectra, extending previous rank-1 results. The extension to general convex functions relies on approximating them with piecewise linear functions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the Lieb-Solovej inequality (comparing $ho$ to its passive rearrangement $ho_{\downarrow}$) to establish entropy minimization, culminating in the application of this spectral theory to derive sharp isoperimetric inequalities for Toeplitz operators on the sphere.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Let $\mathcal{P}_{N}$ be the $(N+1)$-dimensional Hilbert space of analytic polynomials of degree at most $N$. This is the natural environment to define $SU(2)$ (Bloch) coherent states. Let $Q_{ρ}$ be the Husimi function of a density operator $ρ$ on $% \mathcal{P}_{N}$. We prove an isospectral version of Lieb-Solovej inequality: if $ρ^{\downarrow }$ is obtained by placing the eigenvalues of $ρ$ in decreasing order along the monomial basis, then \begin{equation*} \int_{\mathbb{C}}Φ(Q_{ρ}(z))\,dm(z)\leq \int_{\mathbb{C}}Φ(Q_{ρ^{\downarrow }}(z))\,dm(z) \end{equation*}% for every convex function $Φ$ on $[0,1]$. Applying the corresponding reversed inequality to the concave function $Φ(t)=-t\log t$ gives the Wehrl entropy. In the process it is show that the output state of $ρ$ under Lieb-Solovej's channel is majorized by the output of the state $ρ^{\downarrow }$.   As an application, among all measurable subsets of the sphere having a fixed area, spherical caps maximize every partial sum of the eigenvalues of Toeplitz operators with symbol $1_Ω$. Equivalently, caps maximize all Ky Fan norms, leading to the sharp inequalities, previously known for $r=1$: \begin{equation*} \sum_{j=1}^{r}λ_{j}(Ω) \leq r-\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}(r-k)\binom{N+1}{k} m(Ω)^{k}\bigl(1-m(Ω)\bigr)^{N+1-k}. \end{equation*} This implies isoperimetric inequalities for all Schatten sums of $T_Ω$, obtained without using the spherical isoperimetric inequality.

</details>
