**Summary.** This paper rigorously classifies all oriented circulant graphs that support Perfect State Transfer (PST) during a continuous-time quantum walk. By employing advanced number theory involving quadratic Dirichlet characters, the authors prove that PST can only occur if the graph's underlying structure is determined by conductors $\Delta \in \{3, 4, 8\}$. This provides a complete mathematical blueprint for realizing such quantum transport phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep mathematical characterization of quantum transport phenomena on structured graphs, which has direct implications for designing quantum communication channels or quantum simulators based on lattice structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to classify all oriented circulant graphs that exhibit Perfect State Transfer (PST) between distinct vertices.

**Main result.** PST in these graphs is strictly limited to cases where the graph's structure is governed by conductors $\Delta \in \{3, 4, 8\}$, which corresponds to square-free radicands of 1, 2, and 3.

**Method.** The classification uses advanced number theory, specifically properties of odd primitive quadratic Dirichlet characters and Fourier analysis applied to the graph's Hermitian adjacency matrix spectrum.

**Model / system.** The model is a continuous-time quantum walk on an oriented circulant graph $	ext{Cay}(\mathbb{Z}_n, C)$. The dynamics are governed by the Hamiltonian derived from the graph's Hermitian adjacency matrix $A$.

**Key observables.** Perfect State Transfer (PST) and Multiple State Transfer (MST), which depend directly on the Fourier eigenvalues of the adjacency matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The conductor $\Delta$ of the underlying quadratic Dirichlet character, and the graph order $n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the graph is finite and simple, relying heavily on number theoretic tools related to characters and gcd-classes.

**Figures summary.** Table 2 provides a comprehensive classification summarizing representative graphs exhibiting PST, categorized by their conductor $\Delta$, square-free radicand $\delta$, order $n$, and counts of specific transfer cardinalities ($h$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing spectral properties using quadratic characters (Section 2), deriving general state transfer theorems from the spectrum (Section 3), and finally applying these constraints to restrict the possible conductors ($\Delta$) to complete the classification (Section 4).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The continuous-time quantum walk on an oriented circulant graph is determined by the Fourier eigenvalues of its Hermitian adjacency matrix. We classify perfect state transfer (PST) between distinct vertices in every nonempty oriented circulant graph. We show that each such graph is described by an odd primitive quadratic Dirichlet character of conductor $Δ$, a set of gcd-classes, and a choice between the two orientations of each selected class. For a graph of order $n$, we derive an explicit formula for every Fourier eigenvalue without assuming that $n/Δ$ is coprime to $Δ$. We prove that PST occurs only for $Δ\in\{3,4,8\}$ and give necessary and sufficient conditions on the connection set for each conductor. Equivalently, the square-free radicands of oriented circulant graphs with PST are exactly $1$, $2$, and $3$. More generally, when $λ_j=\sqrt{D}η_j$ with $η_j\in\mathbb{Z}$, congruences satisfied by the integers $η_j$ determine all PST pairs and times, the minimum period, and the largest vertex sets supporting multiple state transfer (MST). In this class, pretty good state transfer is equivalent to PST. We also determine the connected orders and enumerate the resulting graphs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper evaluates Quantum Machine Learning techniques for selecting rare events in high-energy collider physics. It demonstrates that parameterised quantum models provide an inherent robustness against detector noise and data shifts compared to state-of-the-art classical methods. This suggests QML can be a valuable tool for building reliable analysis pipelines in complex experimental settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is HEP, the core methodology—using quantum circuits to induce a specific inductive bias (robustness) that resists input perturbations better than highly expressive classical models—is relevant for understanding model stability in complex physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing machine learning methods robust enough for high-energy physics data analysis that can maintain performance despite detector degradation or shifts in data distributions.

**Main result.** Quantum models, specifically Quantum Autoencoders and classifiers, demonstrated superior robustness compared to expressive classical baselines when tested under controlled feature-level smearing.

**Method.** The study compares quantum (QAEs) and classical autoencoders/classifiers for both unsupervised anomaly detection and supervised signal classification tasks.

**Model / system.** The system is the analysis of collider event data, where models are trained on reference conditions but tested under controlled feature-level smearing to simulate detector variability. The quantum components involve angle encoding and parameterized unitary circuits.

**Key observables.** Anomaly detection scores (based on fidelity to vacuum state), discrimination performance retention, and the shift in output score distributions under perturbation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detector smearing strength (perturbation level) and training sample size ($N_{train}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison assumes that fixing model parameters while varying input features (smearing) is a valid test of robustness. The study also notes limitations regarding realistic detector systematics and finite-shot statistics.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the Mean Squared Error deviation of anomaly scores for various models (AE, VAE, QAE variants) as a function of increasing detector smearing strength, showing quantum models maintain better stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper investigates robustness in two settings: unsupervised anomaly detection using QAEs trained on background events, and supervised classification using data reuploading techniques. Results compare these quantum approaches against established classical ML baselines under simulated noise.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Robust machine-learning methods are becoming increasingly important for high-energy physics data analysis as experiments enter the era of higher luminosity and future higher-energy colliders. Detector degradation, changing running conditions and calibration drift can shift data distributions, causing models trained on clean reference samples to degrade after deployment. We investigate whether parameterised quantum models provide a useful inductive bias for robust collider-event selection in two complementary settings. In the unsupervised study, quantum autoencoders trained on background events are compared with classical and variational autoencoders for anomaly detection. In the supervised study, quantum classifiers with data reuploading are trained to distinguish a supersymmetric signal from background and are compared with linear and multilayer-perceptron classifiers. All models are trained under reference conditions and subsequently evaluated under controlled feature-level smearing while their parameters and preprocessing transformations are held fixed. On clean inputs, the quantum autoencoders achieve competitive anomaly-detection performance, including in the low-false-positive-rate regime relevant for triggering, while the deeper data-reuploading classifier attains discrimination comparable to the non-linear classical baseline. Under smearing, the quantum models generally exhibit smaller shifts in their output scores and retain their discrimination more effectively than the expressive classical baselines. These results suggest that parameterised quantum models can provide a useful robustness inductive bias for collider-event selection and motivate further studies with realistic detector systematics, finite-shot statistics and quantum-device noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rethinks quantum computation by deriving its computational structure from the relativistic description of a massive spin-1/2 particle (the Dirac carrier). It shows that this intrinsic structure imposes limitations on possible quantum gates, distinguishing between mathematical symmetries and physically realizable operations. Ultimately, it provides necessary and sufficient conditions for achieving full control over this fundamental information carrier.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical framework linking fundamental particle physics symmetries (Dirac equation) directly to the constraints on quantum computation, offering a physical basis for gate sets beyond abstract assumptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how computational structure can be derived intrinsically from a fundamental relativistic quantum system, rather than assuming abstract carriers first.

**Main result.** It establishes the Dirac information carrier as a source of a constrained quantum logic (sector-preserving and sector-coupling gates) whose full controllability requires specific physical resources beyond simple unitary transformations.

**Method.** The analysis uses group theory applied to the symmetry algebra ($\mathfrak{su}(4)$) governing the spin-1/2 system, distinguishing between mathematical unitaries and physically admissible quantum logic gates.

**Model / system.** The model is the massive spin-1/2 particle described by the Dirac equation. The resulting information carrier is termed the 'Dirac ququart,' possessing an intrinsic positive- and negative-energy decomposition ($H_+ \oplus H_-$).

**Key observables.** Admissible quantum logic gates, sector coherence, and controllability conditions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mass ($m$), momentum ($\vec{p}$), and the structure of the Poincaré representation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that computational structure must arise from the intrinsic symmetries dictated by relativistic field theory (Dirac equation).

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the mathematical flow involves mapping generators from $	ext{Spin}^+(1, 3)$ through the Dirac carrier to $SU(4)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first derives the intrinsic computational structure from the positive/negative energy decomposition of the Dirac equation. It then analyzes the symmetry group ($SU(4)$) and defines physically admissible gates. Finally, it uses group theory (normalizers) to determine the necessary conditions for achieving full single-carrier controllability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computation has traditionally been formulated by postulating abstract information carriers and subsequently identifying physical systems that realize them. We adopt the opposite viewpoint and ask what computational structure is supplied by a fundamental relativistic quantum system itself. Focusing on the simplest nontrivial massive spin carrier, spin-1/2, we show that its relativistic description supplies a native four-dimensional information carrier through the Dirac equation. The resulting Dirac information carrier possesses an intrinsic positive- and negative-energy decomposition that induces a physics-constrained computational structure comprising sector-preserving and sector-coupling quantum logic. Restricting the relativistic computational structure to the positive-energy sector and taking the nonrelativistic regime recovers the familiar Pauli qubit description. We distinguish mathematical unitary transformations from physically admissible gates and show how charge-conjugation structure, charge superselection, and associated reference-frame resources can constrain quantum logic beyond the sector-preserving structure. Finally, under appropriate physical assumptions, we establish the conditions under which this structure supports full single-carrier controllability. Our work establishes the Dirac information carrier as the massive spin-1/2 instance of a broader programme in which computational structures are derived from the relativistic representation carried by the underlying physical system.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper resolves an open problem by proving that the min-Rains relative entropy does not provide a tight upper bound for exact distillable entanglement under PPT operations. The authors construct explicit counterexamples using specific bipartite states to demonstrate a strict separation between the true distillation rate and the theoretical bound, revealing subtle asymptotic structures in quantum information theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental theoretical constraints on quantum information processing, specifically refining our understanding of entanglement manipulation under physical constraints like PPT operations, which is crucial for designing quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central question is whether the min-Rains relative entropy provides a tight upper bound for the regularized exact PPT distillable entanglement of a bipartite quantum state.

**Main result.** The authors prove that the min-Rains relative entropy is not always tight, establishing a strict inequality between the true distillable rate and the min-Rains bound using specific counterexamples.

**Method.** The proof involves constructing two independent bounds—an upper bound derived from a range-supported tensor witness and a lower bound related to the min-Rains relative entropy—and showing they are strictly separated for certain states.

**Model / system.** The analysis concerns bipartite quantum states undergoing exact entanglement distillation under completely PPT-preserving operations. The focus is on the support projection $P$ of the input state, often analyzed using rank-three qutrit systems as a concrete counterexample.

**Key observables.** Regularized exact PPT distillable entanglement ($E_{\infty}^{0,	ext{PPT}}(ho)$), min-Rains relative entropy ($R_{\min}(ho)$), and the witness values ($\mu = \|T\Gamma\|_1$ and $\|R\Gamma\|_{\infty}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison relies on specific numerical ratios derived from the state support, such as $391/250$ versus $6393/10000$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of completely PPT-preserving operations and utilizes techniques involving partial transposition ($\Gamma$) and tensor witnesses.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the comparison relies on establishing strict inequalities between calculated numerical bounds derived from witness operators.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the problem by comparing $E_{\infty}^{0,	ext{PPT}}(ho)$ to $R_{\min}(ho)$. It then develops the necessary mathematical tools (range witnesses and dual certificates) to establish both an upper bound ($\log \mu$) and a lower bound ($-\log M(P)$), culminating in a concrete counterexample showing the separation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exact entanglement distillation converts a noisy bipartite state into a maximally entangled state with zero error. Under completely PPT-preserving operations, the additive min-Rains relative entropy provides a single-letter upper bound on the regularized distillation rate. An interesting problem in entanglement theory dating back to 2016 has been whether this bound is always tight. Here we resolve this question in the negative. The key is a tensor-stable rigidity absent from the min-Rains relaxation: every feasible exact-distillation effect must act as the identity on the support of the input state. We convert this constraint into a new single-letter upper bound using a generally non-Hermitian, range-supported witness. For a rank-three subspace, we show this upper bound lies strictly below the min-Rains relative entropy for every state with that support. Thus, the constraint discarded by the min-Rains relaxation remains relevant under arbitrary tensor powers. Our result rules out the min-Rains relative entropy as a closed-form formula for exact PPT distillable entanglement and reveals the subtle asymptotic structure of exact entanglement manipulation under PPT operations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a systematic framework to predict the error floor in quantum error correction codes by analyzing their detector error models. It uses exhaustive enumeration of 'trapping sets'—specific graph structures causing decoding failure—to bypass costly simulations. The results establish trapping-set analysis as a powerful tool for guiding the joint design of both quantum codes and decoding algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology of using structural analysis (trapping sets) to predict performance limits is highly relevant for designing robust quantum computation protocols by identifying inherent hardware/algorithmic weaknesses.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to understand the structural weaknesses of message-passing decoders used for quantum error correction in the low-error-rate regime.

**Main result.** Trapping-set analysis provides a practical framework that accurately predicts the achievable error floor for iterative decoders, revealing performance gaps between code distance and actual decoding capability.

**Method.** The authors introduce a systematic framework involving exhaustive enumeration of 'trapping sets' directly applied to the detector error model (DEM) of QLDPC codes.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes Quantum Low-Density Parity-Check (QLDPC) codes under circuit-level noise, modeled via their Detector Error Model (DEM). The analysis focuses on message-passing decoders like RelayBP and ImpulseBP.

**Key observables.** Error floor (minimum logical error rate), Trapping Sets (TSs), low-weight fault configurations, and the gap between code distance and practical performance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low-error-rate regime, circuit-level noise, weight of error patterns, and the structure of the Tanner graph associated with the DEM.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on restricting the search to 'leafless elementary trapping sets' (LETSs), which is motivated by classical LDPC literature but its applicability to QLDPC/DEMs is noted as an assumption.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically defines the DEM structure based on fault origins ($D(r)$, $A(r)$, $U(r)$) derived from circuit scheduling, then applies graph theory algorithms (like dpl-search) to enumerate LETSs, and finally evaluates these sets against various iterative decoders.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Message-passing decoders are among the most promising candidates for scalable quantum error correction, yet their behavior in the low-error-rate regime remains poorly understood under realistic circuit-level noise. In this work, we introduce a systematic framework for identifying the graph structures that govern decoder failures and for using them to predict the resulting error floor. We apply exhaustive trapping-set enumeration directly to the detector error model of a bivariate bicycle code and test all low-weight fault configurations supported on the resulting structures. This converts the analysis of extremely rare logical failures into a finite structural search, avoiding the prohibitive cost of direct Monte Carlo simulation. We evaluate the framework on three iterative decoders with substantially different architectures and decoding heuristics. Remarkably, for \texttt{RelayBP}, the resulting prediction accurately reproduces the simulated error floor; for the others, it remains within the same order of magnitude. Despite their differences, leafless elementary trapping sets capture a substantial part of the low-weight error-floor contribution for all three decoders. Moreover, each decoder admits failures caused by fault configurations well below the correction capability implied by the circuit-level distance, revealing a substantial gap between code distance and practical iterative-decoding performance. These results establish trapping-set analysis as a practical framework for predicting error floors, exposing the structural weaknesses of iterative decoders, and guiding the joint design of decoding algorithms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a highly optimized quantum circuit decomposition for the double qubit excitation gate. The authors achieved a record low CNOT count of 12, significantly improving upon previous state-of-the-art implementations. This research is vital for advancing practical quantum computing by minimizing required physical resources like gates and depth.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource optimization in quantum circuit design, which is crucial for mapping complex theoretical gates onto near-term hardware architectures used in AMO simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is achieving an effective implementation of high-level quantum gates, specifically the Double Qubit Excitation Operator ($U_{klij}(	heta)$), for practical quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors report a novel decomposition requiring only 12 CNOT gates, which sets new state-of-the-art records for CNOT count (12), CNOT depth (10), and total circuit depth (16).

**Method.** They developed a specific quantum circuit decomposition technique based on the double qubit excitation operator's definition, comparing it against existing methods like Gray-code expansion.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes gate implementation within a 4-qubit system using the computational basis ordering $|q_i q_j q_k q_l\rangle$. The target operation is a continuous rotation between states $|0011\rangle$ and $|1100\rangle$.

**Key observables.** CNOT count, CNOT depth, single-qubit gate count, and total circuit depth are the primary metrics used for comparison.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rotation angle $\theta$ is a key parameter defining the excitation operator; the system size is 4 qubits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the high-level double-excitation operator structure, baseline 14-CNOT circuits, previous SOTA 13-CNOT circuits, and finally, the proposed optimized 12-CNOT circuit.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the necessity of implementing complex gates, defines the target operator, presents a comparison against existing state-of-the-art methods using metrics like CNOT count, and concludes by detailing their superior 12-CNOT decomposition.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Effective implementation of high-level quantum gates is essential for practical quantum computing. To the best of our knowledge, we present the first reported 12-CNOT decomposition of the double qubit excitation operator, improving upon state-of-the-art (SOTA) implementations with 13 CNOTs. Our new circuit has the lowest CNOT count (12), lowest CNOT depth (10), and lowest total circuit depth (16) among all the previous SOTA circuits. Further, we only added 2 extra one-qubit gates compared to the lowest one-qubit gate count (11) among the previous SOTA circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles a foundational question in quantum learning theory: whether quantum examples offer an advantage over classical ones. Using complexity arguments relative to oracles, the authors prove that such a separation exists for generating certain distribution classes. This advances the understanding of where quantum resources provide strictly more computational power than classical ones in machine learning contexts.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, demonstrating computational separations between classical and quantum resources in learning theory provides fundamental insights into the limits of quantum computation that could inform resource requirements for simulating complex physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether quantum examples provide a computational advantage over classical examples when performing learning tasks within the PAC framework.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that, relative to an oracle, there exist distribution classes that can be efficiently generated by a quantum learner using quantum examples but cannot be generated by a quantum learner restricted to only classical examples.

**Method.** The authors use computational complexity theory, building on one-way permutations and establishing separations between the sample complexity required for generation/learning under quantum versus classical access models.

**Model / system.** The setting is theoretical learning theory (PAC framework), utilizing abstract concepts like concept classes induced by auxiliary functions ($g$) and relying on computational hardness assumptions related to inverting one-way permutations.

**Key observables.** The ability to efficiently PAC generate a distribution class, quantified by the required sample complexity advantage between quantum and classical access methods.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The existence of an oracle for a one-way permutation ($g$), and parameters related to pseudoperiods ($\varepsilon(f, a)$) in specific constructions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Results are shown relative to the assumption of oracle access to auxiliary functions (like $g$) and rely on the assumed existence of quantum-resilient one-way permutations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates separations between classical/quantum computation power; Figure 2 depicts the PAC learning setting using an inducing function $g$; Figure 3 shows a quadrilateral structure related to pseudoperiods.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing general oracle separations (Theorem 1.2), then presenting the main result showing quantum example advantage for generation (Theorem 1.3), and finally detailing a specific separation using Simon's algorithm context based on pseudoperiods (Theorem 4.2).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the power of quantum examples, as compared to classical examples, in the PAC learning framework. Here, we have two learning algorithms, both with access to quantum computation, but one gets quantum examples, whereas the other gets classical examples. It was previously unknown whether there were learning tasks that can be efficiently performed but not by the latter. Our primary result is to show that relative to an oracle, there are distributions that can be efficiently generated by a quantum learner with access to quantum examples, but not by a quantum learner with access to only classical examples, making progress to answering this question in the affirmative.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the complex problem of finding the precise ground-state energy asymptotics for interacting bosons on a lattice using the Birman-Schwinger method. The authors introduce a powerful order-matching criterion to determine when simple asymptotic expansions are insufficient, allowing them to derive complete results that are cross-validated numerically against an exact closed-form benchmark.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous development of the order-matching criterion provides a general tool for analyzing strong-coupling limits in lattice many-body physics, which is crucial for accurately determining physical constants beyond leading-order approximations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the precise strong-coupling ground-state asymptotics for a lattice three-boson system using the Birman-Schwinger analysis.

**Main result.** The authors derived the complete strong-coupling ground-state asymptotics at $K=\pi$, including the coefficient of the $O(1/\mu)$ term, and established a general order-matching criterion for such analyses.

**Method.** The methodology combines an exact closed-form benchmark derived from a K-uniform elliptic reduction with a newly formulated order-matching criterion to rigorously determine necessary asymptotic refinements.

**Model / system.** The system is the three-boson Schrödinger operator on a lattice ($\mathbb{Z}^2$) with pairwise contact interactions, analyzed in the strong-coupling limit ($\mu 	o \infty$).

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy asymptotics ($z_\pi(1/\mu)$), spectral gap, and the fiber Fredholm determinant $\Delta_\mu$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($\mu$), total quasimomentum ($K$), and the lattice momentum $p$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on asymptotic expansion techniques for eigenvalues derived from the Birman-Schwinger operator, requiring careful application of the order-matching criterion.

**Figures summary.** Tables compare exact vs. leading-order Fredholm determinants at $K=\pi$, and show numerical convergence confirming the derived asymptotic constants.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the Birman-Schwinger framework, then derives an elliptic reduction for the determinant, formulates and proves the order-matching criterion (Lemma 4.2), applies this to find the full asymptotics at $K=\pi$, and finally compares results with known cases like $K=0$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the strong-coupling Birman-Schwinger analysis of the three-boson lattice Schroedinger operator at the exceptional quasimomentum K = pi. First, we provide an exact closed-form benchmark for the fiber Fredholm determinant at a distinguished flat momentum, valid for every quasimomentum K, obtained via a uniform elliptic reduction of the two-dimensional lattice integral. Second, we formulate and prove a general order-matching criterion. This criterion decides whether a leading-order Fredholm determinant asymptotic, with relative accuracy of order 1/mu, suffices to determine the constant-order additive correction in the strong-coupling energy, or if the next-order refinement is required. Applying the criterion at K = pi, we derive the complete strong-coupling ground-state asymptotics, including the coefficient of the order 1/mu term. The results are cross-validated against the exact closed-form benchmark and two independent high-precision numerical schemes. Furthermore, we determine the corresponding spectral gap and prove an exact Fredholm-determinant underestimation factor equal to 2. Additionally, we independently confirm that the previously established K = 0 constant is correctly derived and requires no analogous refinement. Finally, we compare the orders of asymptotic precision controlled across recent lattice few-body models and draw a structural parallel with the parity classification of topological band structures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Symphony, an advanced compiler that optimizes Hamiltonian simulation circuits by treating the entire Pauli sequence as a global algebraic structure represented by the Binary Symplectic Form (BSF). By applying controlled-Pauli Clifford transformations directly to this BSF tableau, it achieves superior circuit compression compared to existing methods. This results in substantial reductions in required two-qubit gates and overall circuit depth for quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful algorithmic tool for quantum circuit compilation, directly impacting the feasibility of running complex algorithms like VQE or QAOA on near-term hardware by minimizing gate count and depth.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is efficiently compiling Hamiltonian simulation workloads, which are expressed as Pauli exponential sequences, by fully exploiting their global algebraic structure.

**Main result.** The proposed method achieves significant reductions in two-qubit gate count (average 59%) and circuit depth (average 91%) compared to naïve synthesis on standard benchmarks.

**Method.** It introduces Symphony, a holistic compilation approach utilizing the Binary Symplectic Form (BSF) representation of Pauli strings. This involves applying generalized controlled-Pauli Clifford transformations directly to the global BSF tableau for simultaneous simplification and parallelism extraction.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum computation simulating time evolution governed by Hamiltonians expressed as weighted sums of Pauli strings ($\sum c_k P_k$). The input is a sequence of Pauli rotations, analyzed using the BSF representation which encodes commutation relations via symplectic geometry.

**Key observables.** Two-qubit gate count and circuit depth are the primary optimization metrics. Other diagnostics include the BSF weight reduction and the rank of interaction matrices for two-qubit blocks.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The efficiency is measured by factors like $1.14–1.58	imes$ improvement in CNOT gates and $1.87–5.67	imes$ improvement in circuit depth over prior state-of-the-art compilers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Prior methods are limited by constrained paradigms, such as strict adherence to 'first-diagonalize, then-search-CNOT' or computationally expensive long-horizon searches; Symphony overcomes these limitations holistically.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between naïve synthesis and simultaneous simplification via Clifford conjugation on a BSF tableau. Other figures detail how Clifford transformations act as symplectic updates on the BSF structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theory around Pauli commutation using the symplectic form, introduces Symphony's global BSF transformation framework, details the causality-preserving block rescheduling heuristic, and validates its performance against state-of-the-art compilers across multiple benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hamiltonian simulation is a core quantum workload, underpinning variational quantum algorithms and Trotterized time evolution. Such programs are expressed as Pauli exponential sequences, exhibiting structural patterns that are highly amenable to high-level synthesis and optimization. Existing compilers, however, fail to fully unlock the optimization potential of their global algebraic structure, even when employing advanced graph- or tableau-based methods.   We present Symphony, a holistic compilation approach built on the binary symplectic form (BSF) representation of Pauli strings. Unlike prior group-wise BSF simplification and path-based Pauli network synthesis, Symphony applies generalized controlled-Pauli Clifford transformations directly to a global BSF tableau, adaptively reducing active Pauli rows and emitting eligible two-qubit blocks other than single-qubit rotations in a forward Clifford frame. Following algebraic simplification, Symphony performs a causality-preserving block rescheduling heuristic that respects frame-induced dependencies while exposing extensive two-qubit block parallelism opportunities. This streamlined compilation style comprehensively exploits simultaneous simplification and commutativity opportunities, achieving efficient global optimization without relying on computationally expensive heuristics or long-horizon searches. Across the generic Hamiltonian simulation benchmarks in HamLib, Symphony achieves average reductions of 59% in two-qubit gate count and 91% in circuit depth. It strictly Pareto-dominates prior state-of-the-art compilers, requiring 1.14--1.58$\times$ fewer two-qubit gates and especially shrinking two-qubit circuit depth by a substantial factor of 1.87--5.67$\times$ on average.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, resource-efficient reversible quantum circuit formulation for modular reduction using Crandall reduction tailored for pseudo-Mersenne moduli. By analyzing the folding and normalization steps, the authors derive two variants that significantly reduce the required T-count and T-depth compared to established methods like Barrett reduction. This work demonstrates the practical importance of exploiting modulus-specific arithmetic structure in designing fault-tolerant quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on computation rather than AMO physics, the rigorous analysis of resource scaling (T-depth/T-count) and fault tolerance is highly relevant to any quantum simulation or algorithm implementation that requires deep circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing highly efficient, reversible quantum circuits for modular reduction, which is a critical component in quantum algorithms like Shor's algorithm.

**Main result.** The authors propose two variants of Crandall reduction that achieve significantly lower T-count and T-depth (up to 46.9% reduction at n=10) compared to optimized folding Barrett reduction, even when analyzed under fault-tolerant surface code models.

**Method.** They translate classical modular arithmetic techniques for pseudo-Mersenne moduli ($q = 2^n - c$) into explicit reversible quantum circuits by analyzing the necessary folding and normalization steps.

**Model / system.** The work is situated in fault-tolerant quantum computation, utilizing resource estimates (T-count, T-depth) derived from surface code analysis. The arithmetic operations are modeled using specialized quantum gates for addition, multiplication, and subtraction.

**Key observables.** T-count, T-depth, required qubits, and estimated runtimes under the Sparse Blossom decoder.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Modulus size parameter $n$, the constant $c$ defining the modulus structure ($q=2^n-c$), and the specific normalization steps (one-step vs. two-step).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The resource estimates are based on worst-case logical bounds, assuming the use of a T-depth-one primitive for temporary logical-AND computation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall circuit structure for small parameters (n=4, c=3), detail quantum circuits for arithmetic components like adders and multipliers, and show specialized logic gates used in the reduction process.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the structural advantage of Crandall reduction over Barrett reduction. It then derives explicit reversible quantum circuit formulations (Crandall-1 and Crandall-2) based on normalization requirements, followed by detailed resource analysis yielding closed-form T-depth formulas, culminating in performance comparisons against existing methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Modular arithmetic is central to quantum algorithms for cryptographic problems, including Shor's algorithm and Grover-based cryptanalysis, with modular reduction contributing substantially to circuit cost. Pseudo-Mersenne moduli $q=2^n-c$ allow classical Crandall reduction to replace division with folding and constant arithmetic, providing a structural opportunity for more efficient quantum modular reduction than Barrett reduction. We translate this advantage into a reversible quantum setting by deriving explicit folding and normalization conditions for $2n$-bit inputs. To the best of our knowledge, this constitutes the first exact reversible quantum circuit formulation of Crandall reduction. Based on this formulation, we develop two variants: Crandall reduction-1 is designed to minimize execution cost through one-step normalization, whereas Crandall reduction-2 uses two-step normalization to support a wider range of $c$ with limited overhead. Logical resource estimates show that both variants require fewer qubits and lower T-count and T-depth than optimized folding Barrett reduction. At $n=10$, Crandall reduction-1 reduces both T-count and T-depth by approximately 46.9% relative to optimized folding Barrett reduction. Surface-code analysis further shows that, at $n=20$ under the Sparse Blossom decoder, the estimated runtimes of the two variants are 30.05 ms and 35.39 ms, respectively, compared with 53.77 ms for optimized folding Barrett reduction. These results demonstrate the practical value of exploiting modulus-specific arithmetic structure in fault-tolerant quantum circuit design.

</details>

**Authors:** Stuart Hadfield  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.11385v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

For H = K3, the constructed graph G(H) has L = 9, N = 56, and maximum cut counts |# MC(G)| = 2|# MC(H)| = 12. This figure was created with assistance from...</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that evaluating expectation values for QAOA circuits at depth $p \geq 2$ is computationally intractable, specifically showing it is \#P-hard. By constructing specialized graph gadgets, the authors reduce the problem of counting maximum cuts to calculating a specific coefficient in the QAOA's resulting Laurent polynomial. This establishes a significant complexity barrier for variational quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a strong theoretical barrier, suggesting that simulating or optimizing complex quantum algorithms beyond shallow circuits requires computational resources equivalent to solving hard counting problems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the computational complexity of evaluating expectation values for variational quantum algorithms, specifically QAOA applied to MaxCut.

**Main result.** The authors prove that for fixed depth $p \geq 2$, evaluating these expectation values (and related quantities like gradients/Hessians) is \#P-hard, meaning it is as hard as counting optimal solutions.

**Method.** The hardness proof refines previous work by constructing specialized 'counting gadgets' within the QAOA framework. This allows mapping the problem of counting maximum cuts ($\#	ext{MC}(G)$) to the coefficient extraction from a Laurent polynomial derived from the expectation value.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) applied to finding the MaxCut of a graph $G$. The system is described by cost and mixer Hamiltonians, leading to quantum states $|\psi_p(\gamma, eta)angle$ at depth $p$.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the cost expectation value $F_p(G; \gamma, eta) = \langle\psi_p|C_G|\psi_pangle$, and also single pairwise correlators $\langle Z_r Z_s angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The depth parameter $p$ is critical: $p=1$ is tractable ($\in 	ext{FP}$), while fixed $p \geq 2$ exhibits \#P-hardness.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The hardness results apply to worst-case families of graphs and require evaluating the expectation value with exponential precision ($\epsilon = 2^{-cN}$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a balanced counting gadget for $K_3$, demonstrating how the number of maximum cuts in the constructed graph relates directly to the original graph's count.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes tractability for $p=1$ (Chunk 4). It then builds upon this by introducing specialized gadgets and mathematical tools (Laurent polynomials) to prove that exact evaluation becomes \#P-hard for fixed $p \geq 2$ (Chunks 1 & 2). Finally, it extends the hardness result to computing derivatives like gradients and Hessians (Chunk 3).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Evaluating expectation values is a critical task for variational quantum eigensolvers, and for parameterized quantum circuits and other quantum algorithms more generally. We consider the well-studied case of the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) for the MaxCut problem. Recent work of Wang et al. [arXiv:2511.20212] showed this task to be NP-hard in general for any QAOA depth $p\geq 2$, complementing past results showing efficiently computable formulas for $p=1$ with arbitrary problem graphs. We sharpen this dichotomy showing that for $p\geq 2$ exact or exponentially precise cost expectation value evaluation is #P-hard under deterministic polynomial-time Turing reductions. Hardness at $p\geq 2$ is shown to remain even for evaluating single pairwise correlators $\langle Z\otimes Z\rangle $, as well as for highly restricted sets of algorithm parameters. Our proof refines the NP-hardness construction of Wang et al. that recovers the maximum cut value from the largest exponent of a QAOA Laurent polynomial, utilizing a distinct and simpler construction that extracts a value proportional to the total number of maximum cuts, in addition to the optimal cut value. Thus we show that the QAOA expectation value hardness transition from $p=1$ to $p=2$ is not only from tractability to optimization hardness, but to that of counting optimal solutions. As an application we show our results imply analogous hardness results for computing gradients and Hessians of QAOA circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates which working medium maximizes the efficiency of standard heat engines like Stirling, Otto, and Brayton. By analyzing a general thermodynamic potential, the authors prove that maximum efficiency is achieved when the system's caloric equation of state is linear in temperature. This result extends beyond ideal gases to include models like harmonic oscillators.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical thermodynamics, the rigorous mathematical framework linking efficiency maximization to specific functional forms (linear temperature dependence) provides insight into optimal constraints for energy conversion processes relevant in quantum systems modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the optimal working medium for an ideal heat engine cycle, moving beyond the standard assumption that the ideal gas is always optimal.

**Main result.** Maximum efficiency across multiple cycles (Stirling, Otto, Brayton) is achieved when the working medium's caloric equation of state is linear in temperature ($m=1$).

**Method.** The authors derive exact efficiency expressions for three canonical heat engine cycles using a general polynomial Helmholtz potential and analyzing their dependence on system parameters.

**Model / system.** The analysis considers general thermodynamic systems defined by a polynomial Helmholtz potential, applied to the ideal Stirling, Otto, and Brayton cycles. The working mediums include ideal gases, classical harmonic oscillators, and rubber band models.

**Key observables.** Efficiency ($\eta$) of the heat engine cycles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exponents $n$ and $m$ derived from the fundamental thermodynamic relation; cycle ratios (e.g., compression ratio $\kappa$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The underlying thermodynamic relationship is assumed to be a polynomial function of molar volume and temperature, and endoreversible cycles are analyzed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows Stirling efficiency ($\eta_S$) maximized at $m=1$, and Figure 2 shows Otto efficiency ($\eta_O$) suggesting maximization near $m=1$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives closed-form efficiencies for the Stirling, Otto, and Brayton cycles based on a general fundamental relation. The analysis consistently points to the condition $m=1$ as maximizing performance across all studied engines.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Given a particular heat engine cycle, what is the optimal working medium that results in the highest efficiency? While one might jump to the conclusion that it must surely be the ideal gas, the situation is actually more intricate. Starting with a general Hemlholtz potential that depends polynomially on molar volume and temperature we derive exact expressions for the ideal Stirling, Otto, and Brayton cycles. We find that quite universally the maximal efficiency is achieved for thermodynamics systems, whose working mediums are linear in temperature. This includes the ideal gas, but also classical harmonic oscillators and pheonmenological models of the rubber band.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors investigate the interplay between spin nematic order and scalar spin chirality in $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$ using polarized neutron scattering. They find that $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$ exhibits a high-temperature dynamic spin nematic phase, which couples with an external magnetic field to induce anomalous Hall effects (AHE) and anomalous Nernst effects (ANE). This provides strong evidence for composite spin orders driving room-temperature spintronic phenomena in antiferromagnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects exotic magnetic orders (nematicity) observed through neutron scattering directly to measurable transport phenomena (AHE), providing a concrete platform for studying emergent quantum magnetotransport effects in correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the relationship between spin nematic order, scalar spin chirality (SSC), and anomalous magnetotransport effects in tetragonal magnetic compounds.

**Main result.** In $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$, a dynamic spin nematic phase exists above $T_N$ that couples with an external field to induce measurable AHE and ANE, phenomena absent in the analogous $	ext{CaMnBi}_2$.

**Method.** The research employs polarized neutron scattering (LPA) to probe spin excitations and measures magnetotransport properties like the Anomalous Hall Effect (AHE).

**Model / system.** The system under study is the tetragonal lattice $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$ compared to $	ext{CaMnBi}_2$, which are characterized as collinear antiferromagnets. The physics involves complex spin orders: nematicity (breaking rotational symmetry) and SSC (a three-spin, time-reversal symmetry breaking order).

**Key observables.** Spin excitation anisotropy ($M_a$ vs $M_b$ vs $M_c$), AHE/ANE magnitude, and the temperature dependence of magnetic scattering intensities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical temperatures like $T_N \approx 270-290	ext{ K}$ and a nematic crossover temperature around $400	ext{ K}$; external magnetic field strength ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed AHE/ANE in $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$ is due to coupling between the dynamic spin nematic phase and SSC, which is not present in $	ext{CaMnBi}_2$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate temperature dependence of scattering cross sections ($\sigma^{	ext{SF}}$) showing changes in magnetic moment anisotropy across $T_N$ and $T_S$, alongside plots detailing the AHE response vs. field for both compounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes background on spin nematicity and SSC, uses polarized neutron scattering to characterize the distinct dynamic spin nematic phase in $	ext{YbMnBi}_2$ above $T_N$, confirms the collinear AFM structure via multiple measurements, and finally links this high-temperature nematic state to field-induced AHE/ANE.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A spin nematic order, analogous to the nematic liquid crystal, characterizes the spontaneous breaking of spin-space rotational symmetry while preserving time-reversal ($T$) symmetry. In contrast, scalar spin chirality (SSC), a composite three-spin order, breaks $T$ symmetry and is known to induce an anomalous Hall effect (AHE). Although a spin nematic phase has been suggested in frustrated magnets and the square-lattice iridate, how it might affect magnetotransport properties is unknown. Here we use polarized neutron scattering to show that tetragonal $A$MnBi$_2$ ($A$ = Ca, Yb) is a strictly $c$-axis-aligned collinear antiferromagnet (C-type), with $T_N \approx 270$ K and 290 K, respectively. On cooling from 450 K to $T_N$, low-energy spin excitations in YbMnBi$_2$ spontaneously change from isotropic to anisotropic in spin space within the tetragonal plane, forming a dynamic spin nematic phase around 400 K due to heavy Yb-induced spin-orbit coupling, before gapping out below $T_N$. Similar measurements on CaMnBi$_2$ reveal isotropic paramagnetic scattering without a spin nematic phase above $T_N$. Under an in-plane magnetic field, the Yb$^{3+}$ moments may interact with the dynamic spin nematic phase to induce nonzero SSC, giving rise to AHE and an anomalous Nernst effect (ANE) in YbMnBi$_2$ that are absent in CaMnBi$_2$ above $T_N$. A symmetry-based Ginzburg-Landau analysis shows that coupling terms between the nematic order and SSC are allowed under an external magnetic field, which could explain the rapid increase of AHE with field in YbMnBi$_2$. Our results provide compelling evidence for dynamic SSC-induced AHE and ANE in the paramagnetic phase of a compensated collinear antiferromagnet, opening a new avenue for the physics of composite spin orders and room-temperature spintronics without magnetic order.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper uses NV magnetometry to image magnon dynamics in YIG films, revealing that the approach to thermal equilibrium is not random but proceeds through discrete, multi-stage parametric scattering cascades. By quantifying these steps and mapping out the resonance structure, it establishes spatially resolved magnonics as a powerful tool for studying non-equilibrium many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This provides an experimental realization and quantitative framework for visualizing non-equilibrium many-body kinetics in a solid-state quantum system, directly addressing energy redistribution mechanisms beyond simple thermalization assumptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is understanding how coherently driven quantum many-body systems redistribute energy through discrete, non-equilibrium steps before reaching thermal equilibrium.

**Main result.** The system's relaxation proceeds via a multi-stage, deterministic scattering cascade involving magnons in the slow magnon regime, rather than purely stochastic processes.

**Method.** The authors combine NV magnetometry for micron-scale spatial imaging with theoretical modeling of parametric scattering instabilities to track energy flow.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Yttrium Iron Garnet (YIG) thin film hosting magnons (bosonic excitations). The dynamics are modeled using coupled-mode theory and nonlinear wave equations describing four-magnon interactions and subsequent cascading parametric resonances.

**Key observables.** Spatial growth profiles of scattering products, frequency shifts ($\omega_{NL}$), Rabi oscillation decay rates ($\Omega_R$), and the resonance structure in momentum space.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength ($g$) of the four-magnon interaction, the microwave driving intensity ($I$), and the operating regime near ferromagnetic resonance (FMR).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Standard perturbative approaches fail to capture the multi-stage kinetics; the analysis relies on identifying dynamic selection rules favoring momenta where group velocity vanishes ('slow magnon regime').

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, ODMR spectra showing nonlinear dips, and Rabi oscillation measurements confirming spatial decay of coherent excitations.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses by first isolating the elementary four-magnon interaction strength using two-tone driving, then observing a spontaneous, multi-generation scattering cascade driven by intense single-frequency excitation. Finally, theoretical modeling maps out the resonance structure across different cascade orders.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how coherently driven quantum many-body systems redistribute energy prior to thermal equilibrium remains a central challenge in many-body physics. Here, we utilize nitrogen-vacancy (NV) magnetometry to perform micron-scale spatial imaging of room-temperature magnon dynamics in a yttrium iron garnet (YIG) thin film. We resolve a hierarchy of discrete parametric scattering events that serve as deterministic stepping stones toward thermalization. By applying a two-tone wave-mixing protocol, we first isolate the elementary four-magnon interaction and extract its coupling strength via the spatial growth of the scattering product. We then drive the system with an intense single-frequency excitation near ferromagnetic resonance, revealing that magnon-magnon interactions trigger a spontaneous, multi-generation scattering cascade. We demonstrate that in each generation, the dominant scattering channels correspond to one of the out-scattered magnons being in the slow magnon regime, reminiscent of the enhancement of optical nonlinearities in slow light systems. We capture this dynamics quantitatively using a near field magnonics framework and extract the cascade order and nonlinear coefficients directly from power-dependent frequency shifts. By revealing the multi-stage dynamical process through which monochromatic injected magnons evolve toward equilibrium, our work establishes spatially resolved magnonics as a powerful platform for visualizing non-equilibrium many-body kinetics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a general and highly robust method to engineer arbitrary static O(N) lattice gauge fields in artificial systems. By exploiting symmetry-protected zero modes within bipartite units, the authors ensure that the target Hamiltonian is realized exactly, avoiding perturbative errors. This technique has been experimentally validated using acoustic resonators for realizing $\mathbb{Z}_2$, SO(2), and SO(3) topological models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, experimentally accessible blueprint for engineering complex quantum Hamiltonians—specifically those containing non-trivial gauge fields—using only local interactions in platforms like acoustics or photonics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is realizing arbitrary static O(N) lattice gauge configurations within artificial physical systems.

**Main result.** The authors present a general scheme that achieves this by encoding the target gauge field into the symmetry-protected zero modes of sublattice-imbalanced bipartite units, demonstrated successfully across multiple topological models.

**Method.** The methodology involves constructing an effective Hamiltonian ($	ext{H}_{	ext{eff}}$) by projecting the microscopic tight-binding model onto the zero-mode subspace, ensuring this projection is an exact spectral block rather than a perturbative approximation.

**Model / system.** The framework is demonstrated using acoustic crystals (coupled resonators), realizing specific models like a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ quadrupole topological insulator, an SO(2) Hofstadter model, and an SO(3) non-Abelian topological insulator.

**Key observables.** Transmission spectra, energy/frequency spectra of bulk/edge states, and field localization at boundaries are used as key diagnostic observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The construction relies on using only positive microscopic couplings (for experimental feasibility), and requires a sufficiently large intra-unit gap to isolate the zero-mode subspace.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that all necessary physical couplings can be realized as positive real numbers, allowing for mapping continuous gauge fields onto discrete artificial atom couplings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the general framework of using a three-site unit to create an O(N) artificial atom. Specific applications show the realization of edge states and flux patterns in acoustic resonators for the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ TI, SO(2) model, and SO(3) insulator.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical zero-mode construction using bipartite units, details the general procedure for projecting to an exact gauge Hamiltonian, and then presents experimental demonstrations across three distinct physical realizations ($\mathbb{Z}_2$, SO(2), SO(3)).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gauge fields are fundamental to modern physics, but prescribed gauge configurations are often difficult to implement in artificial systems. Here, we present a general scheme for realizing arbitrary static $\mathrm{O}(N)$ lattice gauge configurations using symmetry-protected zero modes of sublattice-imbalanced bipartite units. The target $\mathrm{O}(N)$ link on each bond is encoded in the connectivity and strengths of positive microscopic couplings. By decoupling the zero-mode manifold from the remaining modes, the target gauge Hamiltonian forms an exact spectral block of the microscopic tight-binding model rather than a perturbative approximation. We experimentally demonstrate this framework in acoustic crystals through a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ quadrupole topological insulator, an $\mathrm{SO}(2)$ Hofstadter model, and an $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ non-Abelian topological insulator. Our results provide a general and accessible route to gauge-field physics in artificial systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides an analytical solution for the stationary state of a memristor model incorporating mobile charged vacancies and their electrostatic interactions. By linearizing the governing equations, the authors derive expressions for vacancy profiles and demonstrate that the device's limiting resistance is robustly independent of the interaction strength in the leading order approximation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While this is condensed matter physics (memristors), the mathematical techniques—linearization of non-local PDEs, use of integral invariants, and analysis in limiting regimes ($p 	o 0, p 	o \infty$)—are highly relevant to theoretical studies of complex many-body dynamics or open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to find analytical expressions for the stationary (limiting) vacancy distributions within a closed memristor model that incorporates long-range electrostatic interactions between mobile charged vacancies.

**Main result.** Despite the inclusion of strong electrostatic interaction, the limiting resistances in both 'on' and 'off' states remain independent of the interaction strength ($\alpha$) at leading order.

**Method.** The analysis focuses on solving the steady-state condition ($J=0$) by linearizing the governing non-linear partial differential equations using a logit transformation ($\Lambda = \log(c/(1-c))$) and solving the resulting equation via quadrature integration.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional memristor containing mobile charged vacancies. The dynamics are governed by a continuity equation coupled with Poisson's equation, modeling charge transport influenced by vacancy concentration $C(x)$ and an electric potential $\Phi$.

**Key observables.** Stationary vacancy distributions $c(x)$, limiting resistances in 'on'/'off' states, and the derivative scaling factor $\upsilon$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electrostatic interaction strength ($\alpha$), applied current density ($p$), and filling factor ($r$). The analysis explores limits like $p \ll 1$ and $p \gg 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The full kinetic equation is too complex, necessitating the focus on stationary states ($J=0$). Results regarding resistance independence hold only in the leading linear order of vacancy concentration.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates the dependence of the derivative scaling factor $\upsilon$ on reduced current strength $p$ for various fillings and interaction strengths ($\alpha$), showing symmetry around half-filling.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the governing equations (continuity, Poisson) and then simplifies them to stationary state forms. It employs a logit transformation to linearize the system, leading to an equation solved via quadrature integration involving two constants ($\Lambda_0$ and $\upsilon$). Finally, asymptotic expansions for $\upsilon$ are derived.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A nonlinear model of a memristor based on charged mobile vacancies is considered taking into account the electrostatic interaction between them. This interaction significantly affects the stationary (limiting) vacancy distributions formed under the action of the electric current flowing through the memristor, for which analytical expressions are obtained in this work. Between the regions with reduced and increased vacancy concentrations, an intermediate electrically neutral region is formed due to the electrostatic interaction. Interestingly, the limiting resistances in the ``on'' and ``off'' states of such a memristor do not depend on the strength of the electrostatic interaction, at least in the leading first order in the vacancy concentration.

</details>

**Summary.** The research shows that patterned superconducting films act as superconducting diodes when illuminated by structured light. By exploiting the geometric asymmetry of holes (metacrystals), the system generates a directional supercurrent imbalance and measurable photovoltage even without external bias. This establishes a powerful, non-invasive method for controlling superconducting transport using optical fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum electrodynamics/optics with superconductivity, showing how engineered geometry can induce non-linear, directed transport phenomena using light, which is relevant for understanding coherent control in mesoscopic superconducting circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study demonstrates how structured light can induce a superconducting diode response in patterned films without applying a static bias or permanently altering the material.

**Main result.** Structured optical driving generates measurable, non-zero directional supercurrent imbalances and rectified photovoltages, establishing patterned superconductors as viable platforms for light-tunable diodes.

**Method.** The analysis relies on solving the generalized time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau equation using numerical simulations to model the coherent condensate response.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a superconducting film perforated with geometrically asymmetric holes (metacrystals). The dynamics are governed by the TDGL equation, incorporating an optical drive via a time-dependent vector potential.

**Key observables.** Rectified dc photovoltages ($V_{dc}$), zero-bias directional supercurrent imbalance ($\eta_{LR}, \eta_{UD}$), and the nonlinear dependence of $V_{dc}$ on optical intensity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The response is highly controlled by the hole array geometry, the helicity ($s$), and orbital angular momentum index ($\ell$) of the incident structured light.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses exclusively on the coherent condensate response, neglecting dissipative effects like optical heating or nonequilibrium quasiparticle dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup using structured light, show time-dependent current textures revealing chiral flow patterns, and plot $V_{dc}$ vs. intensity to confirm nonlinear rectification.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes how structural asymmetry interacts with various optical modes (Gaussian, Laguerre-Gaussian) under both continuous and pulsed driving conditions to quantify the resulting directional supercurrent imbalance and photovoltage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Structured light offers a route to control superconducting transport without permanently modifying the material or applying a static bias. Here we show that structured optical driving can generate a superconducting diode response in patterned superconducting films with asymmetric holes. Using generalized time-dependent Ginzburg Landau simulations, we find that optical driving produces rectified dc photovoltages and zero bias directional supercurrent imbalance in a junction free geometry, with continuous-drive diode efficiencies of order $10^{-3}$ and pulsed efficiencies reaching $10^{-2}$. The response is controlled by both the hole array and the optical mode. Increasing the number of asymmetric holes enhances rectification, reversing circular helicity reverses the diode polarity, and the optical spatial mode strongly modifies the magnitude and polarity of the directional response. Pulsed excitation enhances the zero bias line cut current imbalance to the percent level. For linearly polarized illumination, the asymmetric metacrystal converts the drive into local chiral supercurrent motion, inducing an inverse Faraday effect like mechanism for dynamical time reversal symmetry breaking. These results establish patterned superconducting films as a viable platform for light-tunable superconducting diode behavior.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how two localized, unstable field configurations called sphalerons collide in a scalar field theory with false vacua. Using numerical simulations, the authors demonstrate that these collisions lead to highly complex outcomes, including the transient formation of metastable vacuum bubbles. This reveals richer dynamics than previously understood for such systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study explores non-equilibrium dynamics far from equilibrium (vacuum decay/field evolution), which shares conceptual links with phase transitions and domain wall dynamics studied in statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex collision dynamics of bright sphalerons within a $(1+1)$-dimensional deformed $\phi^6$ scalar field theory that possesses false vacua.

**Main result.** The collisions exhibit rich final states, most notably the dynamic formation of long-lived bubbles of the false vacuum bounded by kink-antikink pairs, which repeatedly collapse and re-expand before decaying.

**Method.** The research combines linear stability analysis to characterize sphaleron properties with extensive numerical simulations to track the nonlinear time evolution following head-on collisions.

**Model / system.** The system is a $(1+1)$-dimensional real scalar field theory governed by a deformed $\phi^6$ potential, analyzed using two specific realizations: the barrier model and the well model. Sphalerons are non-topological solitary wave solutions existing in these metastable vacuum backgrounds.

**Key observables.** Final states (kink-antikink pairs, oscillons), bubble formation/decay dynamics, sphaleron lifetime ($	au$), and oscillation frequencies ($\omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The deformation parameter ($s$) of the potential and the initial collision velocities are crucial parameters governing the outcome.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that collisions occur when the intrinsic instability decay rate is slow enough to allow for a measurable interaction time. The initial two-sphaleron configuration used for simulation is an approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate potential energy landscapes (barrier vs. well), sphaleron profiles, quantum potentials, eigenvalue spectra, and phase diagrams mapping out various final states resulting from collisions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reviews the static properties and linear instabilities of the sphalerons in both vacuum models. It then proceeds to numerically simulate head-on collisions across parameter space, mapping out the resultant stable or transient field configurations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the collision dynamics of two bright sphalerons in a (1+1)-dimensional deformed $φ^6$ scalar field theory with a symmetric potential possessing false vacua. Two one-parameter realizations of the model, referred to as the barrier and well models, are considered and their static and linear instability properties are first reviewed. We then study head-on collisions of boosted sphalerons over a broad range of initial velocities and deformation parameters. The scattering dynamics exhibit a rich variety of final states, including the production of kink-antikink pairs, long-lived oscillons in true and false vacuum, multiple oscillons propagating in false-vacuum regions, and radiative decay. A particularly remarkable outcome is the emergence of a long-lived bubble of the false broken vacuum bounded by a kink-antikink pair, which repeatedly collapses and re-expands before eventually decaying into an oscillon. These results demonstrate that deformed $φ^6$ theories with false vacua exhibit considerably richer sphaleron dynamics than previously known and provide new insight into the role of unstable localized configurations in nonlinear field theories.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes electron transport in nanoscale silicon nanosheets by rigorously accounting for phonon confinement and remote-phonon scattering effects. The findings demonstrate that realistic boundary conditions significantly lower predicted mobility, but importantly, the interaction with high-kappa gate dielectrics (via IPPs) does not negatively affect transistor performance, suggesting compatibility for future devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state device physics rather than fundamental AMO phenomena, the detailed treatment of quantized lattice vibrations (phonons) interacting with confined charge carriers shares mathematical parallels with open quantum systems theory involving coupling to bosonic baths.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study theoretically investigates electron transport in an ultra-thin silicon nanosheet, focusing on how phonon confinement and interface hybrid plasmon-phonon excitations (IPPs) affect device performance.

**Main result.** Realistic boundary conditions for phonons significantly reduce the predicted room temperature mobility compared to bulk approximations. Crucially, IPP scattering is found not to negatively impact FET performance, potentially increasing the saturated velocity by cooling the electrons.

**Method.** The authors employed a combination of methods including calculating band structures using pseudopotentials and solving eigenvalue equations derived from continuum models for phonon confinement and dielectric continuum limits for IPPs.

**Model / system.** The system is a double-gated (100) silicon nanosheet with a thickness of 1.6 nm, featuring $	ext{SiO}_2/	ext{HfO}_2$ gate stacks. The model incorporates quantum confinement effects on both electron wavefunctions and phonon dispersion relations.

**Key observables.** Electron mobility ($\mu$), high-field saturated velocity, and the influence of various scattering mechanisms (phonon confinement, IPP scattering).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nanosheet thickness ($t_s = 1.6$ nm), room temperature, gate dielectric materials ($	ext{SiO}_2$, $	ext{HfO}_2$), and boundary conditions for phonons.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses an approximated elastic continuum model for acoustic phonons and the dielectric continuum limit for IPPs; it neglects extrinsic scattering sources like interface roughness or charged defects.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 (mentioned in notes) compares band structures calculated with parabolic vs. flat potential profiles, confirming small bandgap changes due to gate bias variations.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the physical system and Hamiltonian, detailing the methods for calculating electronic subbands under confinement, modeling phonon interactions via boundary conditions, analyzing the impact of IPPs, and concluding with performance implications for high-kappa materials.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study theoretically electron transport in an top-and bottom-gated (100) 1.6 nm-thin silicon nanosheet with SiO2/HfO2 gate stacks, focusing on the intrinsic physical processes that affect transport: the confinement of phonons and the presence of interface hybrid plasmon-phonon excitations (IPPs or `remote phonons'). The band structure is calculated using local empirical pseudopotentials; an approximated elastic continuum model is used to consider the confinement of acoustic phonons; the dielectric continuum limit is used to deal with the IPPs. We find that the electron mobility is affected significantly by the boundary conditions chosen to deal with phonon confinement. The more realistic assumption of phonons clamped at the SiO2/HfO2 interfaces and optical phonons at the Si/SiO2 interfaces results in a room temperature mobility much smaller than what is obtained using the common assumption of bulk phonons in the elastic, high-temperature approximation. We also find that, as a result of the complicated structure of the primed subbands, the high-field saturated velocity is significantly lower than its bulk value, as it had been measured in the past in the case of Si inversion layers but never explained theoretically. Finally, we find that IPP scattering does depress the low-field mobility but to a small extent, thanks to the presence of the interfacial SiO2 layers and to the proximity of the metal gates. Moreover, by keeping electrons `cooler', IPP scattering results in a higher saturated velocity. Therefore, the presence of high-kappa materials in the gate-insulator stacks should not affect negatively the performance of field effect transistors based on Si nanosheets.

</details>
