**Summary.** The research shows that light beam routing at the nanoscale can be achieved intrinsically within TMD monolayers without needing external nanostructures. By using angle-resolved cathodoluminescence, the authors prove that the dark exciton possesses an out-of-plane dipole moment, causing it to emit directionally at large angles relative to the surface normal.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates a solid-state platform for controlling photon emission directionality purely through quantum mechanical selection rules (dipole orientation), which is highly relevant for integrated photonic devices and open quantum systems engineering.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To achieve nanoscale beam routing of light without relying on external nanostructuring elements.

**Main result.** The intrinsic excitonic transitions within TMD monolayers, particularly the dark exciton, can produce highly directional emission channels at large angles.

**Method.** Low-temperature, angle-resolved cathodoluminescence spectroscopy is used to probe and distinguish between different excitonic species based on their angular emission profiles.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on monolayer Transition Metal Dichalcogenides ($	ext{WSe}_2$, $	ext{MoSe}_2$, $	ext{MoTe}_2$), which host distinct excitonic species: bright exciton, trion, and dark exciton. These species possess different transition dipole orientations (in-plane vs. out-of-plane).

**Key observables.** Emission energy, emission angle ($	heta$), and the relative intensity of $	ext{X}^0$, $	ext{X}_T$, and $	ext{X}_D$ as a function of collection angle.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature (down to 83 K), local dielectric environment, and the presence/thickness of encapsulating layers like hBN or graphene.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the distinct angular emission profiles are solely determined by the orientation of the respective excitonic transition dipoles.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, band diagrams, and measured spectra showing how $	ext{X}_D$ appears at large angles due to its out-of-plane dipole, while $	ext{X}^0$ and $	ext{X}_T$ peak near the surface normal.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of nanoscale light routing, details the use of TMDs with multiple excitonic species, presents experimental measurements using angle-resolved CL to map out dipole orientations, and concludes that intrinsic material properties can achieve directional emission.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Routing light at the nanoscale typically relies on nanostructured surfaces to imprint directionality on the emission. Using low-temperature, angle-resolved cathodoluminescence spectroscopy, we show that the intrinsic excitonic transitions of a semiconductor can themselves produce routed emission. We probe monolayers of WSe$_2$, MoSe$_2$, and MoTe$_2$ and resolve the excitonic species of monolayer WSe$_2$ -- the bright exciton, the trion, and the spin-forbidden dark exciton -- through their distinct angular emission profiles. While the in-plane transition dipoles of the bright exciton and trion radiate predominantly toward the surface normal, the out-of-plane dipole of the dark exciton, inaccessible under normal-incidence optical excitation, produces a directional emission channel at large angles. We further tune the balance between neutral and charged exciton emission through the local dielectric environment. Our results establish dark excitons in TMD monolayers as a platform for directional light emission in compact photonic architectures without additional nanostructuring.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a microscopic theory explaining an acoustic plasmon resonance observed in thin superconducting films. It shows this mode arises from the transverse redistribution of charge densities across the film thickness, bypassing limitations of older models like the Carlson-Goldman scenario. The resulting theoretical predictions for the mode's dispersion and temperature scaling align remarkably well with recent experimental findings.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical mechanism for observing collective excitations in superconductors that are otherwise predicted to be invisible or insensitive to superconductivity, connecting fundamental quantum many-body physics to measurable electromagnetic responses.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses an observed acoustic plasmon mode in superconducting films that critically depends on the superconducting transition temperature, challenging the conventional Anderson-Higgs paradigm.

**Main result.** A microscopic theory was developed showing that transverse charge redistribution across finite thickness allows for a unique coupling mechanism to EM radiation, predicting an acoustic mode whose dispersion and temperature scaling match experiments.

**Method.** The authors employed a microscopic kinetic theory approach, linearizing the kinetic equation and using Poisson's equation to derive polarization operators for normal and superfluid components.

**Model / system.** The system is quasi-two-dimensional superconducting films of finite thickness (d), involving both normal and superfluid charge densities. The model incorporates effects from both clean and dirty transport regimes.

**Key observables.** Acoustic plasmon mode frequency ($\omega_k$), damping rate ($\Gamma$), phase velocity ($s$), and their dependence on temperature ($T$) and film thickness ($d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting gap ($\Delta$), film thickness ($d$), temperature relative to $T_c$, and the ratio of mean free path to coherence length ($l/\xi_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes global charge conservation and is most accurate in the low-frequency, overdamped regime ($\omega	au \ll 1$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the temperature dependence of the normalized acoustic mode dispersion and damping rate in the dirty regime, confirming predicted scaling laws.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the conflict with established theory (Anderson-Higgs) to developing a microscopic kinetic framework. It derives general dispersion relations using polarization operators, analyzes different transport limits (clean/dirty), and finally presents results matching experimental observations regarding thickness and temperature dependence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent experiments on superconducting films have revealed an acoustic plasmon mode that depends critically on the superconducting transition, directly challenging the long-standing Anderson-Higgs paradigm regarding the stiffness of the plasma spectrum in superconductors. In this Letter, we provide a microscopic theoretical framework that explains this behavior and establishes the physical conditions under which classical Anderson-Higgs constraints are bypassed. We demonstrate that in films of finite thickness, the transverse redistribution of normal and superfluid charge densities enables a unique coupling mechanism to electromagnetic radiation - a feature fundamentally absent in the conventional Carlson-Goldman scenario. Our theory predicts an acoustic mode whose dispersion, temperature scaling, and dependence on film thickness are in remarkable agreement with recent experimental observations. By delineating the regime of this acoustic response, we reconcile the observed electromagnetic activity of collective excitations with the fundamental principles of superconductivity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates $	ext{PtBi}_2$, a 2D ferroelectric metal, demonstrating its potential for next-generation electronics. Using first-principles calculations, the authors predict high operational temperatures and significant spin-charge conversion effects. They conclude by proposing a novel nonvolatile memory device concept based on domain wall switching.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter electronics, the study of polarization switching coupled with charge/spin currents touches upon emergent order parameters and non-equilibrium dynamics that are relevant to advanced quantum control concepts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the electrical transport properties of intrinsic two-dimensional (2D) ferroelectric metals, aiming to overcome the limitation that traditional ferroelectrics are often insulating.

**Main result.** The material exhibits high Curie temperature ($T_C \approx 800\ 	ext{K}$), sizable charge-to-spin conversion efficiencies (Edelstein and SHE), and enables conceptual design of a nonvolatile Ferroelectric Metal Field-Effect Transistor (FEM-FET).

**Method.** The research employs first-principles calculations combined with the semiclassical Boltzmann transport equation to model linear, nonlinear, and spin transport responses.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the intrinsic 2D ferroelectric metal $	ext{PtBi}_2$. The theoretical framework involves analyzing its polarization switching, electronic band structure, and resulting charge/spin currents under applied fields.

**Key observables.** Electrical conductivity ($\sigma_{ab}$), Edelstein effect coefficient ($\chi$), Spin Hall conductivity ($\sigma^{ext}_{i;ab}$), Nonlinear Hall effect (NLHE), and ferroelectric polarization ($P$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High Curie temperature ($\sim 800\ 	ext{K}$); applied electric field ($E$); uniaxial strain.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The transport analysis relies on the semiclassical Boltzmann theory, and an effective thickness scaling is used for comparison with bulk materials.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate temperature-dependent polarization confirming high $T_C$, phonon dispersion showing structural phase transitions (FE $\leftrightarrow$ NP), and conductivity anisotropy measurements distinguishing different phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the material's ferroelectric stability via AIMD, calculates linear/nonlinear transport coefficients using Boltzmann theory, discusses spin-related effects (Edelstein/SHE), and culminates in proposing a device concept (FEM-FET) based on domain wall physics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Breaking the conventional stereotype that ferroelectrics are necessarily insulating, two-dimensional (2D) ferroelectric metals combine seemingly incompatible switchable electric polarization and metallic conductivity, providing a fertile ground for the discovery of novel electrical transport phenomena and the development of innovative electronic devices. Using the semiclassical Boltzmann equation and first-principles calculations, we systematically investigate the linear and nonlinear transport responses of the intrinsic 2D ferroelectric metal \ch{PtBi2} to an applied electric field. Our \textit{ab initio} molecular dynamics simulations reveal that it possesses a high Curie temperature reaching $800~\text{K}$. We propose that the crystal structure of its high-temperature paraelectric phase can be explicitly distinguished through simple measurements of the in-plane electrical conductivity. Quantitative calculations of the Edelstein effect and the intrinsic spin Hall effect demonstrate a sizable charge-to-spin conversion efficiency, highlighting its potential in spintronics. We also find that a Berry curvature dipole-induced nonlinear Hall effect emerges in uniaxially strained \ch{PtBi2}. Furthermore, we highlight the unique advantages of 2D ferroelectric metals in gate-controlled transport applications. Based on the domain wall scattering mechanism, we conceptually design a novel ferroelectric metal field-effect transistor (FEM-FET) capable of nonvolatile switching between high-resistance and low-resistance states under a gate voltage. Our work not only unveils the rich transport physics in 2D ferroelectric metals but also provides valuable insights into the design of next-generation nonvolatile memory and spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that the topological structure of a magnetic vortex induces an emergent curved space metric analogous to the exterior of an Ellis wormhole for electrons. The key findings are two experimentally distinct signatures: measurable electron deflection governed by the vortex winding, and a quantized Berry phase response dependent on winding parity. This establishes a powerful 'texture–geometry dictionary' applicable to quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, physical mechanism—a magnetic vortex—to generate an effective curved spacetime geometry that can be probed using standard quantum scattering techniques in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the topology of a magnetic vortex dictates the effective geometry experienced by itinerant electrons, linking defects to curved spacetime analogues.

**Main result.** A single magnetic vortex defect successfully generates an emergent metric realizing the exterior spatial geometry of an Ellis wormhole. This effect is characterized by two distinct, measurable signatures: electron deflection and a half-flux Aharonov-Bohm response.

**Method.** The analysis combines deriving effective metrics from spin textures with solving geodesic equations and performing partial-wave scattering analyses to quantify quantum phase shifts.

**Model / system.** The system models electrons propagating in a background coupled strongly to an elementary magnetic vortex. The geometry is mathematically compared to the exterior of an Ellis wormhole, and the results are tested by emulating the metric in designer honeycomb lattices.

**Key observables.** Electron deflection angle ($\Theta(b)$), Berry phase flux (Aharonov-Bohm response), and the effective curved space metric ($ds^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Vortex winding number ($q$), Hund exchange coupling ($J$), and the characteristic Ellis length scale ($a_q \propto q/\sqrt{J}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the exterior, core-truncated region of the wormhole geometry; lattice simulations require valley symmetrization to isolate the metric response.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate vortex textures and corresponding geodesics showing negative Ricci curvature. Other figures demonstrate universal scaling laws for deflection angles ($\Theta \propto q^2/J$) and show engineered hoppings reproducing continuum geodesic profiles in lattices.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the effective metric from spin texture variations, calculating observable quantities like deflection via elliptic integrals, establishing two independent signatures (metric vs. Berry flux), and finally validating these predictions by simulating wave-packet propagation on artificial honeycomb lattices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strong coupling to a magnetic texture makes an electron propagate through an emergent curved space. We show that an elementary vortex realizes the exterior spatial geometry of an Ellis wormhole: an ultrastatic throat with radius fixed by the topological charge and Hund exchange, cut off at short distances by the microscopic core. Two separable signatures follow directly: the electron deflection collapses onto a single Ellis curve governed by the vortex winding and exchange coupling, while the spin Berry phase produces a half-flux Aharonov--Bohm response switched on and off by winding parity. The same metric can be emulated in a designer honeycomb lattice, where the valley-symmetrized wave-packet response follows the predicted exterior geodesic. These signatures are accessible through real-space electron deflection and scattering, providing experimentally distinct probes of the emergent geometry and Berry flux. The magnetic vortex thus turns a topological defect into a tunable curved-space lens for electrons in quantum materials and designer lattices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study models supercurrents across junctions involving an altermagnet and superconductors. By analyzing the junction's response in different magnetic polarization regimes, the authors predict that a specific noncoplanar magnetic configuration induces a robust Josephson diode effect. This demonstrates how fundamental magnetic symmetries can govern macroscopic electronic transport properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges superconductivity with magnetism in a highly non-trivial way, predicting novel electronic transport phenomena (diode effects) directly controlled by the symmetry of the underlying magnetic order parameter. This is relevant for understanding superconducting spintronics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates spin-polarized supercurrents and the emergence of a Josephson diode effect (JDE) in junctions formed by an altermagnet (AM) sandwiched between two BCS superconductors (SC). The focus is on how magnetic structure dictates nonreciprocal transport.

**Main result.** A noncoplanar exchange field profile within the strongly spin-polarized AM regime leads to quantum geometric phases, causing a Josephson diode effect with high charge and perfect spin diode efficiencies. Conversely, other configurations yield normal or suppressed effects.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced quasiclassical Green's function techniques (Gor'kov/Eilenberger formalism) applied to the SC/AM/SC geometry.

**Model / system.** The system is an SC/AM/SC junction where the AM is a d-wave altermagnet. The model incorporates spin-dependent delta potentials at the interfaces and analyzes transport in both weakly and strongly polarized regimes.

**Key observables.** Josephson current-phase relation (CPR), charge diode efficiency, spin diode efficiency, and quantum geometric phases ($\Delta\varphi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative strength of the exchange field ($h_0$) compared to the Fermi energy ($E_F$) and superconducting gap ($\Delta$), which defines the polarization regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes ideal SC/AM interfaces (continuity of Green's functions) and often focuses on quasiparticles at the Fermi surface. The diode effect strongly depends on achieving a half-metallic contribution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Josephson CPR for different magnetic orientations ($\phi_0$) and polarization regimes, showing how current asymmetry ($I_{ch}$ vs $\Delta\chi$) manifests as a diode effect.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theoretical framework using Green's functions, analyzes the weak polarization limit first, and then focuses heavily on the strongly polarized regime to derive the conditions necessary for the Josephson diode effect based on magnetic field geometry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a systematic theoretical study of the Josephson effect in junctions consisting of a d-wave altermagnet (AM) placed between two BCS superconductors (SC). In general, the SC/AM interfaces are spin-active and modeled by spin-dependent $δ$ potentials, allowing for an arbitrary direction of the local exchange field vector. The model is formulated within the fully quantum (Gor'kov) and quasiclassical (Eilenberger) Green's function technique, applied to two distinct cases of (i) a weakly spin-polarized AM (exchange field much smaller compared to the Fermi energy) and (ii) a strongly spin-polarized AM (exchange field comparable to the Fermi energy). We apply our model to the SC/AM/SC geometry, accounting for the Josephson current-phase relation (CPR). In the weakly spin-polarized regime, the CPR displays the normal Josephson effect. Irrespective of the orientation of the altermagnet, the junction undergoes the $0-π$ transition. Depending on the orientation, the system displays the features similar to those of a ferromagnetic or an antiferromagnetic junction. To investigate the spin-polarized currents and nonreciprocal transport as the central results of the present work, we put the main focus on the strongly spin-polarized regime. Within this regime, we distinguish two cases. A coplanar exchange field profile across the junctions displays the normal Josephson effect; however, with a pure and stable long-range second harmonic in the CPR. In contrast, a noncoplanar exchange field profile gives rise to the so-called quantum geometric phases across the junction, leading to the absence of the phase-inversion center in the Josephson CPR. As a result, a Josephson diode effect emerges with a significant charge diode efficiency larger than 30% and a perfect spin diode efficiency of 100%.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that assuming unit magnetic permeability ($\mu=1$) when analyzing THz spectroscopy data for metals like Al and Cu leads to inaccurate electrodynamic characterization. By employing a self-consistent retrieval method, the authors show that the effective permeability significantly deviates from unity due to large screening currents induced by time-varying magnetic fields. This highlights the necessity of treating $	ilde{\mu}$ as an independent variable in such analyses.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical electromagnetism, the concept of induced macroscopic fields (screening currents) leading to parameter misinterpretation mirrors how boundary conditions or approximations can fundamentally alter the perceived quantum state or response in complex open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The standard assumption of unit relative magnetic permeability (mu=1) when analyzing THz spectroscopy data for metallic films introduces significant errors in retrieving the true electrodynamic parameters.

**Main result.** The effective permeability ($	ilde{\mu}$) significantly deviates from unity, a deviation attributed not to intrinsic magnetism but to macroscopic screening currents governed by the Faraday-Neumann-Lenz effect.

**Method.** The authors employed a self-consistent retrieval method (CTVT) on both transmission and reflection THz time-domain spectroscopy data to independently determine complex permittivity ($	ilde{\epsilon}$) and permeability ($	ilde{\mu}$).

**Model / system.** The study investigates highly conductive thin films of Aluminum and Copper deposited on substrates, analyzing their response in the terahertz frequency range using Drude dielectric modeling.

**Key observables.** Complex permittivity ($	ilde{\epsilon}$), complex permeability ($	ilde{\mu}$), complex refractive index ($	ilde{n}$), and normalized impedance ($	ilde{z}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Plasma frequency ($\omega_p$), relaxation time ($\omega_	au$), skin depth ($\delta(\omega)$), and the ratio of THz wavelength to skin depth ($\lambda_{in} / \delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption challenged is $	ilde{\mu}=1$; the analysis requires treating $	ilde{\epsilon}$ and $	ilde{\mu}$ independently for accurate characterization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate time-dependent THz signals, polar representations of electrodynamic parameters showing phase constraints, and comparisons of skin depths calculated under different assumptions ($	ilde{\mu}=1$ vs. free retrieval).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem with $	ilde{\mu}=1$, details the self-consistent retrieval methodology using THz-TDS data for Al/Cu films, presents results showing large deviations in $	ilde{\mu}$, and finally provides a physical interpretation linking this deviation to screening currents via the FNL effect.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The electromagnetic response of metallic films is commonly analyzed in terahertz spectroscopy by assuming unit relative magnetic permeability. In this work we show that this assumption introduces significant distortions in the electrodynamic retrieval of highly conductive films. Aluminum and copper films, 10 nm thick, were investigated by terahertz time-domain spectroscopy in both transmission and reflection configurations. By applying a self consistent retrieval method that independently determines the complex permittivity and permeability, we show that the Drude-type dielectric response is systematically accompanied by a permeability that strongly departs from unity. This deviation is intrinsically linked to the reactive impedance of the films, which clarifies the light induced onset of large screening currents within a transversally confined geometry. A phenomenological interpretation based on the Faraday Neumann Lenz mechanism and a lumped-element model of the film impedance accounts for the observed trends. These results indicate that the common assumption $\tildeμ=1$ in non-magnetic Drude films can lead to an incomplete or biased electrodynamic characterization in the terahertz regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how multi-soliton necklaces can maintain stable propagation in a specialized nonlinear medium. The stability arises from a delicate balance: repulsion among solitons in one component is counteracted by the confining effect of the boundary structure in a second coupled component. Numerical simulations confirm that careful initialization based on system dynamics leads to robust, long-lived metastable states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it explores complex nonlinear wave dynamics in optical systems, drawing parallels between quantum field theory concepts (solitons, nonlinearity) and physical phenomena like light guiding and confinement, which touches upon advanced topics in quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study elucidates a mechanism for the quasistationary propagation of multi-soliton necklaces within a two-component envelope system exhibiting competing cubic-quintic nonlinearity.

**Main result.** Metastable propagation is achieved by balancing inter-soliton repulsion (in one component) against boundary confinement (from the flattop region in the second component), showing robustness even with added noise.

**Method.** The research relies on numerical simulations tracking the radial position of maximum intensity and comparing initialization methods to assess stability over long propagation distances.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by a two-component envelope ($\psi_1, \psi_2$) governed by a nonlinear Schrödinger type equation with a Hamiltonian incorporating cubic-quintic nonlinearity. The confinement mechanism is analogous to nonlinear total internal reflection at the boundary of a flattop region.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the radial position of maximum intensity ($R_m(z)$) and the stability/persistence of the soliton necklace over propagation distance $z$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the number of solitons ($N$), the phase difference between adjacent solitons ($\Delta	heta = \pi$ for repulsion), and the geometry defined by the flattop region width.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a quasistationary regime where stability is achieved through a specific balance of nonlinear forces, and initial simulations suggest that using the mean oscillation radius enhances robustness significantly.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate amplitude distributions for soliton necklaces (e.g., N=10), track the evolution of the necklace radius $R_m(z)$ under different initialization conditions, and compare stable propagation with noisy inputs.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model using a two-component envelope equation; it then analyzes the stability mechanism by relating soliton repulsion to boundary confinement; finally, numerical simulations demonstrate robust metastable propagation when specific initial conditions (mean oscillation radius) are used.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present quasistationary ring-shaped soliton necklaces in a two-component envelope propagating in a medium with competing cubic-quintic nonlinearity. Metastable propagation of soliton necklaces results from a balance of repulsion between adjacent out-of-phase solitons in one component and confinement by the boundary of a flattop region in the other. Numerical simulations demonstrate metastable propagation over about a hundred diffraction lengths, even with random noise added to the input envelopes. The maximum number of solitons in metastable necklaces can be controlled either by changing the size of individual solitons or by adjusting the width of the flattop region hosting the necklace.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, formally verified lock-free Software Transactional Memory (STM) designed specifically for managing the shared, hierarchical state in automated condensed-matter measurement instruments. By providing atomic subtree updates and fast snapshots, it overcomes concurrency bottlenecks associated with traditional locking mechanisms. This robust framework enables complex, multi-threaded scientific workflows involving AI automation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum physics theory, the robust, high-concurrency state management techniques developed here are crucial for building complex, real-time control systems necessary for advanced quantum experiments like trapped ion or cavity QED measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is managing concurrent, shared state access from multiple threads (instrument control, data acquisition, GUI) in automated condensed-matter measurements without introducing deadlocks or significant latency.

**Main result.** The authors designed and formally verified a lock-free Software Transactional Memory (STM) framework that allows for atomic subtree updates and $O(1)$ consistent snapshots of hierarchical state.

**Method.** They implemented the STM using custom atomic shared pointers and a multi-phase CAS protocol to manage tree structure commits, which was rigorously checked using TLA+ and tested under weak memory models.

**Model / system.** The system is an open-source measurement platform used for NMR and ODMR experiments, modeling its state as a naturally hierarchical tree structure (KAME model).

**Key observables.** ODMR contrast map, frequency-swept spectrum, and the ability to acquire consistent subtree snapshots.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The performance advantage is shown by achieving near $O(1)$ snapshot cost compared to $O(\log N)$ path copying in traditional COW structures.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The design assumes that bounding retries via an oldest-first arbitration mechanism can maintain system-wide livelock-freedom, although general liveness proof remains complex.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the node tree structure of ODMR measurements, the overall KAME system architecture showing the STM layer, and the GUI workflow demonstrating AI agent interaction with measurement state via APIs.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the necessity of concurrent access management in scientific instruments, introduces the lock-free STM as the solution, describes its tree structure and atomic operations (snapshot/transaction), provides formal verification results (TLA+), and discusses practical implementation interfaces (Python/ZMQ).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Automated measurement of condensed-matter experiments requires instrument-control, data-acquisition, and user-interface threads to access shared, naturally hierarchical state concurrently. Coarse-grained locking can delay acquisition and cause sample loss, whereas fine-grained locking requires deadlock-prone lock ordering across instruments. Instead, we describe the lock-free software transactional memory (STM) that has been at the core of an open-source measurement platform for 16 years, in nuclear magnetic resonance experiments and, more recently, in optically detected magnetic resonance experiments. The STM organizes this state as a tree and provides atomic subtree updates and consistent subtree snapshots. After initial bundling, an unchanged subtree snapshot is acquired in $O(1)$ time through a custom lock-free atomic shared pointer. Within each bounded TLA+ configuration, TLC exhaustively checks the state space, establishing the safety and livelock-freedom properties specified for that configuration. Bounded executions of the atomic shared-pointer implementation are separately checked under the C11 weak-memory model. The same Snapshot and Transaction interfaces are exposed to Python scripting and AI-assisted automation.

</details>

**Authors:** Tianxing Jiang, Lianchuang Li, Haiyan Zhu, Hongyu Wang, Junchao Tian, Wenzhao Wang, Weiyi Pan, Haitao Wang, Changlin Zheng, Hongjun Xiang, Changsong Xu, Donglai Feng, Tong Zhang

**Summary.** This work systematically explores how the magnetic spin spiral state in layered multiferroic NiI2 responds to changes in film thickness and mechanical strain. By combining advanced STM measurements with detailed theoretical modeling, the authors establish that both dimensionality crossover and local curvature are powerful methods for engineering non-collinear magnetism and associated ferroelectricity in these 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the coupling between structural deformation (strain/curvature) and electronic/magnetic ordering is central to modern condensed matter physics, relevant for understanding emergent phenomena in low-dimensional quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the spin spiral state, which induces ferroelectricity, evolves as a function of film thickness and local strain in few-layer multiferroic NiI2.

**Main result.** Thickness increases cause a dimensional crossover characterized by an increasing spin-spiral wavelength and wavevector rotation. Furthermore, local film wrinkles induce measurable deflection of the spin spiral wavevector due to modified exchange interactions.

**Method.** The research combines experimental techniques like Spin-Polarized STM with advanced theoretical modeling using DFT+U calculations and spin-lattice dynamics simulations (LAMMPS/SpinGNN++).

**Model / system.** The physical system is few-layer van der Waals multiferroic NiI2, modeled by incorporating complex magnetic interactions including Heisenberg, Kitaev, and biquadratic terms. The Hamiltonian describes the coupled spin-lattice dynamics across different stacking orders (AA, AB, AC).

**Key observables.** Spin-spiral wavelength ($\lambda$), wavevector ($\mathbf{q}$) rotation/deflection, interlayer exchange coupling strength ($J_{nn}^\perp$), and associated electric polarization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Film thickness (1 to 7 ML), local strain/curvature, Hubbard correction parameter U=4.0 eV, and various magnetic exchange parameters ($J_1, K$, etc.).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping interlayer interactions onto effective intralayer couplings and assuming that structural changes dictate the modification of magnetic exchange interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate lattice structures, STM images showing spin spirals, and energy diagrams comparing different stacking orders (AA, AB, AC) and the effect of curvature-induced strain on magnetism.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the basic thickness dependence using SP-STM data, then developing a comprehensive theoretical model incorporating multiple interactions, and finally demonstrating how local mechanical strain (wrinkles/curvature) acts as an additional tuning knob for the magnetic state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Low-dimensional multiferroics hold great promise for integrated magnetoelectric devices. Spin spiral state has recently been shown to induce ferroelectricity in single-layer van der Waals (vdW) material NiI2. However, how this state evolves and can be tuned towards the two-dimensional limit remain unclear. Here, we combine spin-polarized scanning tunneling microscopy, layer-by-layer film growth, and multi-scale theoretical modeling to investigate the spin spirals in NiI2 thin films. As the film thickness increases from 1 to 7 monolayers, we observed a continuous increase of spin-spiral wavelength and a rotation of wavevector from near [110] to [1-10] direction, which evidences a dimensional crossover primarily driven by enhanced interlayer exchange energy. Moreover, we find that the film wrinkles can cause deflection of the spin spiral wavevector, which is caused by local curvature induced modification of exchange interactions. Our findings establish thickness and local strain as two tuning methods for engineering non-collinear helical magnetism and accompanied electric polarization in vdW multiferroics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that characterizing ferroelectric stacking order in layered $	ext{MoS}_2$ requires combining multiple advanced techniques. KPFM alone fails to distinguish between certain neutral stackings (ABA vs. BAB), but the combination of low-frequency Raman spectroscopy and PL successfully resolves these ambiguities by probing complementary structural, vibrational, and electronic properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly quantum information related, the study of polarization switching and structural characterization in 2D materials touches upon emergent collective electronic states and lattice dynamics that are crucial for designing solid-state quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving reliable, non-destructive assignment of the stacking order and ferroelectric polarization state in sliding ferroelectric van der Waals materials like trilayer 3R-MoS2.

**Main result.** A combined approach using KPFM, low-frequency Raman spectroscopy, and photoluminescence is necessary because no single technique can uniquely determine the local structural or electronic configuration of the material.

**Method.** The study combines Kelvin-probe force microscopy (KPFM) to map surface potential, low-frequency Raman spectroscopy to probe shear modes sensitive to interlayer coupling, and photoluminescence (PL) to analyze excitonic signatures.

**Model / system.** The system is exfoliated trilayer 3R-MoS2 flakes on an hBN substrate. The analysis compares different stacking configurations (e.g., ABA vs. BAB), which exhibit distinct physical properties despite some being indistinguishable by electrostatic measurements alone.

**Key observables.** Surface potential contrast ($	ext{KPFM}$); low-frequency Raman shear mode intensities ($	ext{S}1$ and $	ext{S}2$); A-exciton spectral line shapes ($	ext{PL}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The distinction between zero net polarization stackings (ABA/BAB) versus polar ones (ABC), and the failure of simple bond-polarizability models to explain all observed Raman shifts.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Individual techniques are insufficient; a complementary, multi-modal spectroscopic-scanning-probe approach is required for full characterization.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare KPFM maps with Raman intensity maps across different domains (I vs II) and show PL spectra taken from representative stacking regions ($	ext{ABC}$, $	ext{ABA}$, $	ext{BAB}$) to demonstrate the unique signatures of each technique.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for characterization, presents data showing KPFM ambiguity between certain stackings (ABA/BAB), then uses Raman spectroscopy to resolve this degeneracy by observing different shear modes. Finally, PL is used to further distinguish between these spectroscopically similar but structurally distinct domains.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sliding ferroelectricity in parallel-stacked two-dimensional van der Waals materials enables a broad range of novel device concepts, but exploiting it requires reliable, non-destructive assignment of the underlying stacking order and polarization state. Here, we combine Kelvin-probe force microscopy (KPFM) with low-frequency Raman spectroscopy to probe the polarization domains and stacking configurations of a exfoliated trilayer 3R-MoS$_{2}$ flake on a hBN substrate. We find that ABA and BAB - both stackings with zero net polarization - are indistinguishable in KPFM, yet show drastically different low-frequency shear modes. This observation is reproduced across multiple flakes and is corroborated by low-temperature photoluminescence. Notably, the standard bond-polarizability model does not account for the difference in shear-mode activity between the ABA and BAB configurations, indicating that the interlayer Raman response of these stackings is governed by physics beyond a simple polarizability picture. Our results show that none of the here-used individual techniques alone is sufficient to assign sliding-ferroelectric stacking order and motivate a combined spectroscopic-scanning-probe approach.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the interplay between thickness-driven Superconductor-Insulator Transition (SIT) and proximity effects in $	ext{(Cu,C)-1234/YBCO}$ heterostructures. By varying the top layer thickness, they observed that while the material undergoes SIT when isolated, coupling it to YBCO restores superconductivity over a specific thickness range. This work establishes a platform for studying interfacial superconducting coupling in high-temperature superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides experimental evidence on how interfacial coupling can stabilize superconductivity against intrinsic dimensional confinement effects in complex oxide systems, which is relevant for understanding quantum phase transitions in artificial superconducting junctions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the interplay between thickness-driven Superconductor-Insulator Transition (SIT) and superconducting proximity effects in high-temperature cuprate heterostructures.

**Main result.** Reducing the thickness of the top layer causes an SIT, but superconductivity is re-established across the interface with YBCO over a finite range before being suppressed again at very small thicknesses.

**Method.** The research employed Pulsed Laser Deposition (PLD) to fabricate $	ext{(Cu,C)-1234/YBCO}$ heterostructures and characterized them using four-probe electrical transport measurements as a function of film thickness.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an epitaxial heterostructure composed of two cuprate superconductors: $	ext{YBCO}$ (bottom layer) and $	ext{(Cu,C)-1234}$ (top layer), grown on an LAO substrate. The study explores the superconducting coupling at this interface.

**Key observables.** Temperature-dependent resistance ($ho$-$T$ curves), superconducting transition temperature ($T_{c0}$), and normal state resistance as a function of film thickness.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Film thicknesses, particularly varying $	ext{(Cu,C)-1234}$ thickness from $188 	ext{ nm}$ down to $1.2 	ext{ nm}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed recovery mechanism suggests that the robust superconducting state of YBCO helps reestablish long-range phase coherence in the ultrathin $	ext{(Cu,C)-1234}$ layer.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the growth process and test circuits; key results show how $T_{c0}$ drops with decreasing thickness for isolated films (SIT) but recovers when coupled to YBCO before dropping again at the thinnest limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of fabrication ($	ext{PLD}$ growth), structural confirmation ($	ext{XRD}$, $	ext{STEM}/	ext{FFT}$), and systematic electrical characterization across varying thicknesses, comparing isolated film behavior to heterostructure performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting proximity effect and related thickness-driven property evolution remain an important issue in understanding high temperature superconductors. Among proximity systems, superconductor-superconductor (S-S') is special for the existence of intrinsic superconductivity in both materials. Such platform allows the different superconducting orders to compete, couple and reconstruct at the interface. In this paper, (Cu,C)-1234/YBCO heterostructure grown on LAO (001) with fixed thickness of bottom YBCO layer as 150 nm and varied thickness of top (Cu,C)-1234 layer as 188nm, 87 nm, 18nm and estimated 1.2 nm were fabricated and component films were preserved. Electrical transport characterization indicated that as the thickness decrease the (Cu,C)-1234 film degrades and underwent the superconductor-insulator transition (SIT) from thicker to less than 18 nm. In contrast, superconductivity is re-established in transport measurements when the insulating (Cu,C)-1234 layer is coupled to superconducting YBCO As the (Cu,C)-1234 thickness is further reduced to approximately 1.2 nm, the recovered superconductivity is strongly suppressed. The observed thickness dependence is consistent with a scenario in which interfacial coupling restores superconductivity over a finite thickness range before increasing disorder and dimensional confinement dominate in the two-dimensional limit. This work establishes a promising platform for investigating interfacial coupling between cuprate superconductors and provides new insight into the superconducting proximity effect in high-temperature superconducting heterostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates the geometric constraints imposed by non-Abelian quantum geometry on degenerate energy bands. It derives key inequalities linking the metric tensor to the second Chern class, showing that when these bounds are saturated, the parameter space must possess highly structured geometries like quaternion Kähler manifolds. This provides a powerful tool for characterizing topological phases in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical connections between topological invariants ($c_2$) and the geometry of parameter space metrics, which is crucial for understanding robust topological phases in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study derives geometric bounds for the quantum metric related to the second Chern class ($c_2$) in non-Abelian quantum geometry.

**Main result.** The paper establishes key inequalities relating $	ext{tr } g$, $\sqrt{\det g}$, and $|C_2|$, showing that saturation of these bounds implies specific geometric structures like quaternion Kähler manifolds or complex structures.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Quantum Geometric Tensor (QGT) derived from band structure properties, employing Hodge decomposition and algebraic identities involving gauge fields to derive inequalities.

**Model / system.** The focus is on doubly degenerate energy levels characterized by $	ext{SU}(2)$ gauge structures within a four-dimensional parameter space ($M$). Examples include four-band Dirac Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Quantum metric ($	ext{tr } g$), Non-Abelian Berry curvature ($F_{\mu
u}$), and the Second Chern class density ($	ext{Tr}(F \wedge F)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the parameter space (4D) and the gauge group structure ($	ext{SU}(2)$ vs $	ext{U}(2)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes gapped, doubly degenerate band structures and relies on comparing geometric constraints derived from different symmetry groups.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the QGT and its components, deriving the central determinant inequalities using algebraic identities, analyzing the conditions for saturation (which link to specific geometric structures), and finally comparing results between $	ext{SU}(2)$ and $	ext{U}(2)$ cases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the quantum geometry of doubly degenerate energy levels in a four-dimensional parameter space. For degenerate pairs with $SU(2)$ gauge structures, the quantum geometry obeys $\big(\textrm{tr } g\big)^2/16\geq\sqrt{\det g}\geq |\textrm{Tr}(F\wedge F)|/12$. The first inequality characterizes the anisotropy in the metric. The second determinant inequality measures the self-duality of the curvature under Hodge star operation and the inter-level processes that do not close under the three $SU(2)$ rotations of the doubly degenerate levels. The saturation of the determinant bound induces a quaternion Kähler structure on the four-dimensional parameter space, analogous to the complex structure induced by the ideal-band condition in two-dimensional Chern insulators. As examples, four-band Dirac Hamiltonians automatically saturate the determinant bound and possess a topological zero in $\textrm{Tr}(F\wedge F)$. We discuss the comparison to degenerate pairs with $U(2)$ gauge structures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a comprehensive first-principles framework to quantify magnetoelastic coupling in 2D magnets using strain derivatives of exchange interactions. By applying this method to $	ext{NiPS}_3$ and $	ext{CrPS}_4$, the authors demonstrate that while both materials show magnetostriction, $	ext{CrPS}_4$ exhibits significantly stronger, anisotropic coupling, allowing external strain to robustly control its magnetic phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit, quantitative link between lattice strain and magnetic phase transitions is highly relevant. Understanding how external mechanical stress can tune quantum ground states or critical temperatures provides a pathway for designing novel spintronic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study develops a first-principles framework to microscopically link spin interactions (magnetism) with elastic response (lattice structure) by quantifying magnetoelastic coupling in 2D magnets.

**Main result.** The work distinguishes between two regimes: $	ext{NiPS}_3$ shows weak, isotropic coupling, while $	ext{CrPS}_4$ exhibits strong, anisotropic coupling that allows strain to drive transitions between different magnetic phases (e.g., spin-spiral to ferromagnetic).

**Method.** Calculations use DFT to derive strain derivatives of exchange interactions, which are then incorporated into a strain-dependent Heisenberg model solved via Monte Carlo simulations.

**Model / system.** The system involves monolayer magnets ($	ext{NiPS}_3$ and $	ext{CrPS}_4$) modeled by a strain-dependent Heisenberg Hamiltonian. The total elastic energy is expanded to second order in strain, incorporating magnetic contributions derived from exchange coupling changes.

**Key observables.** Magnetostriction, renormalization of the elastic tensor ($A_{ij}$), critical temperatures ($T_c$), and the stability/phase diagram of magnetic ground states (FM vs. SS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strain ($\varepsilon$), Hubbard $U$ correction in DFT, and the magnitude of exchange couplings ($J_{ij}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation retains only the leading contribution from isotropic exchange interactions; calculations are performed assuming a fixed monolayer thickness.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate possible unit cell deformations under strain, show magnetic phase diagrams mapping ground states vs. applied strain ($\varepsilon_x, \varepsilon_y$), and plot $T_c$ dependence on strain.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from developing the general theory of magnetoelastic coupling (Section I), detailing the DFT/MC computational methods for both electronic structure and elasticity (Section II), before applying this unified framework to analyze $	ext{NiPS}_3$ and $	ext{CrPS}_4$ separately.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a first-principles framework for magnetoelastic coupling in two-dimensional magnets based on a strain-dependent Heisenberg model. In this approach, strain derivatives of the exchange interactions provide direct access to magnetostriction and to the magnetic renormalization of the elastic tensor, establishing a microscopic link between spin interactions and elastic response. We apply the method to monolayer NiPS$_3$ and CrPS$_4$, which exhibit contrasting magnetoelastic behavior. NiPS$_3$ shows weak and nearly isotropic spin-lattice coupling, consistent with a robust zigzag antiferromagnetic ground state. In contrast, CrPS$_4$ displays strong anisotropic coupling, leading to strain-driven transitions between spin-spiral and ferromagnetic phases and significant changes in the critical temperature and elastic response. Our results demonstrate a general route to quantify magnetoelastic effects in low-dimensional magnets and highlight CrPS$_4$ as a promising platform for strain engineering of magnetic order.

</details>

**Summary.** This work comprehensively re-examines yttrium superhydrides using multiple experimental techniques and advanced theoretical calculations. The findings consistently point to limitations in the superconducting state of these binary compounds, particularly arguing against room-temperature superconductivity based on measured critical fields and calculated $T_c$ reductions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity rather than quantum information, the rigorous application of advanced many-body theory (Migdal-Eliashberg) combined with high-pressure experimental diagnostics provides a model for testing fundamental condensed matter theories under extreme conditions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To re-investigate the superconducting potential of yttrium polyhydrides ($	ext{YH}_6$, $	ext{YH}_9$, and $	ext{YH}_{10}$) under high pressure, specifically addressing claims of room-temperature superconductivity.

**Main result.** The combination of advanced measurements (pulsed fields, RF susceptibility) and theoretical calculations strongly disfavors the existence of room-temperature superconductivity in binary yttrium superhydrides.

**Method.** A combined approach using experimental techniques like pulsed-field magnetometry, contactless radio-frequency AC susceptibility, and electrical transport measurements, alongside sophisticated first-principles calculations (DFT, Migdal-Eliashberg theory).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the superconducting properties of yttrium hydrides ($	ext{YH}_x$) synthesized and tested within Diamond Anvil Cells under pressures up to 213 GPa.

**Key observables.** Superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$), upper critical magnetic field ($	ext{B}_{c2}(T)$), AC magnetic susceptibility (field screening), and normal-state magnetoresistance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pressure range of 140–213 GPa; superconducting transitions observed at $T_c \approx 218-237$ K for $	ext{YH}_6$ and $	ext{YH}_9$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical modeling incorporates anharmonic effects, which are crucial in reducing the predicted $T_c$ of $	ext{YH}_{10}$ to a more realistic range.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows electric transport measurements ($R(T)$ vs. T and B) for $	ext{YH}_6$; Figure 2 details RF transmission measurements confirming superconductivity in $	ext{YH}_6$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically combines experimental characterization (transport, RF, pulsed fields) to map out the superconducting phase diagram of $	ext{YH}_x$, followed by theoretical calculations that refine and constrain the predicted critical temperatures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Yttrium polyhydrides are benchmark materials in high-pressure superconductivity, yet several key properties of the Y-H system remain insufficiently characterized. Here we combine contact transport, contactless radio-frequency measurements, pulsed-field experiments, and first-principles calculations to reinvestigate YH$_6$, YH$_9$, and YH$_{10}$ in the pressure range 140-213 GPa. Yttrium hydrides YH$_6$ ($\textit{$T_c$}$ = 218-221 K) and YH$_9$ ($\textit{$T_c$}$ = 235-237 K) demonstrate narrow superconducting transitions ($\textit{$Δ$T$_c$}$ = 2-5 K), approaching the limit imposed by thermal fluctuations. Pulsed-field measurements on YH$_6$ up to 60 T establish an extended superconducting phase diagram with a linear slope $\textit{dB$_{c2}$/dT}$ = -0.52 T/K, pronounced transition broadening above 30 T, and negligible normal-state magnetoresistance. We report the radio-frequency AC susceptibility study of YH$_6$, providing evidence for superconductivity via high-frequency field screening in a contactless geometry. Experiments involving Pd incorporation, Pd thin-film sputtering, and Al alloying show strong suppression of high-temperature superconductivity, with no transitions detected above 78-120 K. Finally, using density-functional theory with the stochastic self-consistent harmonic approximation, superconducting density-functional theory, and full-bandwidth Migdal-Eliashberg calculations, we show that anharmonic effects substantially reduce the predicted $\textit{$T_c$}$ of cubic YH$_{10}$ to approximately 260-270 K. These results strongly disfavor room-temperature superconductivity in binary yttrium superhydrides.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates large magnetoresistance in $	ext{CrSBr}$-based van der Waals heterostructures by measuring transport properties as a function of applied magnetic field angle. The key finding is that the MR mechanism stems from a band edge offset that changes linearly with the cosine of half the magnetization angle, confirming spin-dependent interlayer hybridization rather than simple electrode polarization effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state magnetism, the precise mapping of energy levels (band edge shifts) based on magnetic configuration provides insights into spin-dependent coupling mechanisms relevant for quantum device engineering.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to elucidate the physical mechanism responsible for large, non-monotonic magnetoresistance (MR) observed in van der Waals antiferromagnetic heterostructures.

**Main result.** The MR is dictated by a magnetic-configuration-dependent band edge offset, which varies linearly with $\cos(	heta/2)$, ruling out models based solely on electrode spin polarization like the Jullière model.

**Method.** Transport spectroscopy using I-V measurements across varying bias voltages and temperatures, combined with angular dependence analysis of the extracted band-edge offset ($\Phi$).

**Model / system.** The system is a $	ext{hBN}/	ext{few-layer-graphene}/	ext{CrSBr}/	ext{few-layer-graphene}$ vertical heterostructure, utilizing $	ext{CrSBr}$ as the magnetic semiconductor.

**Key observables.** Large MR up to 350% with M-shaped bias dependence; linear variation of band-edge offset ($\Phi$) vs. $\cos(	heta/2$); temperature evolution of transport regimes (VRH vs. activated).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures ($20 	ext{ K}$), magnetic field angle ($	heta$), and bias voltage ($V$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The transport is dominated by tunneling/hopping limited by the band-edge barrier of $	ext{CrSBr}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures show I-V sweeps, MR vs. V plots exhibiting M-shapes, and crucial angular dependence plots demonstrating the linear fit of the band-edge offset against $\cos(	heta/2)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the large MR phenomenon, uses continuous field variation along the hard axis to map the band-edge offset ($\Phi$) as a function of magnetization angle ($	heta$), and analyzes this angular dependence to rule out competing theoretical models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We explore the large magnetoresistance (MR) in \ce{hBN}/few-layer-graphene/\ce{CrSBr}/few-layer-graphene heterostructures and reveal the mechanism behind its non-monotonic bias dependence. Using bias voltage and temperature as independent tuning knobs, we achieve MR up to \SI{350}{\percent} at \SI{20}{K}, characterized by symmetric M-shaped maxima around $\pm 0.5\,\mathrm{V}$. Continuous tuning of the magnetization angle $θ$ via a hard-axis magnetic field shows that the barrier band-edge offset varies linearly with $\cos(θ/2)$, a first-order signature of spin-dependent interlayer hybridization. This linear relationship rules out the Jullière model and a spin-filter projection. We conclude that the magnetic-configuration-dependent band edge, rather than electrode spin polarization, dictates the large magnetoresistance in \ce{CrSBr} junctions.

</details>

**Summary.** This lecture reviews ferromagnetism in ultrathin films, treating them as quasi-two-dimensional magnetic systems. It details how dimensionality dictates unique properties like oscillatory coupling and specific types of magnetic anisotropy. The theoretical analysis uses Renormalization Group techniques to describe phase transitions, linking the physics to established models like the 2D Ising universality class.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of RG theory to finite-temperature phase transitions in low-dimensional quantum systems is a core concept relevant to understanding critical dynamics in many-body physics, bridging statistical mechanics with condensed matter magnetism.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reviews fundamental principles governing ferromagnetism in ultrathin films, focusing on how reduced dimensionality profoundly impacts magnetic properties.

**Main result.** It establishes that the 2D nature leads to unique ground state behaviors, predicting phenomena like oscillatory coupling and phase transitions governed by universality classes such as the 2D Ising model.

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on theoretical frameworks including Landau theory for free energy, Renormalization Group (RG) techniques for critical behavior, and models addressing thermal fluctuations in low dimensions.

**Model / system.** The system studied is ultrathin films of 3d transition metals grown layer-by-layer. These systems behave as effectively two-dimensional magnetic quantum wells, exhibiting phenomena like 'dead' layers and perpendicular anisotropy.

**Key observables.** Spontaneous magnetization ($M_s$), magnetic anisotropy (perpendicular vs. in-plane), oscillatory interlayer coupling, and the critical exponents near phase transitions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Film thickness (ultrathin limit), temperature relative to $T_c$, and dimensionality (2D confinement).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often employs approximations like mean field theory or assumes specific universality classes for phase transitions, though the stripe transition remains incompletely understood.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Helmholtz free energy ($F(M)$) vs. magnetization ($M$) to distinguish between equilibrium and metastable states, showing characteristic hysteresis loops in $M$ vs. applied field ($B$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing the physical system (ultrathin films), detailing key magnetic phenomena (anisotropy, coupling), developing theoretical tools (Landau/RG), and finally analyzing specific transitions like the loss of ferromagnetic order.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In these Lecture Notes we review some of the fundamental principles that have emerged from research on the ferromagnetism of ultrathin films consisting of 3d transition-metal overlayers. Their growth is often layer-by-layer. This growth mode produces quantum wells along the vertical direction that profoundly impact any physical property of the materials. In addition, the vertical confinement establishes spin ensembles that extend to macroscopic distances along the in-plane directions and are finite along the vertical (perpendicular) direction, i.e. they are two-dimensional. Accordingly, they display ground state properties that originate from the two-dimensionality, such as ``dead'' magnetic layers or ``enhanced magnetic moments'', an oscillatory interlayer magnetic coupling and an anomalous perpendicular versus in-plane magnetic anisotropy that produces, in some specific situations, a perpendicular collective orientation of the spins. At finite temperatures, ferromagnetic order is observed to persist and an analysis of the magnetic order of ultrathin films in terms of the renormalization group provides a suitable framework for explaining this observation. The ferromagnetic order is lost at a phase transition which follows closely the two-dimensional Ising universality class, as shown by an accurate analysis of data in the vicinity of the critical point. The perpendicular spin orientation is often observed to turn in-plane by a reorientation phase transition which is also properly described by a renormalization group argument. Finally, the perpendicular spin orientation introduces topological excitations of the ferromagnetic order, consisting of stripes of reversed perpendicular spin direction. The stripe order undergoes a phase transition to the paramagnetic state that is not yet completely understood.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates that placing a Ruthenium underlayer beneath Co/Ni multilayers can induce a dramatic sign reversal in the Anomalous Hall Effect. This effect is experimentally linked to tensile strain generated by lattice mismatch, which theoretical calculations confirm reshapes the electronic band structure and redistributes Berry curvature, offering a new pathway for strain-controlled topological transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the concept of using controlled lattice strain (a structural parameter) to tune topological transport properties like Berry curvature is highly relevant to engineering quantum materials and simulating emergent phenomena in condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how strain, specifically induced by an underlayer, can control the sign of the Anomalous Hall Effect (AHE) in magnetic multilayers.

**Main result.** Introducing a Ru underlayer causes a robust sign reversal of the AHE from positive to negative, which is theoretically attributed to tensile in-plane strain reshaping the electronic structure and Berry curvature near the Fermi level.

**Method.** The work combines experimental transport measurements (measuring anomalous Hall resistivity) with first-principles density functional theory (DFT) calculations that incorporate lattice strain effects.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{Ru}/[	ext{Co}/	ext{Ni}]_N$ multilayers, characterized by a strong (111) crystallographic texture and exhibiting perpendicular magnetic anisotropy. The theoretical modeling uses DFT to calculate the anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{AH}$) under varying strain.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall resistivity ($ho_{AH}$), Anomalous Hall Conductivity ($\sigma_{AH}$), Berry curvature distribution, and lattice strain ($\epsilon$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Multilayer repeat number ($N$), the presence/position of the Ru layer (underlayer vs. capping layer), and the magnitude of tensile in-plane strain.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The sign reversal is primarily due to lattice mismatch inducing tensile strain, rather than simple interfacial atomic intermixing or current shunting effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $ho_{AH}$ hysteresis loops demonstrating opposite polarities based on Ru placement. Theoretical figures illustrate the dependence of $\sigma_{AH}$ on strain and map out the sign reversal of Berry curvature contributions in momentum space ($k_z$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the experimental observation of AHE sign reversal upon placing Ru under the Co/Ni stack, contrasts this with capping layers, and then uses DFT calculations to microscopically prove that tensile strain modifies the electronic bands, leading to a Berry curvature-driven sign change.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The anomalous Hall effect (AHE) is a hallmark transport phenomenon in ferromagnets arising from relativistic spin-orbit interaction. Here, we report an unexpected sign reversal of the AHE in Ru/[Co/Ni]$_N$ multilayers controlled by the stacking sequence of the Ru layer. When Ru is placed beneath, rather than atop, the Co/Ni multilayers, the anomalous Hall signal switches from positive to negative. By systematically varying the multilayer repeat number N and combining transport measurements with first-principles calculations, we show that this reversal originates from in-plane tensile strain imposed by the Ru underlayer, which reshapes the electronic structure and redistributes Berry curvature near the Fermi level. Our findings establish interfacial strain as an effective knob for tuning Berry-curvature-driven transport and suggest a pathway toward strain-controlled topological transport phenomena in magnetic multilayers.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes that Quantum Anomalous Hall Effect can be achieved in 2D oxide monolayers ($	ext{M}_2	ext{DO}_6$) by inducing ferromagnetism solely through the $	ext{O-}2p$ orbitals. By simulating cation deintercalation, the authors demonstrate a robust pathway to realize a high-Chern-number QAHE phase with $\mathcal{C}=4$, opening new design principles for magnetic topological insulators beyond conventional $d$-electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state magnetism, the concept of engineering topological phases using orbital physics—specifically leveraging $	ext{O-}2p$ states rather than transition metal $d$-orbitals—provides a novel paradigm for designing artificial quantum materials relevant to condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is realizing the Quantum Anomalous Hall Effect (QAHE) using a mechanism distinct from traditional partially filled transition-metal $d$ orbitals or correlation-driven moiré bands.

**Main result.** The authors propose that cation deintercalation in $	ext{M}_2	ext{DO}_6$ oxides can induce half-filled $	ext{O-}2p$ ferromagnetism, leading to a high-Chern-number QAHE phase with a Chern number of $\mathcal{C}=4$.

**Method.** The study employs first-principles calculations (DFT) combined with effective low-energy $k\cdot p$ modeling to analyze the electronic and topological properties.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the two-dimensional oxide monolayer $	ext{M}_2	ext{DO}_6$ ($	ext{M} = 	ext{Zn}, 	ext{Cd}$; $	ext{D} = 	ext{Se}, 	ext{Te}$), where magnetism originates from the half-filled $	ext{O-}2p$ orbital after cation deintercalation.

**Key observables.** Spin-polarized Dirac points at K/K′ valleys, a high Chern number ($\mathcal{C}=4$), and quantized anomalous Hall conductance ($\sigma_{xy} = 4e^2/h$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spontaneous ferromagnetism is driven by the $	ext{O-}2p$ manifold; the gap opening due to SOC results in a measurable spin polarization gap (e.g., $0.5-0.6 	ext{ eV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism relies on the feasibility of cation deintercalation and projecting the full $	ext{O-}p$-orbital Hamiltonian onto an effective two-band model near Dirac crossings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structures, nonmagnetic vs. spin-polarized band structures showing gap opening, and schematics confirming the high Chern number plateau ($\sigma_{xy} = 4e^2/h$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of existing QAHE mechanisms, then proposes $	ext{M}_2	ext{DO}_6$ as a novel platform. It details how cation deintercalation activates $	ext{O-}2p$ ferromagnetism, calculates the resulting topological properties (Dirac points and Chern number), and concludes by establishing this general design principle for oxygen-based magnetic topology.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum anomalous Hall effect (QAHE) arises from the interplay between magnetic order and spin-orbit coupling, which opens up a topologically nontrivial band gap to host chiral edge states in the absence of magnetic field. So far, magnetic order of QAHE usually originates from partially filled transition-metal $d$ orbitals or correlation-driven moiré bands. Here, we propose an experimentally accessible family of two-dimensional oxides, M$_2$DO$_6$ (M = Zn, Cd; D = Se, Te), that can realize QAHE from the half-filled O-$2p$ orbital induced spontaneous ferromagnetism. In M$_2$DO$_6$ monolayers, spin-polarized Dirac points appear at K/K$^{\prime}$ valleys and along $Γ$-K/$Γ$-K$^{\prime}$ lines. $C_3$ rotational symmetry then generates eight symmetry-related crossings in the first Brillouin zone. Upon gap opening by spin-orbit coupling, each massive Dirac point contributes half Chern number, resulting in a high-Chern-number QAHE phase with $\mathcal{C}=4$. We establish cation deintercalation as a general strategy to activate O-$2p$ ferromagnetism in oxides. Our finding provides a route to realize QAHE from O-$2p$ ferromagnetism and offers design principles applicable to oxygen-based magnetic topology platforms beyond conventional $d$-electron systems.

</details>
